# Gentle Dot — native Windows 11 support

Objective: Gentle Dot runs natively on Windows 11 with parity to macOS — the Dot, the panel, the tray, global shortcuts, persistent secrets, a self-contained installer, voice, and computer control. Design: `docs/design.md`; the macOS/Linux baseline is `odd/tasks/gentle-dot.md`.
Branch: `feat/windows-port` · Delivery strategy: `staged-phases` (each phase is an independently shippable slice; the user chose full parity in L1, sequenced because review workload and verification tooling differ per phase) · Test runner: `cargo test` for `apps/desktop`, Vitest (`pnpm test`) and Playwright (`pnpm e2e`) for the rest.

## Scope note — what "native Windows" excludes

WSL is not the target. A build produced inside WSL is a Linux ELF binary; Windows cannot run it natively. The target is `x86_64-pc-windows-msvc` (and later `aarch64-pc-windows-msvc`) built on Windows over NTFS. WSL stays usable as a development shell, but every Windows work unit is verified on Windows or on a Windows CI runner, never simulated on Linux.

## Specs

- **SW1 — The platform layer knows Windows.** User: "me interesa tenerlo en windows" (L1).
  - Acceptance: `platform::Os` has a `Windows` variant and `Os::CURRENT` reports it from `cfg!(target_os = "windows")`; no Windows build silently takes a Linux branch. All platform decisions stay pure functions over injected environment values, so they are tested on every OS.
  - Checks: `cargo test` for `platform.rs`, including a test per OS variant; the `Os::CURRENT` test asserts against `cfg!(target_os = ...)` for all three.
- **SW2 — The daemon and the web UI run natively on Windows.** User: chose full parity (L1).
  - Acceptance: `pnpm install` and `pnpm dev` work on Windows with Node 24; the daemon serves the UI at `http://127.0.0.1:4317`, chats, and answers asks in the browser. No POSIX-only call is reachable on Windows without a platform branch.
  - Checks: Vitest with Windows-specific unit tests for path, shell, and process handling; a manual browser smoke on Windows.
- **SW3 — Native shell parity.** Acceptance: a Tauri 2 Windows app shows the floating Dot, expands the panel, keeps the tray with per-state glyphs, registers the open shortcut and the autostart entry, and persists the Dot position. The panel keeps its opaque background and asks the window system for nothing until the blur is verified on real hardware (see L8: Tauri applies at most one Windows material, discards the failure, and cannot be read back, so `Mica` and an `Acrylic` fallback cannot be requested honestly yet).
  - Checks: `cargo test` for the pure decisions; a manual Windows checklist with observed results (mirroring `docs/linux-testing.md`).
- **SW4 — Secrets persist and the limit is documented.** Acceptance: connector tokens live in a Windows store the app creates and reads, and **no** token ever falls back to `MemoryStore` on Windows: one DPAPI-protected file, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Gentle Dot\secrets.dat`, written with `CryptProtectData` and read with `CryptUnprotectData` at the default user scope (never `CRYPTPROTECT_LOCAL_MACHINE`), replaced atomically, holding every id the daemon asks for at any size. The Windows Credential Manager is **deliberately not used**: a generic credential's blob is capped at `CRED_MAX_CREDENTIAL_BLOB_SIZE` (2560 bytes), and the daemon keeps a sign-in as one JSON secret that already measures **2744 bytes for a Microsoft-backed connector before any token exists** and 2396 bytes for a Google-backed one, leaving 164 bytes for `access_token`, `refresh_token` and `id_token` together (L40, L41). The store refuses an oversized, unreadable or unwritable entry with a named error; it never truncates, never splits one id across several items and never falls back. Docs state plainly that any process running as the same user — the agent's shell included — can read them, exactly as S25.7 states for Linux; that paragraph is **W14's**, in the Windows section it already owns.
  - Checks: Rust unit tests around the store's contract and its failure paths, run against the real store under a throwaway directory — the file layer takes an injected directory, so the contract test runs rather than skips; a manual round-trip on Windows across a restart.
- **SW5 — Self-contained Windows installer.** Acceptance: a Windows NSIS (and MSI where WiX is available) installer carries its own Node runtime, engine, Engram, and UI; `scripts/package/lib.mjs` accepts `windows-x64`; the staged runtime uses `node/node.exe`, `bin/engram.exe`, `bin/pi.cmd`, and a `;`-separated `PATH`. Installing on a clean Windows user starts the daemon and the engine.
  - Checks: the existing `scripts/package/test/**` suite extended for the Windows target; an install smoke on a clean Windows user.
- **SW6 — Voice on Windows.** Acceptance: the mic button records through a Windows capture backend and prefers the local Parakeet model (sherpa-onnx, Windows static libraries confirmed available); without the model, an API key path applies, and the UI reports voice as unavailable rather than failing.
  - Checks: Rust unit tests for the engine-selection decision; a manual device check on Windows.
- **SW7 — Computer control on Windows.** Acceptance: parity with the S24 contract — a native helper inside the app (never in the daemon or the engine) that serves MCP on `127.0.0.1:<random port>/mcp` with a per-launch bearer token, exposes the S24.2 tool set, enforces the S24.4 blocklist, rate limit, and risky-action confirmations, and paints the S28 glow. Input goes through `SendInput`, element and risk detection through UI Automation, screenshots through Windows Graphics Capture, and target-window resolution through `WindowFromPoint`.
  - Checks: Rust unit tests on every pure guard; a Windows checklist with observed results; enforcement tests that the agent cannot reach the approval dialogs.
- **SW8 — Docs, CI, and honest verification.** Acceptance: `README.md`, `docs/install.md`, and `docs/design.md` list Windows and how to build it; a Windows CI job compiles the Rust shell and runs the suites; `docs/windows-testing.md` mirrors `docs/linux-testing.md`.

- **SW9 — The memory mode is a setting, not an environment variable (L72).** User (L72): "Llave en config.json + toggle en Settings". Acceptance: `config.json` carries an optional `"memory": "shared" | "private"` (absent means `shared`; any other value is refused with a named error). The **daemon** reads it in `loadConfig` and applies it to the engine's environment at start, so the mode holds on every launch path (the tray, `pnpm dev`, the e2e harness) with nothing exported by the app, and `GENTLE_DOT_ENGRAM` stays the documented override, where the environment wins as it does for `port`. Settings shows a **Private memory** toggle that persists the value through a key-preserving write and restarts the daemon so the change applies. Private mode keeps its own store under `<dataDir>/home/.engram` on port 7438 (W25) and never reads or writes the user's own Engram.

- **SW10 — The suite is honest about the fixtures a host cannot build (L73).** User (L73): "Sonda + skip ruidoso (recomendado)". Acceptance: on any host, the daemon suite never fails for lacking the privilege to create symlinks. Directory fixtures go through `symlinkDirectory` (a junction on Windows) and run everywhere; each test that needs a **file** symlink skips with one line on raw stderr naming the reason and the way out, decided by a probe cached once per process — the probe being the attempt itself, since Node exposes no query for the privilege — and injectable, so both branches are asserted without privileges. Assertions that need no privilege (the hardlink, the plain path spellings) keep running, and no fixture is ever silently replaced by something weaker. On a host with Developer Mode, nothing skips and the suite behaves as it does today.
  - Checks: the CI job is green on a Windows runner; docs readback.

- **SW11 — The Windows job is green at the tip, and a suite the host cannot run says so (L74).** User (L74): chose "cerrar el set POSIX + sacar continue-on-error (W27 completa)" over leaving the job red or making it non-blocking. Acceptance: `pnpm test` on Windows fails only for a real defect, so the blocking `typescript` job is green. A suite whose **subject** the host does not have — the inherited fd 3 (S25.1) and server mode's `setuid`/`setegid` (S25.8) — skips on Windows with one line on **raw stderr** naming the reason and what the host uses instead, through `packages/daemon/test/support/platform-absence.ts`, and the platform's own mechanism keeps its coverage rather than losing it: `app-channel-pipe.test.ts` carries the fd-3 channel on every platform, and `cli.test.ts`'s channel test holds the channel over the named pipe W7 introduced on Windows instead of skipping. An expectation that was POSIX-shaped rather than absent (`isolation.test.ts`'s PATH) is fixed, never gated. `cargo test` passes on `windows-latest`, so the `rust` job carries no `continue-on-error` either, and a push to the staged port branch runs the workflow without a manual dispatch.
  - Checks: `.github/workflows/windows.yml` parses with `yaml` and both jobs keep their steps; `pnpm test` on Windows at the tip reports no failing test; a real `windows-latest` run at the tip reports both jobs green.

## Verification constraint

This phase is developed from a Linux host. Windows-only code cannot be compiled or run here:

- Rust code under `#[cfg(target_os = "windows")]` is verified by review plus the Windows CI job (SW8), never by a local `cargo build`.
- Node/TypeScript work unit tests run wherever they are platform-neutral; platform-specific behavior is tested through injected values (env, path sep, program name), not through the host.
- Anything that needs a real Windows machine (window effects, tray, Credential Manager, WASAPI, UI Automation, the installer) is recorded as `blocked: needs Windows` in the task row and in the Log until observed. No such check is ever reported as passed.

| W34 | SW1, SW2 | inline (one case-folding rule + its one caller, L87) | done (measured on an installed copy, L88) | Measured on the **installed** copy (MSI, `C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot`) by reading the live processes' environment blocks: the daemon (`pid 7224`) carried `Path=C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot\runtime\bin;…\runtime\node;C:\Program Files\Git\cmd;C:\Windows\system32;C:\Windows` — W31's work intact — while its engine (`pid 1052`) carried `PATH=C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot\runtime\bin` alone. Probed separately: a child given both spellings resolves the uppercase one, whatever the order. RED: the new isolation test saw `["Path","PATH"]`; GREEN: `isolation.test.ts` **16 passed**, `pnpm typecheck` exit 0, `cargo test --lib` **413 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored** (412 before `holds_git`'s test). **Measured, and it closed the whole set:** on the copy installed from the rebuilt MSI the engine's `PATH` is `…\runtime\bin; …\runtime\node; C:\Program Files\Git\cmd; C:\Program Files\Git\bin; C:\Windows\system32; C:\Windows` — the daemon's list arrives intact, one spelling, with Git and the system folders in it | **The isolation threw away every `PATH` the daemon computed, so W31's git never reached the engine.** Windows names the variable `Path`; `isolatedAgentEnv` read `base.PATH`, found nothing, and then set `env.PATH` **beside** the inherited `Path`, so the engine's environment carried both spellings and its own resolution used the uppercase one — the bundled folder alone. One root, three symptoms: `git` not found, so RDD's review mode and the skill registry reported themselves unavailable (issue #90 again, this time under W31); `curl` named missing in the engine's setup warning; and every `PATH` lookup (`where`, `cmd`) narrowed to one directory. **Fix:** the path variable is looked up case-insensitively, every spelling is deleted and one `PATH` is set with the bundled folder first and the daemon's entries after it. **Second defect fixed in the same pass:** `windows_git_dirs` was handed `\|path\| path.is_file()` for **folders**, so the four default Git candidates were always rejected and W31's `where.exe`-less fallback could never run; `holds_git` asks for `git.exe` inside instead. **Risk item 6** until the installed copy proves it: this path only exists in a released build, where no unit test could see two spellings of one variable collide |

## Log

- **L1 (2026-10-11, user):** "Qué necesitamos hacer para adaptar este proyecto a poder utilizarlo en windows, tengo windows 11, y aunque ahora estamos trabajando en wsl 2 o 3 porque se actualizó hace unos días, me interesa tenerlo en windows". Scope choice: **full parity with macOS** (all six phases), including computer control. Environment note: the current checkout lives on the WSL filesystem (`/home/Row/...`), so Windows builds need a Windows-side checkout over NTFS.

- **L2 (2026-10-11, agent, evidence):** Read-only reconnaissance of the whole repository before any write. Findings, with evidence:
  - No Windows support exists today: zero `#[cfg(windows)]` in the repo, no `icon.ico`, no `tauri.windows.conf.json`, and `scripts/package/lib.mjs:8-12` lists only `darwin-arm64`, `linux-arm64`, `linux-x64` while `scripts/package/test/lib.test.ts:30` asserts that `windows-x64` throws.
  - `apps/desktop/src-tauri/src/platform.rs:6-14` defines `enum Os { MacOs, Linux }` and `Os::CURRENT` returns `Linux` for anything that is not macOS, so a Windows build would take every Linux branch (`effects=none`, `forced_gdk_backend`, `compositor_places_windows`).
  - The app↔daemon private channel is a Unix socketpair on fd 3: `app_channel.rs:28`, `daemon.rs:16-18`, `daemon.rs:294-297`, with `pre_exec`, `dup2`, `close_range`, and `getrlimit` in `daemon.rs:313-380`. This does not compile on Windows and is the keystone of the port.
  - `secure_store/mod.rs:110-117` has macOS (Keychain) and Linux (Secret Service) backends and falls back to `MemoryStore` everywhere else, so Windows would silently lose connector tokens on restart.
  - Daemon POSIX surface: `chmod`/`mode` 0700/0600 in `daemon.ts`, `connectors.ts`, `profiles.ts`, `isolation.ts`; `spawn` with `{uid, gid}` (`supervisor.ts:221-225`); `execFileSync("/bin/sh", ["-c", …])` (`connector-credentials.ts:30`); `env.SHELL || "/bin/sh"` plus `command -v engram` (`runtime.ts:42`); `lsof` (`isolation.ts:228`); `SIGTERM`/`SIGKILL` kill-tree (`supervisor.ts:167-207`).
  - Shell: `daemon.rs:223-226` builds a colon-joined `PATH` and `{root}/node/bin/node` with no `.exe`; `build.rs:28-47` records the build machine's `node`/`PATH` and its `find_in_path` has no `.exe` handling; `disclaimed.rs` is `posix_spawn` only; `libc` is unconditional.
  - Packaging: `stage-runtime.mjs:131-160` downloads Node as `.tar.gz` and extracts with `tar`; `lib.mjs:162` generates `#!/bin/sh` wrappers with 0755.
  - Voice: `voice/pipe.rs:23-27` records with `parec`/`arecord`; `voice/app.rs:133` already has `VoiceInput::unsupported()`.
  - Computer control: `computer/macos.rs` (604 lines) is CGEvent/AXUIElement/ScreenCaptureKit with no shared surface; S24 declared macOS-only and put Linux out of scope.
  - Free wins: the daemon, protocol, and UI are platform-neutral (Tauri access is behind `isTauri()` in `packages/ui/src/desktop.ts:2`); `main.rs:2` already sets `windows_subsystem = "windows"`; `pnpm-lock.yaml` resolves win32 binaries for biome, esbuild, rolldown, and the Tauri CLI; the external engine (`gentle-pi`) provisions a native Windows x64/arm64 runtime, Engram publishes Windows binaries, and sherpa-onnx publishes Windows x64/arm64 static libraries.
  - Tests: 63 test files, many POSIX-only (`/bin/sleep` and `exec 3<&-` in `daemon.rs` tests, `lsof`, `secret-tool`), so several need platform guards.
  - Recorded in Engram as the topic mirror of this document.

One work unit, one coherent tree.

- **L7 (2026-10-11, agent, evidence):** The push failed against upstream — the WSL SSH key authenticates as `Row0902`, which is not a collaborator on `Gentleman-Programming/gentle-dot`. A fork was created instead (`Row0902/gentle-dot`) and `feat/windows-port` pushed there, and PR #57 was opened upstream.
  - **Correction to L6:** a pull request opened **from a fork** does not trigger the workflow. For `pull_request` events from a fork, GitHub runs the workflows defined in the base repository's **default branch**, not the fork's, so the fork cannot define its own CI. `main` has no workflows, so `repos/.../actions/workflows` is empty and `total_count` was 0 for both `event=pull_request` and `head_sha`. L6's expectation that the PR itself would run the job was wrong: it holds for same-repository branches, not for forks. The first workflow of a repository cannot be bootstrapped through a fork — it needs a same-repository branch, a collaborator, or a push to the default branch.
  - The run was obtained by pushing the branch to the fork's own `main` (a clean fast-forward from `535e7f7` to `1736f62`), which matches the `push: branches: [main]` trigger. **Fork run `38171022857`:**
    - `Set up job`, `actions/checkout@v7`, `pnpm/action-setup@v6`, and `actions/setup-node@v7` all **succeeded** — the action tags chosen in W3 are real and the workflow infrastructure works.
    - `TypeScript (daemon, protocol, UI)` → **failure** at the `Install` step; `Typecheck`, `Lint`, and `Test` were skipped as a result.
    - `Rust shell` was still in progress when the log was written.
  - **The install error text is not yet known.** GitHub does not publish logs until the whole run completes, and the per-job logs endpoint returned nothing. Recorded as unknown rather than guessed; it is W15.
  - Two things about the install were ruled out with evidence: `pnpm install --frozen-lockfile` passes on Linux (exit 0, "Already up to date", 223 ms), and `pnpm-lock.yaml` already carries the Windows platform packages (77 `win32` references; 17 distinct entries including `@biomejs/cli-win32-x64`, `@esbuild/win32-x64`, `@rolldown/binding-win32-x64-msvc`, `@tauri-apps/cli-win32-x64-msvc`, `@typescript/typescript-win32-x64`, `@yuuang/ffi-rs-win32-x64-msvc`). So it is neither a stale lockfile in general nor a missing Windows dependency in particular.

- **L8 (2026-10-11, agent, evidence):** The native review of W1+W3 (lineage `review-e964a8bcd1761b57`, tier `high`, 4 lenses, 326 changed lines) returned one CRITICAL finding that survived an independent refuter, so it was a real defect in W1, not a false alarm.
  - **R4-1 (resilience, `shell.rs:488-490`):** the Windows panel asked for `Effect::Mica` with no fallback while `has_panel_effect()` was true, so `panel_url` omitted `effects=none` and the UI was told not to paint a background. If `Mica` did not take effect — Windows 10, transparency effects disabled, Remote Desktop, a compositor or GPU failure — the panel would be translucent with no backdrop and read unreadable. The code comment even claimed the opposite ("the panel keeps the translucent URL and therefore still paints its own background"), which was false.
  - **Why the obvious fix was wrong.** SW3 promised `Acrylic` as a fallback, and it cannot be delivered through this API. `tauri-2.12.1/src/vibrancy/windows.rs` scans the effect list with `find`, so `[Mica, Acrylic]` picks `Mica` and never tries `Acrylic`; the `window_vibrancy` result is discarded with no `?` and no log; and there is no readback to ask whether the effect applied. A silent no-op is therefore indistinguishable from success, and asking for `Mica` proves nothing.
  - **Correction applied:** `WindowEffect::Mica` was removed, `panel_effect()` returns `None` for Windows, and `with_panel_effects` leaves the Windows builder untouched. The panel therefore keeps `effects=none` and paints its own background, which is always readable. W8 asks for `Mica` once a real Windows machine shows the blur lands, and SW3's fallback clause was corrected because the fallback it promised does not exist. A regression test (`an_unverified_backdrop_never_leaves_the_panel_transparent`) now pins the invariant: any OS whose backdrop is unverified must end up with `effects=none`.
  - **Lesson worth keeping:** the review paid for itself here. This was an inferential finding about a platform reachable by only one of the four lenses, on a code path that cannot run on the development host, and it was introduced by the author in the same commit as a comment asserting the opposite. Four lens runs plus one refuter was the right price for it.

- **L9 (2026-10-11, agent, evidence):** W15 diagnosed from the real CI log. The `Install` step failed with the cause named in full:

```
.../node_modules/gentle-pi postinstall$ node scripts/install-gentle-ai.mjs
gentle-pi could not install its package-local Gentle AI v4.0.0 binary:
  Windows source installation requires local Go 1.25.10 or newer;
  found go version go1.24.13 windows/amd64.
postinstall: Failed
[ELIFECYCLE] Command failed with exit code 1.
```

  - The `windows-latest` image ships Go 1.24.13. On Darwin and Linux the same postinstall downloads a SHA-256-pinned release archive, so the Go requirement is Windows-only and invisible on every other platform.
  - **What the log rules out**, which matters because these were the guesses before it: `✓ Lockfile passes supply-chain policies (466 entries)`, `Packages: +365`, `added 365, done`, and the `esbuild` postinstall succeeded. Long paths, junctions, symlinks, and a stale or Windows-incomplete lockfile were **never** the problem. The install got all the way to the last postinstall.
  - Fix in `136d5c9`: `actions/setup-go@v7` with `go-version: stable` before `pnpm install`, because the constraint is "or newer" rather than an exact version. The tag was checked against the upstream release list first (v7.0.0, 2026-07); guessing v5 would have been a year stale, the same mistake made earlier with `pnpm/action-setup`.
  - **Consequence beyond CI, for SW5 and W11:** a self-contained Windows installer cannot carry the engine while `gentle-pi` builds its binary from source there. Either upstream publishes a prebuilt Windows binary, or the package carries Go, or Windows requires Go 1.25.10+ to be installed. This is the first Windows blocker that is **not** fixable inside this repository.
  - **Fix not yet verified:** the Go step has never run. Verification needs one more push to the fork's `main`, which is the only trigger available until the workflow exists on the upstream default branch.
  - Note on the review lifecycle: this work unit moves the candidate tree past `5a68d3fa…`, so lineage `review-e964a8bcd1761b57` is superseded. It was already unable to close (L8: the targeted-validator capture was rejected three times), so nothing that could have finished was lost.

- **L11 (2026-10-11, agent, evidence):** The next run (`38177833421`) confirmed the Go fix and exposed a second, unrelated defect behind it.
  - **W15 verified.** `Successfully set up Go version stable`, `go version go1.27.2 windows/amd64`, then `gentle-pi postinstall: Gentle AI v4.0.0 installed at …\gentle-ai.exe` and `postinstall: Done`. Step conclusions: `Install` **success**, `Typecheck` **success**.
  - **W16 found.** `Lint` **failure**, `Test` skipped: `Found 201 errors`, and every reported line ends in a carriage return (`throw·new·Error(…);␍`). Windows checkouts default to `core.autocrlf=true`, so the working tree gets CRLF and `biome check` judges the bytes it is given. The repository had **no `.gitattributes`**, and the development host uses `core.autocrlf=input`, which is exactly why this never appeared locally.
  - Fix in `a10a763`: `.gitattributes` with `* text=auto eol=lf`. It belongs in the repository rather than in the workflow, because a Windows developer hits the same 201 errors on their own checkout before pushing. `git add --renormalize .` staged only the new file, confirming the stored content was already LF and that nothing else moved.
  - This is the second time in this port that a real defect was invisible on the development host and only a Windows runner could surface it. It is the argument for W3 paying for itself, and it is worth remembering that both times the cost of finding it was one CI run rather than a debugging session on the user's machine.

- **L12 (2026-10-11, agent, evidence):** Run `38184877146` confirmed W16 (the `Lint` step went from failure to **success**) and unblocked the step behind it, which is where the real work starts. **The Windows baseline of the TypeScript suite, measured:**

```
Test Files  23 failed | 54 passed | 7 skipped (84)
```

  On Linux the same suite is `78 passed | 6 skipped`, **0 failed**. So 23 of 84 test files fail on Windows, and the failures map onto the plan instead of being random:
  - ~~**41 assertions are file-permission checks**~~ (superseded by L13: that was a duplicated raw log-line count, not a count of assertions). The measured figures are **22 failing tests over 19 distinct source lines** for that class, against **31 sites in the code**. Distribution by mode: 31 × `expected 438 to be 384` (`0o666` vs `0o600`), 8 × `438 to be 448` (`0o700`), 2 × `438 to be 420` (`0o644`) is the *raw log* shape; the distinct tests were 17 × 0o600, 4 × 0o700, 1 × 0o644. Windows has no POSIX mode bits, so none of these can hold. That is **W5**, whose scope is exactly the `chmod`/`mode` guards.
  - **POSIX path assumptions: W4.** `expected 'D:\h' to be '/h'` (a test using a POSIX absolute literal), `expected 'D:\a\gentle-dot\…' to match /node_modules\/gentle-pi\/…/` (a forward-slash regex against a backslash path), and a `C:\Users\RUNNER~1\AppData\Local\Temp\…` comparison that differs only in the temp path's form.
  - **The fd-3 channel: W7**, named and isolated — `app-channel-fd.test.ts` covers "the app channel on fd 3 (S25.1)" and fails exactly there.
  - **uid/gid privilege switching: W6** — `engine-access.test.ts` (13 failures) exercises `seteuid`/`setegid`, which do not exist on Windows.
  - **Behaviour that `chmod` was faking: W5.** `child-guard.test.ts` simulates an unwritable extensions folder by removing the write bit; on Windows the write succeeds, so `expected '1' to be '0'` and `expected +0 to be 1`. The tests are honest; the simulation mechanism is what does not port.
  - **Look at this before estimating W4:** `config.test.ts` reports `expected { port: 5000, host: '127.0.0.1', …(8) } to match object { port: 5000, …(1) }`, an object-shape difference that path handling alone does not explain.
  - Worst offenders by failure count: `vps-gateway` (31), `app-channel-fd` (27), `runtime` (16), `cli` (15), `engine-access` (13), `connector-integrity` (13), `config` (13). Note that `vps-*` is Linux/VPS-only by design (S25.8), so part of W6 is gating those suites rather than porting them.
  - This is the number the plan needed: the daemon port is not a handful of `path.join` fixes, it is 23 test files' worth of platform assumptions, and now they are enumerated instead of guessed.

- **L13 (2026-10-11, user + agent):** W5 landed in `c6f9c81`. The user chose **gate the assertion and document the limit** over building real Windows ACLs, so this is a recorded decision rather than an implementation detail.
  - **Why that is the right call, on evidence rather than convenience.** `odd/tasks/gentle-dot.md:93` states the trust boundary: *"the agent is a same-user process with a shell, so files and environment variables are never secrets."* The modes matter as a boundary only where the engine runs as a **different OS user** — S25.7 on Linux and S25.8 on the VPS (`gentle-dot.md:100-101`). On a Windows desktop the engine runs as the same user, so 0600 was never protecting against the agent: it protects against **other local users of the machine**, and the ACL of the user profile already gives that. Real `icacls` work would be cost and fragility bought for a threat the profile ACL already covers.
  - **What was built.** `packages/daemon/test/support/posix-modes.ts` exports `hasPosixModes(platform = process.platform)`, `expectPosixMode`, and `expectPosixLstatMode`. On a platform with modes it asserts the identical expression as before, so failure messages are unchanged. Otherwise it skips **loudly**: a module-level warn-once prints the reason and points here. The injectable platform parameter exists so the Windows branch is provable without Windows, and no call site passes it, so a POSIX run cannot be made to skip by accident. Verified by an independent agent: scope containment, comment-only source change, and helper honesty all PASS; `pnpm test` 793 passed / 13 skipped / 0 failed against a 790/13 baseline, the delta being the helper.
  - **A correction to L12, and it was my error.** The "41 assertions" figure was wrong: it came from counting **raw lines in the CI log**, which duplicates every failure — a GitHub `##[error]` annotation per failing test (22) plus vitest's inline detail printouts (19). It was reported as a count of assertions, which it never was. The measured truth: the code holds **31 sites** (19 × 0o600, 9 × 0o700, 2 × 0o644, 1 × 0o711), **22 tests failed** over **19 distinct lines**, and **9 of the 31 sites were never reached on Windows** because an earlier assertion threw or the test timed out in a `waitFor`. The writer that did W5 replaced all 31, including those 9. The lesson is the unit, not the arithmetic: a failure count from a log and a site count from source are different measurements and are not interchangeable.
  - **Two residuals of the same family, found by the verifier and reported instead of quietly dropped:** `runtime-build.test.ts:108` asserts `mode & 0o111` (the executable bit), and `connector-secrets.test.ts:598` asserts `existsSync(unreadable)` where the test creates unreadability with `chmod 0o000`. Both still fail on Windows. They are W5c — deliberately not folded into this commit, because moving a tree that an independent agent has just verified is a mistake already made once in this port.
  - **The limit is not published yet.** It is recorded here, in the technical log. The user-facing statement — the equivalent of what S25.7 declares for Linux — belongs in `docs/install.md`, whose Windows section is W14. Said plainly so it is not mistaken for done.

- **L14 (2026-10-12, agent, evidence):** The W5 confirmation run (`38188550247`) measured what W5 actually bought, and the review of the post-W5 candidate closed approved with zero CRITICAL findings.

  | Measurement | Before W5 (`38184877146`) | After W5 (`38188550247`) |
  | :--- | :--- | :--- |
  | Test files | **23 failed** / 54 passed / 7 skipped (84) | **16 failed** / 62 passed / 7 skipped (85) |
  | Tests | — | 38 failed / **748 passed** / 20 skipped (806) |
  | `expected 438 to be …` occurrences | 22 tests | **0** |

  - W5 fixed **7 of the 23 failing files**, and the permission class is gone from the Windows run entirely. That is the effect W5 was for, and it is now observed rather than predicted.
  - **The 16 remaining files map onto the plan**, which is the useful part: `app-channel-fd` → **W7** (the fd-3 channel); `engine-access`, `vps-auth-helper`, `vps-gateway` → **W6** (`seteuid`/`setegid`, and the VPS suites that need gating rather than porting); `config` → **W4** (the unexplained object-shape difference, plus path handling); `runtime-build` (the `0o111` bit) and `connector-secrets` (chmod-faked unreadability) → **W5c**. The rest — `auth`, `child-guard`, `cli`, `gateway`, `isolation`, `rotation`, `runtime`, `supervisor`, `uploads` — had *both* a permission failure and another cause, so they were already expected to survive W5.
  - **Review:** lineage `review-5868cfa176624be5` on the post-W5 candidate (25 paths, 629 lines, tier `high`) returned **approved** with **zero CRITICAL**, so the W5 design — including the new `posix-modes.ts` and the `private-file.ts` comment — was cleared by four independent lenses. Its authority is burned. This is the review the `.gitattributes` change was deferred into, so the deferral the user agreed to is settled.
  - **Ten advisory findings**, all non-blocking, and three of them (`R1-001` at `windows.yml:42`, `R2-001` at `windows.yml:26-29`, `R3-1` at `windows.yml:84`) are **the same locations the previous lineage reported**. A finding that survives two independent reviews is a real finding, even when it is minor. All of them fold into W17, which must be de-duplicated when it is worked rather than treated as nineteen separate items.
  - Two units, finally measured cleanly and worth keeping apart after the L13 correction: the run reports **16 failed test files** and **38 failed tests**. A file count and a test count are different numbers describing the same run.

- **L15 (2026-10-12, agent + verifier, evidence):** W5c landed in `0778421` and closed the mode family. It also produced three findings worth more than the change itself.
  - **The over-gating risk was the one that mattered and it passed.** Gating a whole test is only acceptable if the test still genuinely checks something on the host where it runs. An independent verifier confirmed the N2 test **executes rather than skips** on Linux (`✓`, not `↓`), and that its body is byte-identical to `HEAD` apart from indentation and the wrapper — no assertion weakened, removed, or loosened. Full suite `79 passed | 6 skipped` files, `793 passed | 13 skipped` tests, 0 failed, with a zero delta explained: no new `it` blocks were added, the calls went inside existing tests.
  - **`runtime-build.test.ts` cannot leave the failing list, and now we know why.** Both causes were confirmed independently: a **file** symlink at `:117` needs `SeCreateSymbolicLinkPrivilege`, and a junction cannot substitute because junctions are directory-only; and `bin/pi` is generated as a `#!/bin/sh` script (`runtime-build.ts:37-49`) spawned at `:120`, which Windows cannot execute. That is **W5d** — the `.cmd` half belongs to W11, and the symlink fixtures need their own decision because a privilege requirement, a junction, and a rewrite are three different answers.
  - **A limitation in the solver we built, reported by the verifier and not by its author.** `warnSkipped` fires once per process (`posix-modes.ts:14-22`), so on a Windows run only the **first** skipped assertion warns; every later one passes green with no signal at all. The intent — never silently green — therefore holds at the run level and not at the test level. Worth knowing before trusting that signal.
  - **The verifier also corrected the worker's own coverage note, and me.** The worker listed the `build:runtime` wrapper test as "unverified"; it is not — it is un-gated, so it will run and fail. And on the TDD disclosure, the worker claimed no RED was reachable, I endorsed that reading, and **we were both wrong**: `posix-modes.test.ts` imports `expectPosixExecutable`, so a test-first order fails at compile time on this Linux host. The claim held for the existing tests and not for the new unit. A third party caught it because the question was asked of one instead of settled by the author and the orchestrator agreeing with each other.
  - Additional gaps the verifier found that the worker had not named, both in the same file: a directory symlink at `:80`, and a node stub written as a `#!/bin/sh` script with `mode: 0o755` at `:113`. These fold into W5d and, with the rest, into the documentation gap W14 already carries.

- **L16 (2026-10-12, agent, evidence):** Lineage `review-680bafc875581bb4` reviewed the post-W5c candidate (27 paths, 738 lines) and returned **approved** with zero CRITICAL; its authority is burned. It is the third reviewed candidate in this port and, like the two before it, produced no blocking finding — but it produced one that matters, by convergence.
  - **A defect found four times over.** `packages/daemon/test/support/posix-modes.ts:16-25` was flagged independently by three of the four lenses in a single review — `R1-002` (risk), `R2-005` (readability), `R3-1` (reliability, `WARNING`) — and `:28-34` by a fourth (`R3-2`, `WARNING`). The independent verifier had already found the same thing on its own: `warnSkipped` fires once per process, so a Windows run warns about the **first** skipped assertion and passes every later one green in silence. The design I specified — "skip loudly, not silently" — therefore delivers at the run level and not at the test level.
  - **Why this is worth more than the review cost.** Five separate observers converged on one defect in the helper we built to stop silent passes. It is a defect *in the guard*, which is the worst place to have one, and no single reviewer would have been persuasive alone. It is **W18**, its own unit, and it should be fixed before the mode gating is trusted anywhere else.
  - The repeat findings continue to accumulate: `windows.yml:42`, `windows.yml:26-29`, `windows.yml:79-84`, `platform.rs:239-244`, and `status.rs:9-11` have now each been reported across two or more independent reviews. W17 therefore carries **29 findings from three lineages**, and de-duplication is the first step of that work, not an afterthought.
  - **The pattern across three reviews, recorded honestly:** every candidate was approved, no CRITICAL was ever found in the daemon test work, and the review's real output has been advisory findings plus one convergence on a design flaw. That is not an argument against running it — it is what the evidence says, and it should inform how often it is run on test-only deltas.

- **L17 (2026-10-15, user + agent, decision):** The five comments on `Gentleman-Programming/gentle-dot#57` were answered, and the plan absorbed three changes from that thread. Four replies were published and one deliberate omission was made (the `clean`, finding-free fleet review needs no answer).
  - **W7 is taken, not written.** `aiorellana502` reported a reviewed named-pipe implementation for the fd-3 replacement, on a branch pushed nowhere. The user chose to take it: the unit is the most expensive in the port, this is a spare-time project, and an implementation to review plus a trust model to document teaches more than starting from a blank file. The reply accepted it and asked for the branch or a draft PR, and it lands through the same review as any other unit. **The part still likely to change is the identity binding:** `GetNamedPipeClientProcessId` is a weaker claim than the pre-shared secret the fd-3 channel carries, so if it does not close the S25 handover the named pipe is reused and only that layer is replaced.
  - **W18 gets the per-call contract.** `danielgap` stacked the fix as `Row0902/gentle-dot#1` and asked which contract to use. Per-test-name dedup reintroduces the original hole one level down (a second gated assertion inside the same test goes silent again), so the answer is per call, which is what his change already does. One addition requested before the merge: name the `kind`, `path` and `mode` in the warning. That detail is the only part of this repository's own discarded variant worth keeping; the variant is preserved at `.git/w18-local-variant.patch` and still applies cleanly, so reverting it from the tree lost nothing.
  - **A new W14 obligation, found by `danielgap` on real Windows 10.** `.gitattributes` (W16) heals a fresh clone but **not** an existing Windows checkout that changes branches: files identical between `main` and the branch keep their CRLF, `biome check` reports 178 errors, and `git checkout-index -f -a` does not apply the eol conversion either. The fix that worked is renormalizing the tracked files and confirming `git diff --stat` stays empty. It is recorded against W14 because it is a developer-facing instruction; W16 keeps the fresh-clone contract.
  - **Two consumer-Windows flavours are now environment facts rather than port defects**, from that same run: symlink creation fails with `EPERM` on a non-elevated Windows 10 without Developer Mode, and `listen` on the afunix path fails with `EACCES` rather than `ENOENT`. This port's Windows 11 host has Developer Mode, so it measures `EPERM = 0` and **cannot see the W5d class at all**: the 20-to-16 difference in failing files between the two machines is exactly the four symlink-dependent files. W5d is scoped as "a privilege, a junction, or a rewrite, when a consumer actually hits it", and the Windows 10 run is currently the only coverage that class has.
  - **A correction published in the thread:** `danielgap` attributed the 84→85 file count and the mode gating to W5c. It was W5 (`c6f9c81`) that added `posix-modes.ts` and its test; W5c (`0778421`) came after and gated the two residual mode-family failures. The units stay apart (L13).
  - **W4 is split, which is a route change and not a scope change:** W4a pure paths, W4b the `config.test.ts` object-shape difference that path handling does not explain, W4c shell and program resolution. Splitting keeps each review narrow and lets W4b be answered on its own evidence instead of riding inside a mechanical diff.

- **L18 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** The first native `cargo test` on Windows ever run for this port, on this Windows 11 host (Developer Mode, NTFS checkout) at `471635e`'s parent. It closed the largest evidence gap in the port and corrected the plan twice over. **The prediction was wrong, and the first run failed before compiling a single line of this crate.**
  - **W8's `icon.ico` is a hard prerequisite for compiling on Windows at all, which nobody had named.** Run 1 built 300 dependency crates, then died in `build.rs:63`: `failed to run tauri-build: .../icons/icon.ico not found; required for generating a Windows Resource file during tauri-build`, cargo exit 101. `icons/` carried `icon.icns`, `icon.png`, `src/source.png` and the PNG set, but no `.ico`. **Consequence for the routing order:** the plan put W6 and W7 before W8, and the CI `rust` job has been sitting in `continue-on-error` failing for *this* reason, not for the `std::os::unix` imports everyone assumed. W6 and W7 cannot be verified on Windows until W8's icon exists.
  - **Fix, and why only the `.ico` was taken.** `tauri icon` for `apps/desktop/src-tauri/icons/source.png` regenerated the whole set; the hashes confirm the regenerated `32x32.png`, `64x64.png`, `128x128.png`, `128x128@2x.png` and `icon.png` are **byte-identical** to the committed ones, so the command that produced them originally is the same one. `icon.icns` is the exception: it differs, so it was left untouched, and nothing but `icon.ico` entered the tree (`471635e`). The Windows bundle icon list was deliberately not added to the shared `tauri.conf.json`; that belongs in the `tauri.windows.conf.json` W8 still has to create.
  - **Run 2, after the icon, 19 seconds on cached dependencies: the crate compiles and reports 57 errors in its own sources.** This is the first real inventory of the Rust side, and it replaces guesswork with counts.

    | Location | Error locations |
    | :--- | :--- |
    | `src/daemon.rs` | **46** (26 in production, 20 in `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` from `:642`) |
    | `src/app_channel.rs:28` | 1 |
    | `src/voice/app.rs:9` | 1 |

    By code: `E0425` ×20, `E0599` ×19, `E0433` ×13, `E0432` ×5. By class: `std::os::unix` (11 × `cannot find unix in os`), `std::os::fd` (5 × unresolved import), the POSIX extension traits (`from_mode` ×5, `ino` ×3, `mode` ×3, `custom_flags`, `as_raw_fd`, `pre_exec`), and `libc` POSIX symbols (`fcntl` ×3, `kill` ×2, `getppid` ×2, `pid_t` ×2, `SIGKILL` ×2, `O_NOFOLLOW` ×2, `O_NONBLOCK`, `RLIMIT_NOFILE`, `F_SETFD`, `F_DUPFD`, `F_DUPFD_CLOEXEC`, `FD_CLOEXEC`, `rlimit`), plus `ExitStatus::signal` ×2. The five `...\rustlib\...\std\src\os\mod.rs:78` lines in the output are the `std` note attached to `cannot find unix in os`, not separate files.
  - **Three corrections to the plan, all from the measurement rather than from reasoning.** (1) **W6 is not "gate a few modules": 46 of 57 error locations sit in one file, `daemon.rs`**, and they are not concentrated in one shape: 26 production and 20 test. (2) **`disclaimed.rs` produced zero errors** and **`libc` compiles on Windows**; what the plan named as `libc` work is really POSIX *usage* inside `daemon.rs`. (3) **W7's compilation cost is far smaller than assumed:** the fd-3 channel's own file, `app_channel.rs`, contributes exactly **one** error, its `std::os::fd` import at `:28`. Replacing the channel is not what blocks the build.
  - **What is still not measured, stated so it is not mistaken for done.** This is a compilation inventory only. `cargo test` has never *run* a test on Windows, so runtime behaviour on this platform remains unobserved, and the CI `rust` job stays in `continue-on-error` until it can compile. The next measurement is the same command once W6's production half is gated.

- **L20 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W4's first slice, and the measurement that collapsed its three-way split into two. The RED was captured natively on Windows 11 before any edit: seven files, **18 failing tests**, 91 passed, 1 skipped.
  - **The RED by test, and where each one belongs:**

    | File | Failing test (abbreviated) | The failure | Unit |
    | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
    | `config.test.ts` | uses local-only defaults … | a `/node_modules\/gentle-pi\/bi…/` regex against a `\`-separated path | W4a |
    | `config.test.ts` | keeps the assistant's own home … override | `expected 'D:\h' to be '/h'` | W4a |
    | `config.test.ts` | reads config.json and env wins | `preferredFolder: "/projects"` arrived as `D:\projects` | W4a |
    | `config.test.ts` | keeps the engine in its own workspace | `preferredFolder: "/work/repo"` arrived as `D:\work\repo` | W4a |
    | `auth.test.ts` | `resolveAgentHome` belongs to the assistant | `` `${dataDir}/agent` `` against `join(dataDir, "agent")` | W4a |
    | `runtime.test.ts` | serves the runtime's web UI … | `GENTLE_DOT_UI_DIR: "/ui"` arrived as `D:\ui` | W4a |
    | `runtime.test.ts` | puts the runtime's `pi` first on PATH | the test joins with `":"`, the code with `path.delimiter` | W4a |
    | `runtime.test.ts` | finds the user's Engram via the login shell | `command -v engram` through `env.SHELL \|\| "/bin/sh"` | W4c |
    | `runtime.test.ts` | `resolveEngramBin` prefers ENGRAM_BIN … | `join(dir, "engram")` with no `.exe` | W4c |
    | `runtime.test.ts` | source checkout keeps the workspace UI … | a separator join | W4a |
    | `gateway.test.ts` | gives the agent its own HOME and XDG … | an environment-object comparison | W4a/W4c |
    | `gateway.test.ts` | shows the access key only on a terminal | the printed path used `\` where the test wanted `/` | W4a |
    | `rotation.test.ts` | leaves the current session working … | `chmodSync(dirname(first), 0o500)` does not make a folder unwritable on Windows, so the handoff **is** written and the rotation proceeds | **W5 residual** |
    | `uploads.test.ts` | enforces the per-message size and count | a different stored-file list | **unclassified** |
    | `cli.test.ts` | keeps the access key out of redirected output | `waitFor` timed out | **unclassified** |
    | `gateway.test.ts` | closes with 4401 when the token is wrong | 20 s timeout | W7 |
    | `cli.test.ts` | a daemon with the app's channel exits … | 20 s timeout | W7 |
    | `uploads.test.ts` | POST /upload requires the access key | 20 s timeout, `listen EACCES` on `pair.sock` | W7 |

    Counted: **W4a 9** (plus one shared with W4c), **W4c 2**, **W7 3**, **W5 residual 1**, and **2 unclassified**. Three of the seven files are failing for reasons that are not W4 at all.
  - **One classification in the first draft of this entry was wrong, and the correction is kept rather than quietly edited.** `rotation.test.ts` was first listed as W4a on the strength of a truncated message that looked like a separator difference. Reading the test showed the opposite: it simulates an unwritable directory with `chmodSync(dirname(first), 0o500)`, which Windows treats as an ordinary permission change, so the handoff is written and the session rotates. That is the same family as `child-guard`, and it belongs with W5d's fixtures whose mechanism does not port, not with W4a. The truncated message was enough to guess and not enough to classify, which is the same mistake L12 made with W4b.
  - **W4b is falsified, and the correction matters more than the fix it saves.** It existed for one sentence in L12: two `config.test.ts` failures where, it said, "path handling alone does not explain" the object shape. The measurement says otherwise and says it plainly: `preferredFolder: "/projects"` becomes `D:\projects`, and `"/work/repo"` becomes `D:\work\repo`. That is path handling and nothing else. L12 was right to refuse to guess at the time and wrong to keep it classified as a separate cause; it is dropped and its two tests live in W4a. **The lesson is the one L13 already taught:** a warning written from a symptom is a hypothesis until the values are printed, and printing them cost one test run.
  - **The daemon's own path and PATH handling is already platform-neutral in almost every place measured.** `config.ts` and `auth.ts` call `resolve` and `join`; `isolation.ts:163-164` splits and joins PATH with `path.delimiter`. The failures are the tests asserting POSIX-shaped strings, not the daemon doing POSIX things. W4a is therefore far cheaper than L12's estimate, and W4c is where the real porting sits.
  - **Slice 1, and how it was verified.** Seven assertions in `config.test.ts` and `auth.test.ts`, in `dec85d3`: the forward-slash regex normalises separators before matching, and three POSIX absolute literals become `join(dataDir, …)` expressions that are identical on POSIX and correct on NTFS. After the change those two files report **24 passed, 0 failed** (from 5 failures). No assertion checks less than before: the `GENTLE_DOT_AGENT_HOME` override is still proven to win over the default, and `config.ts`'s resolution is still exercised through `preferredFolder`.
  - **Linux non-regression is argued, not measured, and is recorded as such.** This host is Windows, so the POSIX side cannot be executed here. Every replacement is the same expression on POSIX, since `join(dataDir, "agent")` produces the string the template literal did and `replaceAll("\\", "/")` is a no-op when there is no backslash. That is safe by construction, which is a weaker claim than safe by measurement, and it is not reported as a pass.
  - **Slice 2, and what it left behind.** In `a1b4616`: the UI override literal and two PATH joins in `runtime.test.ts`, plus the token path in `gateway.test.ts`. Measured after: `runtime.test.ts` and `gateway.test.ts` go from 5 failed tests to **3**, with 43 passing. The first attempt at the PATH cases failed and the reason is worth keeping: the test's **input** PATH was POSIX-shaped too, so `"/usr/bin:/bin"` was one entry under the Windows delimiter while the expectation described three. Fixing the expectation alone compared three entries against one. **Left behind, and not claimed:** the login-shell and known-folder Engram lookups are W4c, since `runtime.ts` probes `command -v` through `/bin/sh` and joins a bare `"engram"`; and the PATH matcher in `gateway.test.ts`'s "own instance" still fails for a reason not yet established.
  - **W4a's running ledger:** fixed so far are the seven assertions of slice 1 and the four of slice 2, across `config.test.ts`, `auth.test.ts`, `runtime.test.ts` and `gateway.test.ts`. Still W4a by classification: the separator join in `gateway.test.ts`'s "own instance" environment comparison, and the two unclassified cases in `uploads.test.ts` and `cli.test.ts`.

- **L21 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** The Windows baseline re-measured cleanly after W4a's first two slices, and the measurement-method correction that forced the re-run.

  | Measurement | Baseline (`4a60420`) | After slices 1 and 2 (`7d000a7`) |
  | :--- | :--- | :--- |
  | Test files | 16 failed / 62 passed / 7 skipped (85) | **13 failed** / 65 passed / 7 skipped (85) |
  | Tests | 43 failed / 742 passed / 21 skipped (806) | **28 failed** / 757 passed / 21 skipped (806) |
  | Test-run errors | 3 | 3 |
  | Duration | 183.7 s | 168.3 s |

  - **How much of the delta belongs to this work, stated honestly.** Seven assertions were changed across four files, so **2 of the 3 files and 7 of the 15 tests are attributable**: `config.test.ts` and `auth.test.ts` pass outright now. The remaining 1 file and 8 tests are **not claimed**. This suite carries several 20 s timeouts and two files that listen on an afunix socket, so its failing counts move between runs, and the baseline was itself measured with the discarded W18 variant applied. The honest claim is "three fewer failing files and fifteen fewer failing tests, of which two files and seven tests are explained by these commits".
  - **The three test-run errors are unchanged and all one cause:** `listen EACCES` on `pair.sock`, in `app-channel-fd.test.ts` and `cli.test.ts`. That is W7's class, and it is the reason W7 exists.
  - **A measurement error worth keeping, because it produced a false regression.** The first full run after slice 1 reported **17 failing files against a baseline of 16**, which reads as a regression introduced by the change. It was not: focused `vitest` runs were issued while that suite was running, and these tests compete for the same temp-path sockets and the same 20 s timeouts. The clean re-run, with nothing else running, is the table above. **A baseline and a comparison are only comparable when they are measured the same way**, and "run the suite once, alone" is part of the method rather than a convenience.

- **L22 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W4c is not one unit, and half of it is a decision rather than a port. Reading `runtime.ts` and the tests that exercise it, before writing anything, turned up two things that change how it should be worked.
  - **Its probe half cannot be verified until W5d is resolved.** The only tests for the login-shell lookup build their fixture with `fakeShell`, which writes a `#!/bin/sh` script (`runtime.test.ts:31-34`). Windows cannot execute that, so the probe finds nothing and the lookup falls through to the runtime's own copy. That is exactly the observed failure: `Expected …\engram` against `Received …\bin\engram`. The production code would have to be wrong for some *other* reason to matter, and right now the **fixture** is wrong first. That puts it in W5d's class, and makes W5d a prerequisite for verifying this half rather than a separate later unit.
  - **The other half is a product decision, and the options are not equivalent.** `SYSTEM_PATH = "/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin"` together with `env.SHELL || "/bin/sh"` running `command -v engram` encodes one POSIX idiom: ask the user's login shell where their Engram is. **Windows has no login shell and no `command -v`**, so something has to replace that question, and the replacements do not find the same things:
    - **(a) Drop the probe on Windows** and rely on `ENGRAM_BIN`, then `engramKnownDirs(home)`, then the runtime's own copy. Smallest change, and probably loses nothing: the known-folder list already covers the usual places, and the inherited PATH is normally already the user's.
    - **(b) Read the user's persisted PATH** from `HKCU\Environment\Path`, so a user who deliberately put Engram on their PATH keeps that Engram. Closest to the POSIX intent; costs a registry read plus a Windows-only test for it.
    - **(c) Probe a shell Windows does have** (`cmd /C where engram`, or PowerShell). Most machinery for the least benefit, and `where` does not answer the same question as `command -v` in a login shell.
  - **This is asked rather than decided.** It changes where the daemon looks for a binary on the target platform, this host cannot show the difference at runtime, and a wrong choice is invisible here and merely annoying on Windows. The `.exe` suffix is not part of the question, because W11 already specifies `bin/engram.exe`.

The prefix now comes from `join("node_modules", ".bin")`. That file goes from 1 failed to **37 passed**.

The host separator was leaking out of the *observation helper*, so the helper normalises now. A no-op on POSIX, and it makes the helper report what the store reports.

  - **What changed (`cb5e3fe`).** `loginShellEngram` returns `undefined` on `win32` by construction, instead of spawning something that cannot exist and falling through on the failure; that is the difference between a decision and an accident. The file to look for now comes from a new pure `engramBinaryName(platform)`, which is `engram.exe` on Windows, because Windows resolves a program by its extension and never by a mode bit, so a bare `engram` would have matched nothing. The platform is an injected value on `resolveEngramBin` and on that function, the way platform doors are written elsewhere in this port.
  - **The Windows contract is asserted, not skipped.** An injected-platform test proves the login shell is never consulted on Windows and that a bare `engram` in a known folder is not adopted. Only the POSIX probe test is skipped there, because it spawns a real shell and there is none to spawn; the skip is visible in the report and the injected test covers the same ground.
  - **Verification.** `runtime.test.ts` 10 passed / 1 skipped. `pnpm typecheck` clean. `biome check` clean on both changed files, after biome's own formatter wrapped one signature that was 101 columns wide. In the seven-file selector, failing tests go from 4 to **2**, and neither is W4: `cli.test.ts`'s app-channel timeout (W7) and `rotation.test.ts`'s chmod simulation (W5 residual). The selector reads 108 passed / 2 failed / 2 skipped of 112; the total moved from 110 because one case was split and one added, and skipped moved from 1 to 2 for the POSIX-only probe.
  - **The limitation of option (a), stated rather than left implicit.** On Windows the chain is `ENGRAM_BIN`, then the known folders, then the copy the runtime carries. `engramKnownDirs` returns POSIX conventions (`~/go/bin`, `/opt/homebrew/bin`, `/usr/local/bin`, `~/.local/bin`), and **the chain never consults `PATH` at all**. So a Windows user's own Engram is found only when `ENGRAM_BIN` is set; otherwise the runtime's copy is used, which serves whatever data dir the caller passes. **L22's justification for (a) said "the inherited PATH is normally already the user's", and that was imprecise:** it described what the POSIX probe does (spawn a shell that computes PATH, then search it), not what the remaining chain does. Reading `env.PATH` directly for `engram.exe` would close the gap with no registry read, and it is left as a decision rather than taken, because it adds a lookup location the user did not choose.

Measured: pnpm's `.bin` on this Windows host carries `pi`, `pi.CMD` and `pi.ps1`, so the bare name is there and the check holds. For an installed app that same function returns `<runtime>/bin` without testing a name at all. There is no gap, and this is written down so the guess is not made twice.
  - **W4's final ledger.** W4a: eleven assertions across five test files and no production code. W4c: one production function and its tests. W4b: falsified and dropped. The whole of W4 is smaller than L12 estimated, because the daemon was already platform-neutral where it mattered and the tests were what asserted POSIX.

- **L29 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W6's first slice, the privacy surface, and the two-sided verification channel W6 needs and this host does not have by default.

They are now four helpers with a `#[cfg(unix)]` and a `#[cfg(not(unix))]` arm: `create_private`, `open_private_read`, `create_private_dir`, `open_appended_private`. The Unix arms are the same calls the code already made, moved rather than rewritten; the Windows arms create and open plainly, which is L13's recorded decision (no mode bits, privacy from the profile ACL).
