  - **The verification problem this exposed, which matters more than the slice.** Rust type-checks only the active cfg, so on this host the `cfg(unix)` arms are never compiled. The repository has **no Linux or macOS CI** — W3's workflow is Windows-only and the README leaves the other platforms to the developer — so a broken Unix arm fails nothing here and would surface on the next Fedora or macOS build. **W6 inverts the plan's verification assumption:** the plan says "this phase is developed from a Linux host", and from Windows it is the POSIX half that cannot be compiled locally.
  - **The channel, found and used.** The Android target was tried first because its std was already installed, and it is not usable: `cargo check --target x86_64-linux-android` dies in `ring`'s build script looking for `clang.exe`, which is the NDK, so that failure is in a dependency rather than in this crate. What works is the Fedora distribution in WSL, which carries `cargo 1.99.0` and the other checkout of this repository. With the user's authorisation for a target directory in that distribution's `/tmp`, the check reads the crate from `/mnt/d` (and therefore sees uncommitted work) and writes only build artifacts there.
  - **The result: `cargo check --lib --locked` compiled `gentle-dot` and finished with 0 errors** in 4 m 58 s, dependencies from scratch. The Unix arms compile, and W6 now has a two-sided check reachable from this machine.
  - **What that check does and does not prove.** It proves the Unix arms compile; their behaviour is preserved by being the same calls moved, with no logic edited. A `cargo test` on the Unix side is the stronger check, and it is the one this port should end W6 with rather than this one.
  - **Deliberately not committed until the check passed.** Nothing in this repository would have caught a broken Linux build, so the only honest order was to verify both arms first and commit second.

- **L30 (2026-10-15, user + agent, evidence):** W6's second slice, the fd-3 channel, gated under the user's **option A**, and the decision that made it small.
  - **The decision.** Windows has no fd 3, so the app cannot carry the private channel there and cannot answer the connector and approval requests that travel over it. The user chose **A: the app accepts sharing**, so `with_channel` declares no handler on Windows and the app behaves as one that never asked for a channel: it attaches to whatever answers on the port, or starts its own. **B would have required `stop_orphan` in Win32** (`OpenProcess` + `TerminateProcess`), which is porting rather than gating.
  - **Why A is the honest interim, not just the cheap one.** Until W7 lands, Windows has no channel under either option, so connectors and approvals are refused either way. Insisting on ownership therefore buys nothing today and costs a unit with its own risk. When W7 arrives the channel returns and `with_channel` recovers its body, with nothing under A to undo.
  - **The trap that was avoided, written down because it is the kind that ships.** Gating `wants_channel` alone, while leaving the handler set, makes `start_plan` return `Attach` and then leaves `wait_ready`'s `(self.handler.is_none() || self.owns_running())` false forever: the app would **hang** waiting for a daemon it is already connected to, rather than fail. Option A keeps `handler` at `None`, so the existing no-channel rule applies unchanged and `wait_ready` is never touched.
  - **What changed (`9b83859`).** `with_channel` gains two arms; `channel_pair`, the `std::os::fd` import and the `UnixStream` import are `#[cfg(unix)]`; `spawn_daemon`'s non-macOS arm becomes `all(unix, not(macos))` and gains a Windows arm that passes stdio and the log and no channel; `cloexec_from` follows its caller; and `Daemon::spawn`'s pair, `channel_end` and `AppChannel` block are gated. The mechanism is gated rather than removed, because W7 is what replaces it and nothing here pretends to.
  - **Measured on Windows 11: `cargo build` goes from 22 errors to 7**, and the seven are exactly the two families predicted before the change: six in the process and signal region (slice 2b) and one in `app_channel.rs` (W6b). Nothing from the channel remains.
  - **A consequence that resolves slice 2b's hardest part.** `stop_orphan` is reached only from `StartPlan::ReplaceOrphan`, which `start_plan` returns only when `wants_channel` is true — and with A that is false on Windows. So the orphan path is **unreachable by construction** there. It still has to compile, but a documented no-op is now honest rather than a gap: it cannot leave a port occupied, because nothing calls it. What the earlier plan called "a no-op that leaves the daemon holding the port" was a property of option B, not of the platform.
  - **Verified as hard as the first slice, and harder where it counts.** `cargo check --tests` with the Fedora toolchain in WSL finished with **0 errors in 6 m 04 s**, covering the test module as well as the lib, because these gates touch `Daemon::spawn` and the `UnixStream` import the tests use.

- **L31 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W6's third slice, the process and signal region, and a correction to the taxonomy the whole unit was sized from.
  - **What changed.** Three functions gain a Windows arm. `may_be_daemon` loses the `getppid` parent check, because Windows has no `getppid` in `std` and this process's parent is not readable there without Win32. `stop_orphan` becomes empty. `stop_child` terminates the child outright, because Windows has no `SIGTERM` and `std` offers no way to ask a child to stop, so the polite phase cannot exist and `grace` has nothing to wait for.
  - **The no-op is honest because option A made it unreachable**, which is the part worth keeping. `stop_orphan` is reached only from `StartPlan::ReplaceOrphan`, and `start_plan` returns that only when the app wants a channel; on Windows `with_channel` declares none, so nothing calls it. It exists so the match stays whole. What the plan called "a no-op that leaves the daemon holding the port" was a property of **option B**, not of the platform.
  - **One degradation is real and is not dressed up.** Stopping a daemon from the app on Windows is a termination rather than a request to finish. On Unix the daemon gets `SIGTERM` and a grace period first. Windows has no equivalent in `std`, and the daemon cannot stop itself the way it documents either, because that self-stop is driven by the channel closing and there is no channel there yet. W7 restores the mechanism; until then, a stop is abrupt.
  - **A correction to the taxonomy, and the whole unit was sized from it.** `voice/app.rs:9` was never one of the errors. It is the pre-existing `unused import: Preferred` warning, present in the first build of this session too, and the `awk` that produced the per-file breakdown counted `-->` lines belonging to warnings as well as errors. So the 33 errors were **32 in `daemon.rs` and 1 in `app_channel.rs`**, which is exactly the arithmetic the slices produced: 22 after the privacy slice, 7 after the channel slice, 1 after this one.
  - **Measured on Windows 11: 7 errors to 1.** Verified the same way as the others: `cargo check --tests` under the Fedora toolchain in WSL, **0 errors in 5 m 17 s**.
  - **What W6b actually is, found by reading `app_channel.rs` rather than trusting the count.** `AppChannel` is built on `UnixStream`: the `writer` field, `start(stream: UnixStream)`, `read(BufReader<UnixStream>)`, and its own tests. Gating it therefore gates the **type**, and that cascades into `daemon.rs` (the `channel` field, `channel()`, the `self.channel` assignments) and `shell.rs` (the wiring that uses the channel). `Handler` and `APP_FD` must survive the gating, because `with_channel` receives the handler on Windows too. So W6b is a medium slice with a cascade, the same shape as L30's, and not the one-line change the error count suggested.

- **L32 (2026-10-15, user + agent, evidence):** W6b, the cascade that closed the lib, and the first time the Rust shell has been built for Windows.
  - **The decision inside it, taken by the user.** `connector_command` asks the daemon for its channel and reports `NO_CHANNEL` when there is none. On Windows there is never a channel yet, and `NO_CHANNEL`'s text blames the wrong thing: it says "Gentle Dot did not start this assistant", which is false there, and advises quitting a daemon the app did start. A second constant, `NO_CHANNEL_WINDOWS`, now says what is actually true: connector changes need the private channel between the app and its assistant, and this platform does not carry it yet.
  - **What the cascade was.** Gating `AppChannel` gated a type three files depend on, so it all had to land together or the build would get worse rather than better: `app_channel.rs` (the `AppChannel` type and its `impl`, the `SecretParams` group with its `Debug` impl, `serve_secret`, `Reply`, and the test module), `daemon.rs` (the `AppChannel` import, the `channel` field, `channel()`, and the take inside `stop()`), and `shell.rs` (the `opened` method of the `Handler` impl, the body of `connector_command`, and the `computer` field, which is read only by `opened`).
  - **Measured: `cargo build` reaches 0 errors and produces a binary.** `Finished dev profile` with no errors, and `target/debug/gentle-dot.exe` is 35 MB. **This is the first time the Rust shell has been built for Windows at all**, lib and bin, which the plan's verification constraint expected never to happen on the development host.
  - **The gating left dead code behind, and it was cleaned rather than left as noise.** The change produced seven unused-import warnings and four dead-code items on Windows, all in `app_channel.rs`: the io, sync and `HashMap` imports, `Shutdown`, `MAX_FRAME`, `TOO_LARGE`, `SecretParams` with its `Debug` impl and `parse`, and `serve_secret`. All of them are `#[cfg(unix)]` now. The warning count is 1, and it is the pre-existing `unused import: Preferred` in `voice/app.rs`, present before this session and not part of W6.
  - **One field is silenced deliberately rather than gated.** `ChannelService.computer` in `shell.rs` is read only by `opened`, and carries `#[cfg_attr(not(unix), allow(dead_code))]` with the reason beside it. Gating the field would have cascaded into its construction site for no benefit, and the endpoint genuinely has no reader on Windows.
  - **One edit was wrong and the compiler caught it, which is worth recording because the two-sided check would not have.** Gating `SecretParams` missed its `std::fmt::Debug` impl, and `cargo build` failed on the next run. That is the ordinary value of building: the arms this host compiles are checked here, and the arms it does not are checked under Fedora.
  - **Verified on Linux as well, and that side is clean too.** `cargo check --tests` under the Fedora toolchain in WSL reports **0 errors and 0 warnings** in 6 m 04 s, so the `cfg(unix)` arms compile and no gating left the POSIX build with unused imports either.

- **L33 (2026-10-15, user + agent, evidence):** The first real runs of the app on Windows, and four runtime findings in about fifteen minutes of it. This is the entry that says what compiling could not.
  - **It runs.** `gentle-dot.exe`, built by W6b, starts, provisions `~/.gentle-dot` (`agent/`, `sessions/`, `identity.md`, `token`), spawns its own daemon, and serves: `GET http://127.0.0.1:4317/` answers HTTP 200 with `<title>Gentle Dot</title>`, in 3 ms on the second run. The daemon logs the degraded mode option A designed, and on close the app exits with code 0 and takes its daemon with it, so `stop_child`'s abrupt Windows stop does what it promised.
  - **How to run it, which is not obvious and cost a wrong diagnosis.** `tauri.conf.json` sets `devUrl` to `http://127.0.0.1:5173` and `frontendDist` to the UI's dist folder. The windows are built with `WebviewUrl::App(...)`, and a **debug** build carries `cfg(dev)` (emitted by `tauri-build`), so that URL resolves against `devUrl` and not against the bundled UI. Running `target/debug/gentle-dot.exe` on its own therefore has every webview ask a dev server that is not there, and nothing loads; no retry can fix it, because the page it wants does not exist. **This is Tauri behaviour, not a port defect**, and it is why `tauri dev` runs `beforeDevCommand`: start `pnpm --filter @gentle-dot/ui dev --host 127.0.0.1 --port 5173 --strictPort` first (about a second), or build in release.
  - **Runtime finding 1, unfixed: `process_started` has no Windows arm.** It is still the placeholder that returns `None`, so `record_launch` fails on every spawn with `cannot record the assistant's pid: the assistant is not running` and the pid file is never written. With option A the orphan path is unreachable anyway, so the start plan still works, but the app cannot account for its own daemon. Porting it means Win32 (`GetProcessTimes`). **Decision pending.**
  - **Runtime finding 2, probably not ours: the engine's first run outlasts the agent timeout.** The first-run npm install of `gentle-pi` took about three minutes and the daemon gave up at 180 s: `agent failed to start: Agent did not answer get_state within 180000 ms; retrying`. Both the timeout and the provisioning are platform-neutral, so this is most likely an upstream first-run race that the port merely revealed. **Report it; do not fix it here.**
  - **Runtime finding 3, unfixed: the ungrateful stop leaves a provisioning lock behind.** A run closed mid-provisioning left `~/.gentle-dot/agent/.gentle-shell-setup.lock` in place, and the next run read it and skipped the engine setup with `another gentle-shell process is already provisioning ... skipping automatic setup for this run`. The lock is upstream and platform-neutral, **but the trigger is Windows**: Unix `stop_child` sends `SIGTERM` and the process gets to clean up, while Windows kills outright, which is the degradation L31 recorded as acceptable. **The degradation has a concrete cost**, and it is a first-run experience bug: close the app while it provisions and the engine stays half-installed while every later run skips the setup. Measured limit: the engine still started (`/health` reported `agentState: idle`), so it is not a dead end. **Decision pending.**
  - **Runtime finding 4, fixed in `a91ebfd`: `tray icon is missing`.** The webview's console reported it as an unhandled rejection, from `tray_by_id` returning `None` inside `set_dot_state`. The cause was ordering: `build_windows` ran first and `build_tray` second, so a webview that loaded the UI immediately could call `set_dot_state` before the tray existed. `build_tray` cannot have failed, because it propagates with `?` and the app started. **The two lines are now swapped**, and the verification is the Vite log: every `tray icon is missing` line is from the earlier run, and the run after the fix produced none.
  - **What this says about the method, and it is the entry's point.** Two of the four findings are Windows-specific, and **none of the four was visible to the compiler**. The two-sided check built in L29 proves the types close on both platforms; it says nothing about whether the Windows arms work. **Running the app is therefore a verification step for any unit that touches a platform arm, not an extra** — and it belongs in W6's closing checks, where it currently is not.
  - **A diagnostic worth keeping:** the Vite dev server's log is the only place the frontend's rejections appear. The app's own stdout does not show them.

- **L26 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W17's item 3 is closed. It is the one finding in that set with four independent observers, and the only one whose subject was still reachable.
  - **What changed.** `.github/workflows/windows.yml`'s `rust` job said `continue-on-error` comes off "when W7 lands". That condition was never the whole story: the job was failing before a single line of this crate compiled, because `tauri-build` needs `icons/icon.ico` (W8), and it now fails on W6's 57 compile errors instead. The comment states the honest condition (W6 for the compilation, W7 for the channel) and carries the inventory, so the job says what it actually reports. The display name moves from "until W7" to "until W6 and W7".
  - **Verified by parsing, not by inspection.** A CI file is not comment-only by accident, so the diff was checked to touch only comment lines and one scalar, and the document was then parsed with the `yaml` package already in the pnpm store: jobs are still `typescript` and `rust`, `continue-on-error` is still `true`, the rust job still has its four steps, and the `on:` triggers are unchanged. Comment text cannot break a YAML document, but "cannot" is worth one parse when the file's whole job is to run CI.
  - **Where W17 now stands.** Item 3 is this entry, item 5 was `d9c299f`, and item 6 is W18. Items 1, 2, 4, 7, 8, 9 and 10 remain locations whose finding text was never persisted, so each one has to be re-derived from the code as it stands rather than applied from the record.
  - **One W14 obligation is deliberately still open, and this is the deliberate part.** The reply in the PR thread said the `.gitattributes` finding would be "recorded against W14 as a line in `docs/install.md`". It is recorded against W14 in that row and in L17, with the exact instruction, and **the line itself has not been written into `docs/install.md`**, because that document has no Windows section yet: one stray Windows note in a macOS and Linux guide is a fragment of W14 rather than a piece of it. It lands with the rest of the section. `runtime.ts:25` hardcodes `SYSTEM_PATH = "/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin"`; `loginShellEngram` runs `env.SHELL || "/bin/sh"` with `command -v engram`; and both `resolveEngramBin` paths join a literal `"engram"` with no `.exe`. None of those can work on Windows, and all three are production code rather than fixtures.

- **L34 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W8's bundle configuration, measured, and the four things the measurement decided instead of assuming.
  - **What changed.** `apps/desktop/src-tauri/tauri.windows.conf.json` is new, following the shape `tauri.macos.conf.json` and `tauri.linux.conf.json` already set: `bundle.targets: ["nsis", "msi"]`, the Windows icon list `["icons/icon.ico"]` (L18's decision: the shared `tauri.conf.json` stays untouched so bundling on macOS and Linux is not put at risk), `publisher` and both descriptions mirroring Linux, and `windows.nsis.installMode: "currentUser"` so installing needs no administrator and the app lands per-user like everything else it owns.
  - **Measured: both bundles are produced, by the documented command.** `pnpm build` in `apps/desktop` (release, Windows 11) finished with `makensis` for the NSIS and WiX's `candle` + `light` for the MSI: `Gentle Dot_0.1.0_x64-setup.exe` (8,357,338 bytes, and 8,361,278 once L35 corrected the description) and `Gentle Dot_0.1.0_x64_en-US.msi` (11,890,688 bytes, unchanged by that correction). WiX was available without installing it by hand, which is what let the MSI stay in the target list rather than being demoted to a note.
  - **The payload was inspected, not installed.** `7z x` on the NSIS installer lists `gentle-dot.exe`, `uninstall.exe` and the NSIS plugins, and the MSI's `Property` table was read through the `WindowsInstaller` COM object opened read-only. The exe inside the installer hashes differently from the standalone `target/release/gentle-dot.exe`, which is the "patching with bundle type information" the build log reports; it carries `ProductName`/`FileDescription` `Gentle Dot`, `FileVersion` `0.1.0`, `CompanyName` `Gentleman Programming`, and its icon extracts to a 32×32 PNG that is the rose and byte-identical to the standalone exe's — so the embedded icon is the app's and not Tauri's default. The MSI reports `Manufacturer = Gentleman Programming`, `ProductName = Gentle Dot`, `ProductVersion = 0.1.0`, `ALLUSERS = 1` and a stable `UpgradeCode`. **Nothing was installed on this machine.**
  - **A difference worth recording before W11.** The NSIS installer is per-user (`currentUser`, `HKCU`); the MSI is per-machine (`ALLUSERS = 1`) and needs an administrator. Both ship, so that is a real choice for whoever installs, not an accident.
  - **A gap that is W11's, named so it is not mistaken for W8's.** Neither bundle carries a runtime: the payload is the app exe and its uninstaller only. `tauri build` alone cannot produce the self-contained installer SW5 promises, because `bundle.resources` is injected at release time by a packaging script (`lib.mjs`'s `tauriReleaseConfig`, whose only caller today is `package-mac.mjs` — the Linux builder stages its runtime differently and there is no `package-windows.mjs` yet), and the Windows one is W11. W8 makes the bundler produce a Windows installer; W11 makes it carry Node, the engine and Engram.
  - **`bundleVCRuntime` was decided by measurement, not by taste.** The field exists for sidecars that need the Visual C++ runtime. `objdump -p` on the `node.exe` 24.21.0 that W11 will stage imports only `KERNEL32`, `CRYPT32`, `WS2_32`, `USER32`, `dbghelp`, `ADVAPI32`, `IPHLPAPI`, `USERENV`, `SHELL32`, `ole32` and `WINMM` — no `VCRUNTIME140.dll` and no `MSVCP140.dll` — so the DLLs are not bundled and the installer stays small.
  - **`.cargo/config.toml` needed nothing, which is a falsified expectation rather than an omission.** W8's row named "`.cargo/config.toml` Windows targets" as work. `cargo build --release` in `apps/desktop/src-tauri` finished with exit 0 (7 m 02 s) and `pnpm build` then produced both bundles, with no Windows entry in that file, whose contents are macOS-only by design ("Everything here is scoped to macOS targets, so Linux builds use the system `cc`"). Adding an `[env]` block with this host's SDK paths would hardcode one machine's layout into the repository to work around a local shell that lacks MSVC's `INCLUDE`/`LIB`; that is a host fact, not a repository defect, and it was not committed. **A `cargo test` on this commit's tree was not run, and is not claimed:** a bundle-configuration change is exercised by `tauri-build` during the release build and by the bundler, both of which ran. The 404-passed `cargo test --lib` result recorded for the W7 review came from a different tree and is deliberately not cited here as W8's evidence.
  - **One build failure looked like the configuration and was not.** The first `pnpm build` died in `ring`'s build script with `cl.exe` exit code 2. `cargo build --release` from the same environment then compiled `ring` and finished with exit 0, and after `cargo clean -p ring` **the same `pnpm build` recompiled it and produced both bundles**. So it was not reproducible and is not attributable to the chain or to the new configuration; the likely cause is a resource-limited cold full-parallel build on this host. The measured statement is the honest one: it happened once and did not reproduce.
  - **`Mica` is split out as W8d, and W8's own row asked for it under a condition that is now the reason not to do it.** W8 said to request `Mica` "once a real Windows machine shows the blur lands", and this is one (Windows 11 Pro, build 26100, `EnableTransparency = 1`, not a remote session), so `Mica` would very likely land here. That is not the question that decides it: `Mica` does not exist on Windows 10, which this port supports, and Tauri discards the material's failure with no readback (L8), so an unconditional request makes the panel unreadable on exactly the platform the port is for. The visual check was therefore **not run, deliberately and not silently**: its result cannot change the conclusion for the machines that cannot be tested, and a screenshot of one machine is not evidence about the others. W8d carries the decision and the shape it would take.

- **L36 (2026-10-15, user + agent, decision):** W8d is deferred until the channel lands, and it takes the gated shape when it is picked up.
  - **The decision.** W7 is the functional gap — without a channel the app can neither change connectors nor answer an approval — while the panel's blur is cosmetic and blocks nothing. So W8d waits behind W7 instead of competing with it. When it is picked up it is not an unconditional request: it is a pure decision over the injected Windows facts (build ≥ 22000, `EnableTransparency`, `SM_REMOTESESSION`, DWM composition), with tests, replacing L8's blanket `an_unverified_backdrop_never_leaves_the_panel_transparent`.
  - **Why the ordering is not arbitrary.** W8d changes a user-visible surface that only one platform can see, and W7 changes the contract the whole port exists to restore. Deferring the cosmetic one keeps the review workload where the risk is.
  - **What is deliberately still unmeasured.** Whether `Mica` lands on this machine. It is Windows 11 Pro build 26100 with `EnableTransparency = 1` and no remote session, so it very likely would — but the answer cannot change the conclusion for Windows 10, RDP, or a compositor failure, so a screenshot of one machine would buy an anecdote rather than a decision. The row stays `pending` rather than `blocked`, because what it waits for is a prerequisite in this table (ordering), not someone outside it.

- **L38 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W6c closed the test-side POSIX surface, and the measurements that decided how.
  - **The classification came from what exists, not from what compiles.** All 21 primary error sites belong to a test, helper or import whose *subject* is absent on Windows: the fd-3 socketpair W7 replaces, POSIX permission modes, symlinks, fifos, inode identity, `SIGTERM`/`SIGKILL`, `getppid`, and `Daemon::channel` (which exists only under `cfg(unix)`, because option A gives Windows no channel). So "port it" was not available: porting these means inventing platform behavior, not adapting a test. The change is 29 `#[cfg(unix)]` attributes plus the split of one `std::io` import whose `BufRead`/`BufReader` half only the gated tests use. **No test was removed** — the `#[test]` count is 28 before and after — and no test body was rewritten except the two below.
  - **The coverage delta is measured rather than assumed.** 19 of `mod tests`' 28 tests are gated; 9 keep running on both platforms. A writer's first pass gated two more tests than the list it was given and reported that instead of doing it quietly; both are justified — one spawns `/bin/sleep` and calls `process_started`, which is a placeholder returning `None` on Windows so its `unwrap()` would panic, and the other asserts a POSIX mode.
  - **Two failures the gating exposed, and they are test bugs rather than product bugs.** `launch_spec_uses_build_time_paths` asserted a string suffix against a build-time path that Windows renders with `\`; `overrides_win_over_the_bundled_runtime` compared `runtime.join("node/bin/node").display().to_string()` against a `program` that production builds with `format!("{root}/node/bin/node")` at `daemon.rs:295`. Production is consistent with itself; both tests hardcoded a separator. They are fixed the way W4a fixed the TypeScript suite — compare components (`Path::ends_with`) and compare `Path`s rather than their renderings — rather than gated, because hiding a separator expectation behind a platform gate is the silently-green move W18 exists to prevent.
  - **One production line, and it is an attribute.** Gating removed the only callers of `Daemon::with_health` on Windows, so it warned as dead code. It carries `#[cfg_attr(not(unix), allow(dead_code))]` with the reason beside it, the same treatment L32 gave `ChannelService.computer`; the method itself is not gated, so the Unix API is untouched.
  - **Windows, verified by the parent rather than taken from the writer.** `cargo test` exits 0, lib target at `341 passed / 0 failed / 2 ignored`, **0 compile errors against 41 before**. The counts are worth reading carefully: the log's later `test result: ok. 0 passed` lines belong to the bin target and the doc-tests, and reading only those would have looked like an emptied suite.
  - **Linux, and one failure with its cause named.** `cargo test --lib` under the Fedora toolchain in WSL compiles every gated item and reports `381 passed / 1 failed / 6 ignored`. The failure is `config::tests::saves_the_shortcut_privately_in_place` asserting `0o600` at `config.rs:244`, a file this change does not touch, and a probe names the cause: the checkout lives on `/mnt/d`, a 9p drvfs mount of `D:\`, which reports mode `777` for every new file whatever creates it (`umask` is 0022, `/tmp` gives 644, `/mnt/d` gives 777). A POSIX mode assertion cannot hold on that mount, so the Linux side is verified as compiling and running with that one environment-bound assertion named instead of glossed.
  - **What this does and does not do for CI.** The reason the `rust` job could not become blocking is discharged: the test target compiles and Windows passes its suite. Removing `continue-on-error` is nevertheless its own decision, and it needs the workflow comment's condition rewritten first. **The job has still never executed on a runner**, so "expected to pass" is a prediction from a Windows 11 host and not a measurement of `windows-latest`.
  - **One limit stated so it is not read as done.** A gated test vanishes from the count instead of announcing itself, so on Windows nothing currently covers the arms W6 added: the abrupt `stop_child`, and the four private-file helpers' Windows arms. That is the W18 shape one level up, and it is named here rather than left implicit.

- **L37 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** `cargo test` on Windows at `7cffcf1`, measured because PR #57's body claimed it had never run.

By class: `E0599` ×17 (missing methods: `Permissions::mode`, `Metadata::dev`/`ino`, `ExitStatus::signal`, `Daemon::channel`), `E0425` ×9 (`libc::fcntl`, `getppid`, `mkfifo`, `SIGKILL`, `channel_pair`), `E0433` ×8 (`std::os::unix`), `E0061` ×4 (`channel_pair` arity), `E0432` ×2, `E0407` ×1 (`opened` is no longer a member of `Handler`, which is option A doing its job).

What matters is each error's **primary** location; `:431` is a secondary span of an error whose subject is at `:889`. The check is now stated rather than assumed: every primary location is `>= 767`, and `mod tests` begins at `:765`.

The second half is still true and the first half is not. It also **refines the CI condition**: the `rust` job that is still `continue-on-error` cannot become blocking until the test target compiles, and that is neither W6's compilation nor W7's channel — the workflow comment's two-clause condition is incomplete. The work is split out as **W6c**, because L28 named exactly this split and left it as a decision rather than a port.
