- **L35 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W8's independent verification, and the one advisory it was worth correcting.
  - **What was verified, and how.** `gentle-ai-verify` ran over `ec3dc8b` (high risk, item 5: delivery and installer configuration). It did **no rebuild and no install**: it re-derived `tauri.windows.conf.json`'s schema validity by resolving the downloaded Tauri 2 schema's `$ref`s, re-measured both artifacts, re-extracted the NSIS payload, read the MSI's `Property` table through `WindowsInstaller.Installer`, and re-read `lib.mjs` and `.cargo/config.toml`. All seven claims came back **PASS**, no blocker.
  - **Three independent confirmations worth keeping.** The MSI's `UpgradeCode` is not merely stable, it is the UUIDv5 of `Gentle Dot.exe.app.x64`, so it is deterministic — which is exactly why pinning it costs nothing today. The patched exe differs from the standalone one by **exactly three bytes** at offset 27,385,915 (`NSS` → `UNS`), which is the bundle-type marker and not a rebuild. The extracted icon hashes identically to `icons/icon.ico` (`cc84fbae…`), so what the exe carries is the file in the repository rather than Tauri's default.
  - **The advisory that mattered was not cosmetic.** `longDescription` said "The package carries its own Node.js runtime, engine, and Engram", copied from `tauri.linux.conf.json` where it is true. On Windows the payload is the app exe and its uninstaller, so that sentence ships a false claim in the installer's visible metadata. It was fixed test-first: `scripts/package/test/tauri-windows-config.test.ts` pins W8's decisions — the two targets, `currentUser`, the `.ico` here and never in the shared config, an explicit WebView2 mode, a pinned `upgradeCode`, no Windows entry in `.cargo/config.toml`, and that the description never promises a runtime the bundle does not carry. It ran **RED (3 failed / 4 passed)** before the fix and **GREEN (7 / 7)** after, with the whole `scripts/package/test` folder at 43 passed. The three failures were exactly the three gaps: the false promise, the unpinned `upgradeCode` and the inherited WebView2 default.
  - **What the fix changed.** `longDescription` drops the runtime sentence until W11 can make it true; `windows.wix.upgradeCode` is pinned to `{EFEB5D6D-FA61-5841-8B75-DA089BA35404}`, the value already produced, so the product identity cannot drift if `productName` ever changes; and `windows.webviewInstallMode` is stated as `{type: "downloadBootstrapper", silent: true}` rather than inherited, because "what happens when WebView2 is missing" is a real question about an installer and the answer belongs in the file that configures it. The regenerated bundles are confirmed with the corrected file. **The bundler accepts the keys the test can only type-check:** `pnpm build` finished with exit 0, `makensis` and WiX `candle`+`light` ran again, and the MSI reports the pinned `UpgradeCode = {EFEB5D6D-FA61-5841-8B75-DA089BA35404}` unchanged, which is the point of pinning it. One number moved and is recorded rather than smoothed over: the NSIS installer is now 8,361,278 bytes against 8,357,338 before, while the MSI is byte-identical in size at 11,890,688. The cause of a 3,940-byte *increase* from a *shorter* description was not investigated, because nothing depends on it: no test asserts an installer's size, since a bundler's output size is not a contract.
  - **One advisory deliberately not actioned.** `bundle.copyright` is unset and the exe's `LegalCopyright` is empty. `tauri.macos.conf.json` and `tauri.linux.conf.json` do not set it either, so leaving it alone keeps the three platforms consistent; filling it in is a product decision about notice text, not a Windows port defect.
  - **Correction (W11b, measured — see L55): the premise under this fix was never measured, and it is false.** This entry says the sentence "ships a false claim in the installer's visible metadata". The built bundles say otherwise: the MSI carries no `Comments` and no `ARPCOMMENTS` property, the generated `installer.nsi` derives every version-info key from `${PRODUCTNAME}` with no description define, and a byte search over both installers does not contain the sentence. `bundle.longDescription` is simply **not emitted by Tauri's Windows bundlers**, so removing it from the conf was harmless but the wrong repair for the stated cause. What survives is the **policy** the test pins (a bundle must not claim a runtime it does not carry) and the shorter description; what does not survive is the claim that the sentence was reaching a user.
  - **The verification's own limit, stated.** It could not install, so the NSIS `installMode` effect, the WebView2 bootstrap, and any shortcut or autostart behaviour at install time are configured and schema-valid but unobserved. Nothing in W8 installs anything, on this machine or in CI, and the payload was only ever extracted to a temporary folder.

- **L39 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** what the port still needs, and the three gaps the inventory found that no row owned.
  - **Where the port actually stands, counted two ways because one number would lie.** By the parity list the project itself wrote (the Dot, the panel, the tray, global shortcuts, persistent secrets, a self-contained installer, voice, computer control) plus the app-to-daemon channel that carries connectors and approvals: **3 done, 2 partial, 4 absent**, which is 45% with the partials at half weight and 55% with them whole. By the plan's units: **12 done, 12 pending, 1 dropped**. The two numbers disagree for a reason worth stating — the pending half contains the four heaviest blocks in the project, so "half the units" is well under half the remaining work. A single percentage here is an estimate, and the table is the honest artefact; the number is for orientation only.
  - **The architecture is already shaped for the port, which is the finding that matters for effort.** `computer/` is 4,967 lines but its macOS backend is `macos.rs` at 604 and the rest is platform-neutral logic (`mcp.rs` 1,839, `risk.rs`, `blocklist.rs`, `session.rs`, `coords.rs`, `encode.rs`); `voice/` is 4,112 with `macos.rs` at 323. So the work is writing a Windows backend behind a seam that already exists as a clean refusal (`computer/app.rs` has a `cfg(not(target_os = "macos"))` arm and `voice/engine.rs`'s `fallback_engine()` returns `EngineName::None`), not restructuring the app. That does **not** make the backends small: UI Automation, `SendInput` and Windows Graphics Capture are a large API surface, which is why W13 begins with an explore.
  - **`computer/windows.rs` is a false friend, and worth naming so nobody counts it.** It is 159 lines about `CGWindowListCopyWindowInfo` and `kCGCursorWindowLevel` — a macOS window list, one of the topics that happens to be called "windows". It is macOS-only code, not a Windows arm, and it is the only `windows.rs` in the crate.
  - **The three gaps that had no owner, now W19, W20 and W21.** `process_started`'s missing Windows arm and the provisioning lock were both recorded in L33 with "Decision pending" and never entered the table; the `!command` shell call was found by this inventory and had never been noticed. Each row names what is measured, what the decision is, and that it had no owner until it.
  - **What is blocked by others, so the critical path is visible.** W7 is the largest functional gap (no channel means no connector changes and no approvals) and depends on a contributor rebasing `Row0902/gentle-dot#2` and dropping the facade; W18 depends on `Row0902/gentle-dot#1`; and W11 carries a recorded constraint — the Windows installer cannot carry the engine while `gentle-pi` builds its Gentle AI binary from source on Windows, so a prebuilt Windows binary, a bundled Go, or a Go 1.25.10 requirement has to be chosen.
  - **The pairs that must land together, which the unit list hides.** W7 gives Windows a channel and W10 gives it a store, and connector secrets on Windows need **both**; either alone still fails closed. That is why W10 is the next unit and not a later one.
  - **Two limits this inventory states rather than leaving implicit.** The Windows CI job has still never executed on a runner, so nothing in this port has been observed by CI; and W17's advisory set cannot be fully worked from the record, because only identifiers, locations and severities were persisted and the finding bodies are gone (L17).

- **L40 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W10's research, done before a line of it is written, because the row names two APIs (`Credential Manager / DPAPI`) without saying which one — and one of them turns out to be **required for a secret the daemon already writes**, not an alternative to the other.
  - **The bindings exist in a crate already in this tree, and the signatures are read from its source rather than recalled.** `windows-sys` 0.61.2 is already resolved in `Cargo.lock` (transitively) and its source sits in this machine's cargo registry, so this is a measurement offline and exact:
    - `CredWriteW(credential: *const CREDENTIALW, flags: u32) -> BOOL` (advapi32)
    - `CredReadW(targetname: PCWSTR, r#type: CRED_TYPE, flags: u32, credential: *mut *mut CREDENTIALW) -> BOOL`
    - `CredDeleteW(targetname: PCWSTR, r#type: CRED_TYPE, flags: u32) -> BOOL`
    - `CredEnumerateW(filter: PCWSTR, flags: CRED_ENUMERATE_FLAGS, count: *mut u32, credential: *mut *mut *mut CREDENTIALW) -> BOOL`
    - `CredFree(buffer: *const c_void)` — every returned credential and its strings live in one block, so `CredFree` is the only deallocator. `CREDENTIALW` carries a `Default` impl; `FILETIME` lives in `Win32::Foundation`, as do `GetLastError` and `ERROR_NOT_FOUND`.
    - The module is gated by `Win32_Security_Credentials` (`= ["Win32_Security"]`, which is `["Win32"]`), the DPAPI pair by `Win32_Security_Cryptography` (`CryptProtectData` / `CryptUnprotectData`, crypt32). So the dependency is one target-scoped block with two features and **no version movement** in the lock.
  - **`list_ids` maps cleanly, and that was the largest unknown going in.** `CredEnumerateW`'s filter is documented as "a name prefix followed by an asterisk. For instance, the filter `FRED*` will return all credentials with a *TargetName* beginning with the string `FRED`", so `dev.gentleman.gentle-dot.connectors/*` is exactly the enumeration `list_ids` needs: no regex, and no walking the user's other credentials. `CRED_ENUMERATE_ALL_CREDENTIALS` (1) exists but requires a `NULL` filter and hands back target names in `namespace:attribute=value` form, so it is the wrong tool for a prefix query. The empty case has a named code — `ERROR_NOT_FOUND` (1168) when nothing matches — which is an empty `Vec`, not a `StoreError`.
  - **A hard limit the row never mentioned, and it lands on a secret that already exists.** `CredentialBlobSize` "cannot be larger than `CRED_MAX_CREDENTIAL_BLOB_SIZE` (5*512)" = **2560 bytes** (confirmed in the crate source: `CRED_MAX_CREDENTIAL_BLOB_SIZE: u32 = 2560`). By comparison `TargetName` is roomy — **32767** chars for `CRED_TYPE_GENERIC`, case-insensitive, and Microsoft asks for a company prefix, which `SERVICE` already is — while `Comment` and `TargetAlias` cap at 256.
  - **And the secret that will not fit is `connector/<id>/signin`.** Measured in this repository instead of reasoned about: `connectors.ts:1022` stores `JSON.stringify(state)` where `state` is pi-mcp's `McpOAuthState` (`oauth/provider.d.ts:3-12`) — `discovery` (an `OAuthDiscoveryState` whose `AuthorizationServerMetadata` carries an `[key: string]: unknown` index signature, so the document is stored nearly whole), `tokens` (with both `access_token` and `id_token`), `clientInformation`, `codeVerifier` and `oauthState`. The `discovery` half alone, measured today over HTTP with no account and no secret involved:

    | Authorization server metadata | Serialized size |
    | :--- | :--- |
    | `accounts.google.com/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` | **1654 B** |
    | `login.microsoftonline.com/common/v2.0/.well-known/openid-configuration` | **1728 B** |
    | `mcp.notion.com/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` | 1058 B |
    | `mcp.sentry.dev/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` | 739 B |

    Add an OIDC `id_token` (a JWT) plus an opaque or JWT `access_token`, and a sign-in crosses 2560 B on the two largest of those four with no exotic provider involved. **Consequence:** SW4's acceptance — "connector tokens live in the Windows Credential Manager … no token ever falls back to `MemoryStore`" — cannot be met as written for that secret kind, and neither the Linux nor the macOS backend has this limit. This is what resolves the row's ambiguity: **DPAPI is not an alternative to Credential Manager here, it is the required companion** for what does not fit, or the `signin` state has to be split across several credentials. That is the decision to take, and it is now a decision with numbers behind it.
  - **Two smaller choices now have documented answers rather than taste.** `Persist` should be `CRED_PERSIST_LOCAL_MACHINE` (2), documented as "visible to other logon sessions of this same user on this same computer and not visible to logon sessions for this user on other computers", over `CRED_PERSIST_ENTERPRISE` (3), which is "visible … to logon sessions for this user on other computers" and would roam connector tokens onto the user's other machines. The ecosystem's most-used Windows store defaults to **Enterprise**, so the safe value is the deliberate one. `CRED_PERSIST_SESSION` (1) is unusable: it dies at logoff, which is exactly the restart the acceptance criterion tests.
  - **The route stays direct Win32, and the ecosystem's own documentation agrees.** `keyring` states that applications "which want to control which credential stores they use on which platforms, or which want to offer more functionality than just reading and writing specific passwords/secrets" "should not be linking to this library at all". Its Windows store (`windows-native-keyring-store` 1.1.0) needs a **regex** to search and maps an entry's target name as `user.service`, which fights both this store's id scheme and its prefix-filter `list_ids`. This repository's two existing backends are direct bindings (`security-framework`, `dbus-secret-service`), so a target-scoped `windows-sys` block keeps the three consistent.
  - **A concurrency warning worth taking seriously, from that store's published test results:** "operating on the same entry from different threads does not reliably sequence the operations in the same order they are initiated", and "changing a credential's persistence type immediately before reading it may cause the read to fail, especially if the credential manager is busy on multiple threads". `SecretStore` is `Send + Sync` and the app answers channel requests, so W10 has to state whether it serializes its calls; a mutex over the four calls is cheap and removes the question.
  - **DPAPI's own status, checked so that a live deprecation is not discovered later.** `CryptProtectData`'s prompt flow through `pPromptStruct` "is deprecated and will be removed in **February 2027**" — and it does not affect this design, because the non-interactive path is passing `NULL`, which is what a background app store must do anyway. Its documented scope is "only the **same user account** on the **same machine**" by default; `CRYPTPROTECT_LOCAL_MACHINE` would widen it to "any user on the machine", which is wrong for tokens; and "if the user's password is reset by an administrator (rather than changed by the user), previously protected data may become unrecoverable unless a recovery agent or DPAPI backup key is in place" — a sentence the Windows limit paragraph should carry, because Credential Manager encrypts with those same keys.
  - **What is still not measured, stated so it is not mistaken for done.** (1) The size of a **real** `signin` JSON. The table above measures public metadata documents, not this user's stored connector state, and `~/.gentle-dot/connector-signin/` lies outside the project root, so reading it needs authorisation — a size-only read (`wc -c`, never contents) is the one measurement that closes the split-versus-DPAPI decision. (2) Where a generic credential appears in Windows 11's UI: secondary sources agree on Control Panel → Credential Manager → Windows Credentials and on `cmdkey /list` listing it, but that is not a primary source for 24H2/25H2, and the manual round-trip on this machine settles it. (3) That `CredReadW` returns a blob written by another same-user process with no prompt — the demonstration of the documented limit, which belongs in the store's own ignored test the way `linux.rs` demonstrates S25.7 with `secret-tool`.

- **L41 (2026-10-15, user + agent, decision):** W10's store is **DPAPI only — option C** — and the Windows Credential Manager is out. The user's verbatim: *"Sí, escribe tú y yo apruebo, me gusta la ruta recomendada y W14"*, answering *"Estoy entre B y C, cuál es más viable?"*.
  - **The three decisions taken:** the SW4 amendment is drafted by the agent for the user's approval; the file is **`%LOCALAPPDATA%\Gentle Dot\secrets.dat`**; and the user-facing limit paragraph is **W14's**, in the Windows section it already owns.
  - **What decided it was not complexity — it was that B buys no security.** Credential Manager encrypts with the same user-scoped DPAPI keys, so a same-user process reads it exactly as easily as it reads our file. Electron documents the ceiling for both in one table: on macOS content is protected "from other users **and other apps** running in the same userspace", while on Windows/DPAPI it is protected "from other users on the same machine, **but not from other apps** running in the same userspace". Two stores behind one identical credential would have been complexity bought for nothing.
  - **The failure class B adds, verified in code rather than imagined.** pi-mcp persists a sign-in in stages: `saveClientInformation`, `saveDiscoveryState` and `saveCodeVerifier` each call `update(...)`, which writes the whole state **before** the OAuth flow can have tokens; `saveTokens` writes it again afterwards. So one id is written several times and changes size across its life — measured: a Notion-shaped state is 2057 B pre-token (inside the 2560 cap) and grows past it when the tokens arrive, and Sentry's is 1525 B. Under two-store routing that id **migrates between stores in the normal path**, and the failure mode is a refresh token left readable in the Credential Manager after the item moved to the file. That is a security-relevant leftover of exactly the shape `macos.rs` already guards against with its "delete then add, and fail if it reappears".
  - **The remaining arguments for C, in the order they were weighed:** `list_ids` is the file's own keys instead of a second enumeration to merge and de-duplicate; the store splits into an injected-directory file layer that is testable on any OS plus one Windows-gated DPAPI call, so `check_contract` runs for real instead of inheriting the `#[ignore]` shape `linux.rs` needs for a live Secret Service; and DPAPI-only is the mainstream Windows member of the Keychain / Secret Service / DPAPI family that Electron itself ships, rather than a local invention.
  - **What C costs, recorded rather than left implicit.** (1) SW4's acceptance named the Credential Manager and had to change — and note that **B would not have honoured that sentence either**, because a Microsoft-backed sign-in cannot fit in a generic credential even when empty: B needed a qualifying clause, C needed the sentence rewritten. (2) Windows loses an OS-level surface where the user could audit and delete the items (`cmdkey /list`, the Credential Manager UI), so revocation stays the app's own — removing a connector deletes its secrets — and the limit paragraph has to say where the file is and that deleting it clears everything. (3) Atomic replacement and the concurrency guard are ours to own, which the Credential Manager research had already flagged as necessary either way.
  - **Consequences for the plan, so that nothing is inherited silently.** There is **no W10a/W10b split**; the unit shrinks to roughly `linux.rs`'s size and stays in the one-day band. The dependency needs only `Win32_Foundation` and `Win32_Security_Cryptography`. L40's CredMan findings are kept as the **reason the Credential Manager was rejected** and are explicitly **not** implementation guidance. And the manual check changes shape: no `cmdkey /list`, but a restart with the file present and the daemon reading the sign-in back.
  - **The ceiling is named, not hidden.** Neither option protects against malicious same-user code, and that is the state of the art rather than an oversight: Chrome moved past plain DPAPI for exactly this reason with App-Bound Encryption (Chrome 127, July 2024) — a privileged COM service running as SYSTEM that validates the calling executable's identity and path before releasing decryption primitives. That is a unit of its own if the ceiling ever has to rise; for W10 it is one honest sentence in W14's paragraph.

- **L42 (2026-10-15, agent, evidence):** W10's baseline before its first write, and the build incident that hid it. Baselines are measured here for a reason (L21, L25), and this one also produced the compiler-level symptom L34 never had.
  - **The baseline itself: `cargo test --lib` on Windows 11 at `86c7d9e`, 341 passed / 0 failed / 2 ignored**, tests in 1.12 s. It matches L38's figure exactly, which is what a baseline is for.
  - **The first attempt did not fail in this crate at all.** It died in `lzma-sys` v0.1.20's build script: `cl.exe` exit code 2, and the captured log carried **no compiler output whatsoever**. `lzma-sys` is not optional here — `zip` depends on `xz2`, which depends on it — so the test target cannot be built without it.
  - **A method error worth keeping, because it hid the failure.** The job was launched as `cargo test --lib 2>&1 | tail -25`, and the runner reported **exit code 0** for a build that had failed: the status belonged to `tail`. Every later command in this session prints `CARGO_EXIT=${PIPESTATUS[0]}`. An exit code read through a pipe is not the build's exit code.
  - **The diagnosis was reproduced by hand rather than guessed.** Running the exact `cl.exe` line the build script printed — same source directory, same `-I` list, with `-Fo` redirected into the project's own `target/` so cargo's artifacts were untouched — fails deterministically:

    ```
    xz-5.2/src/common\sysdefs.h(33): fatal error C1083: No se puede abrir el archivo incluir: 'stddef.h': No such file or directory
    ```

    The cause is the shell, not the repository: this one has `INCLUDE` and `LIB` **empty** and `cl.exe` not on `PATH`. cc-rs finds the compiler through its own probing, but the invocation it produced carried no Windows SDK include paths, so the very first system header fails.
  - **And then the retry passed, in the same shell, with the same empty `INCLUDE`.** So the honest conclusion is not "this environment cannot build C": cc-rs normally injects the SDK paths itself, and what failed once was that injection during a cold parallel build. That makes this **the same symptom L34 recorded for `ring`**, now reproduced and with its compiler-level signature named for the first time — an empty-stderr `cl.exe` exit 2 against a dependency's build script is a missing SDK include path, which is exactly why L34's captured output could not be attributed to anything.
  - **The practical rule this port should follow, and W14's `docs/windows-testing.md` should carry:** a cold build that fails inside a dependency's build script with a silent `cl.exe` error is run through `%TEMP%\w8cargo.bat`, which sets `INCLUDE`/`LIB` explicitly — **see the correction below, which supersedes this bullet's first draft** (it recommended a retry and then a Developer Command Prompt, and this shell cannot use the latter). Nothing is committed for this: the environment is a host fact, which is L34's stance and stays unchanged.
  - **Two limits, stated so the entry is not read as more than it is.** (1) This does not explain L34's `ring` failure with certainty — that one was never reproduced and its output was not kept — so it is recorded as the same **symptom**, not as a proven same cause. (2) A warm `target/` can mask the whole class: L38's successful run and this baseline both succeeded against dependencies already compiled, and only a cold build exercises the injection.
  - **Correction, same session, and it replaces the advice rather than the numbers — because this entry's mechanism was reasoning, not a measurement.** The claim that "cc-rs normally injects the SDK paths itself, and what failed once was that injection" was never verified. The memory check at the close of the session found that an **earlier session had already measured this class** (Engram `1302` and `1298`): this shell has no MSVC `INCLUDE`/`LIB`, `cargo` therefore fails inside C dependencies (`ring`, `zstd-sys`) with `cl.exe exit code 2` / `stddef.h`, **the branch compiles only because those dependencies were already cached**, and the recipe that works is a `.bat` setting `INCLUDE` and `LIB` explicitly — `%TEMP%\w8cargo.bat`, `w8build.bat` and `w8test.bat` are on this machine and were confirmed present — while **`vcvars64.bat` does not run well from git-bash**, which was exactly the fallback this entry recommended.
  - **The methodological flaw is the part worth keeping.** Running the build script's printed `cl.exe` line by hand **bypasses cc-rs entirely**, so it reproduced the *symptom* (`C1083 stddef.h`) without reproducing the *failure path*: a symptom obtained by removing the thing the real path supplies is not a diagnosis. It is the third time in this log that a claim written from a symptom had to be corrected (L13, L20), and the first where the correction came from the project's own memory instead of from a fresh measurement — which is the argument for searching it before writing a diagnosis.
  - **What survives, and what is now explicitly unclaimed.** The numbers stand: the first run failed, the retry passed, the baseline is 341 passed / 0 failed / 2 ignored, and an exit code read through a pipe is not the build's exit code. **Why the retry passed is not explained here and is not claimed** — it is recorded as observed. What changes is the rule: a cold build failing this way goes through `%TEMP%\w8cargo.bat`, not through a retry followed by a Developer Command Prompt this shell cannot use.
  - **Addendum, same session — the measurement I promised was impossible, so the number came from a computation instead.** No connector was ever signed in on this machine: `~/.gentle-dot/connector-signin/` does not exist, `connectors.json` does not exist, `agent/mcp.json` is 23 bytes and the only other state file is 198. So there was nothing to measure, and the decision cannot wait on a machine that has no data. What replaces it is reproducible arithmetic on **real public documents**, using the exact `McpOAuthState` shape the daemon serializes:

    | Provider | AS metadata (serialized) | discovery | state with **zero** tokens | bytes left for tokens |
    | :--- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
    | Google | 1392 | 1815 | 2396 | **164** |
    | Microsoft | 1728 | 2163 | **2744** | **−184, already over** |
    | Notion | 1058 | 1476 | 2057 | 503 |
    | Sentry | 739 | 944 | 1525 | 1035 |

    (The state column includes `clientInformation` with a 96-char `client_id` and a 64-char `client_secret`, a 64-char `codeVerifier`, a 32-char `oauthState`, the server URL, `tokensExpireAt` and the `tokens` object with empty strings. Each provider's AS metadata is paired with a real MCP server's protected-resource metadata (193 B) as a stand-in for the resource half; Sentry publishes none, so its row is optimistic.)
  - **Read the Microsoft row as the decision, not as a warning.** A Microsoft-backed connector **cannot store even an empty sign-in** in a Windows generic credential: 2744 > 2560 by 184 bytes, before a single token exists. Google leaves 164 bytes for `access_token` + `refresh_token` + `id_token` combined, and an OIDC `id_token` is a JWT that alone is several hundred bytes, so Google crosses it too. Notion and Sentry would fit until the tokens are large. In other words the failure is **provider-dependent and deterministic**, not rare, and it is silent unless the store refuses loudly.
  - **A correction to this entry's own earlier figures, kept rather than smoothed over.** The first table in L40 measured Google at 1654 B and Microsoft at 1728 B; those were **raw HTTP bodies**, pretty-printed. The serialized sizes of the parsed objects — which is what actually gets stored, because `parseAuthorizationServerMetadata` does `{ ...input, ...overrides }` and therefore keeps the whole document — are 1392 and 1728. The Microsoft body happened not to carry whitespace; Google's did.
  - **The strongest finding is about verification, and it is the project's recurring theme.** This repository's own sign-in fixture is `{ serverUrl: "x", tokens: { access_token: "notion-access", token_type: "Bearer", refresh_token: "notion-access-refresh" } }` (`connector-secrets.test.ts:366-369`) — **121 bytes, 21 times smaller than the cap**, with no `discovery`, no `id_token` and no `clientInformation`. So no test in this repository can see this defect on any platform, and the same fixture would pass on Windows while every real Microsoft sign-in failed. That is the W5d/`chmod` shape again: a fixture that cannot observe the platform's truth.
  - **What this does to the decision, stated as three options rather than a recommendation dressed as one.** (a) **Credential Manager only** is now impossible for real providers unless the *daemon* stops storing the sign-in as one secret, which crosses W10's boundary and touches code shared with macOS and Linux. (b) **Credential Manager plus a DPAPI-encrypted file** for what does not fit keeps the schema unchanged, and — the argument that decides it favours this — **the documented limit is the same sentence for both halves**, because Credential Manager encrypts with the same user-scoped DPAPI keys: any same-user process can read either. So the honest Windows limit paragraph stays one statement, not two. (c) **DPAPI file only**, which is the simplest of the three and removes Credential Manager entirely; it is readable in the row's own wording ("Credential Manager / DPAPI") but it contradicts **SW4's acceptance text**, which names the Credential Manager, so it is a spec change for the user and not an implementation choice. Whichever is taken, a **size guard with a named error is mandatory**: truncating or silently falling back to `MemoryStore` would reintroduce exactly the silent loss W10 exists to close.

- **L43 (2026-10-16, agent, evidence):** W10 verified on Windows 11 and closed — the suite over the change, the independent verifier, the two tests its probes added, and a native review the provider itself declined to require.
  - **The suite over the change, and the arithmetic that makes it a measurement rather than a number.** `cargo test --lib` = **355 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored, exit 0**, against the baseline 341/0/2 taken at `86c7d9e` (L42). 341 + 12 new unit tests = 353, and the 2 boundary tests added below take it to 355; the ignored count rises from 2 to 4 with the pair of real-`%LOCALAPPDATA%` round-trip tests. The focused selector `secure_store` is **20 passed / 2 ignored**: 4 shared `mod.rs` tests, 14 store tests and the 2 ignored round-trips; the second focus run before the boundary tests was 18/2, which is the same tree minus those two.
  - **A stale comment was corrected rather than left to mislead the next reader.** `secure_store/mod.rs`'s note on `check_contract` said the contract ran "against `MemoryStore` here and against a throwaway keychain service in the ignored macOS test". The Windows store now runs it for real, so the note names that arm too — the one place in this unit where the docs drifted from the code between the two sessions.
  - **The independent verifier is where this entry's strongest evidence comes from, and it is stronger than a re-run.** `gentle-ai-verify` (risk item 2, credentials) returned **PASS on all eight claims with no blocker**, and it did not merely re-run the tests: it inspected the store's own artifacts under `target/test-scratch/secure-store-windows/` and found `{"secrets":{"connector/x/value/token":"AQAAANCMnd8BFdERjHoAwE/Cl+sB…"}}` — base64 DPAPI ciphertext and the id as the map key, nothing more — with a repository-wide `grep -rl` for the test's plaintext value (`s3cr3t-value-9f2a`) returning **nothing**. It also confirmed rejected input leaves the file byte-identical by reading the corrupt and oversize artifacts rather than trusting the assertions, and verified the process-wide single-mutex claim at the consumer (`shell.rs:821`), which the writer's evidence had not.
  - **The one reproduction it could not make, stated rather than glossed.** The RED baseline — 28 compile errors, `CARGO_EXIT=101` before `windows.rs` existed — needs the file deleted to reproduce, which a read-only verifier cannot do; it is recorded as writer-reported. Same for the two-process real-profile round-trip, which writes outside the scratch directory and was run by hand in the previous session, not re-run here.
  - **The verifier's two probes were adopted, and they close a real gap in the writer's own fixtures.** The writer refused an oversized secret but never pinned that the ceiling tests `>` and not `>=` — `exactly_the_ceiling_is_accepted_and_one_over_is_refused` now stores exactly `MAX_SECRET_BYTES` and asserts one over is refused with the file byte-identical. And no fixture stated that a rejected command writes nothing at all — `every_rejected_command_leaves_the_file_byte_identical` now runs `InvalidId` through `put`/`get`/`delete` and an oversize `put` and asserts the stored `kept` value and the file bytes are unchanged. Both are SW4 rules, both pass, and neither would have been caught by the suite as it stood.
  - **The native review was not skipped — the provider declined to require it, and that is a different thing.** `gentle-ai review assess` over the committed range (`0fa47d7`, base `86c7d9e`) reported `risk: high` with the agent's item-2 escalation applied, and `reviewDue: false` with `reviewDueReason: under_budget`. The RDD plan it returned requires writer self-verification and a separate independent verifier — both done — and treats the native review as an additional outside view that never replaces them. `inspect` then offered only a workspace projection whose sole path was the uncommitted `odd/tasks/windows-port.md`, not the code under review, so starting it would have burned authority on the wrong candidate. The unit's plan listed the native review as a step; the provider's own answer superseded that, and it is recorded here rather than silently dropped.
  - **Commit `0fa47d7`** (`feat(desktop): keep connector secrets in a DPAPI file on Windows`) holds the code, the tests and the Cargo change, with `windows.rs` added explicitly as L42 warned it must be; this row close and this entry follow in the docs commit. W10 is closed; **W14 still owes the user-facing limit paragraph**, and W7 is still what makes any of this reachable on Windows.

- **L44 (2026-10-16, agent + delegated writer, evidence):** W14, the Windows documentation, and the honesty decisions inside it.
  - **What landed (`a7a190c`).** `docs/install.md` gains a Windows section (requirements; build from source; `%USERPROFILE%\.gentle-dot` and `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Gentle Dot\secrets.dat`; the DPAPI limit paragraph; a "what does not work yet" list with the owning unit; uninstall; and the two developer notes). `docs/windows-testing.md` is new and mirrors `docs/linux-testing.md`. `docs/design.md` gains a Windows platform part, and its §9 `### Windows` heading — which described the *app windows*, not the platform — is renamed `### App windows`. `README.md` and `apps/desktop/README.md` state Windows honestly.
  - **The port is incomplete, so the docs say so instead of being aspirational.** Every pending surface is named with its unit: the app↔daemon channel (W7, so connectors that need a secret or an approval do not run), the shortcut and autostart (W9, `Alt+Space` is reserved by Windows), voice (W12), computer control (W13, macOS-only today), the `Mica` panel blur (W8d, the panel is opaque), and the self-contained installer (W11, so there is no `pnpm package:windows`). The checklist marks those steps **not yet on Windows** and asks the tester for `N/A` rather than presenting them as working.
  - **What was measured is separated from what is expected, on purpose.** `docs/windows-testing.md` frames its list as expected results and tags only the checks observed on Windows 11 — provisioning `%USERPROFILE%\.gentle-dot`, the daemon spawn, `GET http://127.0.0.1:4317/` answering HTTP 200, the quit that exits 0 and takes the daemon with it, and `cargo test --lib` at 355 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored. Everything else is what a tester verifies, which is the difference between a checklist and a claim.
  - **Three honesty corrections the parent made to the delegated draft.** (1) install.md said the app "runs natively on Windows 10 and Windows 11"; only Windows 11 has been run, so it now says it **targets** both and names where the runs are. (2) windows-testing.md's step 2 carried one "(measured on Windows 11)" tag over three bullets, one of which — the rose appearing — was never observed; the tag now sits only on the measured bullets. (3) design.md's §9 intro named only Linux; it now names Windows too. The `README.md` badge does gain Windows, and the paragraph immediately below it says support is in progress, so the claim and its qualifier travel together.
  - **A pre-existing lint failure surfaced by the check, and it is not W14's to hide.** `pnpm lint` on this tree failed on `packages/daemon/test/config.test.ts`, one line `biome` wanted wrapped. Verified pre-existing by stashing the docs and re-running: it comes from `dec85d3` (W4a). Fixed as its own commit, `57b6cdc`, formatting only, and the file's 6 tests still pass. The one remaining lint error is the untracked `.atl/.skill-registry.cache.json`, which is gitignored and belongs to this host, not the repository.
  - **The delegated writer failed at its own check step, so its output was reviewed rather than trusted.** `gentle-ai-worker` (`mvbezypf-2-rqqa`) reported an error after writing all five files, so nothing it said about its verification was believed: the parent re-ran the link, command and lint checks and made the three corrections above. A writer that dies inside its verification step is a writer with no evidence, whatever it wrote.
  - **SW8's third clause is deliberately left open, because closing it is a different decision.** The `rust` job in `.github/workflows/windows.yml` still carries `continue-on-error: true`, its condition comment still names W6 and W7, and it has **never executed on a runner** — so "the CI job compiles the Rust shell and runs the suites" is a prediction from a Windows 11 host, not a measurement of `windows-latest`. Making it blocking is its own decision (L26, L38) and is not taken here.

- **L45 (2026-10-16, agent + delegated writer, evidence):** W9 — the Windows panel shortcut and autostart, and the premise the measurement corrected.
  - **What landed (`6d3d72c`).** `platform::Os::default_shortcut` is a pure decision with a three-variant test: `Alt+Space` for macOS and Linux, `Ctrl+Shift+Space` for Windows. `config::DEFAULT_SHORTCUT` became `config::default_shortcut()`, and `shortcut::normalize`/`shortcut::apply` now take the `Os`, so the refusal messages name each OS's keys — macOS keeps its glyph wording verbatim, Windows and Linux stop showing `⌘ ⌥ ⌃`. `shell.rs` threads `Os::CURRENT` and its fallback order (requested, then the platform default) is unchanged.
  - **Autostart needed no code, and saying so is the unit's other half.** `tauri-plugin-autostart` 2.7.0 uses `auto-launch` 0.6.0, whose Windows backend writes `HKCU\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run` (read in the crate source, not assumed). So W9's "autostart through the registry" was already satisfied by the plugin; it becomes a tester step in `docs/windows-testing.md` instead of a rewrite that would only have re-implemented it.
  - **The plan's premise was wrong, and only the measurement shows it.** Both the W9 row and the docs written in L44 said Windows "reserves" `Alt+Space`. Probed on this Windows 11 host with `RegisterHotKey(NULL, id, MOD_ALT, VK_SPACE)`: **it succeeds**. The OS does not refuse the key; `Alt+Space` is the window-menu shortcut, so a global hotkey on it would take that menu away from every app while Gentle Dot runs. The code comment and all three docs now say that, because the wrong reason was on its way into user-facing text.
  - **The chosen default is verified to bind rather than merely decided.** The same probe on this machine: `Ctrl+Shift+Space` registers (`True`), `Ctrl+Alt+Space` registers, `Win+Shift+Space` is refused, and a second registration of `Ctrl+Shift+Space` fails with Win32 error 1409 — which is the OS error behind the app's "That shortcut is taken by the system or another app." The default names a combination Windows accepts here; what remains unverified is the keypress through the running app, which needs a human at the GUI.
  - **Tests and checks.** `cargo test --lib` = **357 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored, exit 0** (355 before the two new tests; the focused `shortcut` selector is 21 passed, `platform` 13, `config` 13), reproduced by the parent. `pnpm lint` reports only the untracked `.atl/.skill-registry.cache.json`.
  - **What is deliberately left manual.** Nothing in `cargo test --lib` exercises `tauri-plugin-global-shortcut`'s real registration or the tray toggle, so the end-to-end check is `docs/windows-testing.md` step 7 (the keypress and a change in Settings) and step 17 (the `Run` key round trip); that is where a tester closes it.

- **L46 (2026-10-16, user + agent, evidence):** W9's manual check on this Windows 11 machine, and the documentation error it found.
  - **The shortcut passed, all of it.** Pressing `Ctrl+Shift+Space` toggles the panel; `Alt+Space` opens the Windows window menu and not the panel; in Settings, changing the shortcut to `Ctrl+Alt+Space` produced a working toggle and "Reset to default" returned `Ctrl+Shift+Space`, which also toggled; Quit worked. The app's own log carried no `shortcut ... is unavailable` line at any point, which is the binding succeeding at start-up.
  - **One run looked like a W9 failure and was not.** The first launch after the engine reinstalled itself showed the panel's "the assistant did not answer on port 4317" message while provisioning ran; `GET /health` was `{"ok":true,"agentState":"idle"}` once it finished and the second run loaded completely. That is the W19/W20 chain (no pid record on Windows, and the provisioning lock an abrupt stop leaves behind, both already recorded in L33), not the shortcut.
  - **The autostart entry is not where the docs said, and measurement is again what corrected it.** The tray's "Launch at login" registered **`Gentle Dot`** under **`HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run`**, not `HKCU`; `regedit` also needed an `F5` to show it, which is why a first look found nothing while a task manager did. The cause is `auto-launch` 0.6.0's default `WindowsEnableMode::Dynamic`: try machine-wide, fall back to the current user only on access denied. This session's shell is elevated (`net session` succeeds), so machine-wide succeeded. `tauri-plugin-autostart` exposes no way to pin the mode, and `auto-launch`'s `is_enabled`/`disable` read and clear **both** hives, so behaviour is at least symmetric. `docs/install.md`, `docs/windows-testing.md` and `docs/design.md` now name both hives, the real value name, and the refresh.
  - **What this leaves open, stated rather than assumed.** Two lines of step 17: the off path (the `HKLM` value was still present when the registry was checked, so the removal was never observed) and the real login (nobody has signed out and back in with the entry enabled). Both are one click plus a restart for a tester; the on path and `Alt+Space` are measured.
  - **A leftover worth naming, since it is the user's machine.** Because the enabled entry names the repository's **debug** binary (`apps/desktop/src-tauri/target/debug/gentle-dot.exe`), leaving it on would start that build at every sign-in. Turning it off through the tray is the same click the checklist asks for, and it is why the off half is worth closing rather than skipping.
  - **Closed later the same session: the off path passed.** With the entry enabled in `HKLM`, turning **Launch at login** off and quitting removed the value, and a registry query against both hives finds no `Gentle Dot` — so step 17's on-and-off round trip is measured in both directions and the leftover that would have started the debug build is gone. What step 17 still has open is only the real sign-in (nobody has signed out and back in with the entry enabled); the previous bullet's two open lines are now one.

- **L47 (2026-10-16, user + agent, evidence):** The manual run found a production-breaking Windows bug that no test could see, and it is opened as W22.
  - **The symptom, as the user saw it.** After quitting from the tray and reopening the app themselves, it sat on "restarting" and then "starting"; pressing `Ctrl+Shift+Space` to bring it forward produced `cannot read token: … (os error 2)`, while the app's stdout carried the known `cannot record the assistant's pid` (W19). The daemon was answering 200 on 4317 the whole time.
  - **What was ruled out first, because the obvious reading is wrong.** The token existed (`%USERPROFILE%\.gentle-dot\token`, 44 bytes), `config.json` held only `{"shortcut": "Ctrl+Shift+Space"}`, and `GENTLE_DOT_DATA_DIR` was unset. So the app was not missing a file; it was looking in the wrong place.
  - **The cause, and it is one line.** `config.rs:72` resolves the home from `std::env::var_os("HOME")` and falls back to an empty `PathBuf`, so `data_dir` becomes the **relative** `.gentle-dot`. `HOME` is not defined by Windows — it is absent from `HKCU\Environment` and from the machine environment, and only MSYS/git-bash (this agent's shell) sets it — while `USERPROFILE` is always present. Every launch made from the agent's shell worked; the user's own launch from Explorer/the tray did not, which is exactly why the bug survived nine units of testing.
  - **The artifact that makes it a measurement rather than a theory.** `apps/desktop/src-tauri/target/debug/.gentle-dot/daemon.log` exists, written at 18:10 — the timestamp of the user's launch — because the app redirected the daemon's output into a data dir resolved relative to its own working directory. A stray `daemon.log` beside the binary is the bug's fingerprint.
  - **Why no test caught it, stated plainly.** `config::tests::data_dir_defaults_to_home_dot_folder` injects the home path, so it proves the join and never the resolution, and every Windows measurement so far ran from this MSYS shell, where `HOME` happens to exist. It is the W5d/`chmod` shape and L40's 121-byte fixture again: a test that cannot observe the platform's truth.
  - **What it costs, and why W22 is not cosmetic.** Without it the app is unusable from Explorer, the Start menu, or autostart — the only paths a real user has — and **W9's autostart is neutralised**, because the login launch has no `HOME` either. The daemon is unaffected, since Node resolves `USERPROFILE`, which is why the failure reads as "the assistant is not running" while it is running.
  - **Recorded rather than fixed here.** The shape is written into W22 — a pure home decision beside `platform.rs`, used by `config::load_config`, that fails with a named error instead of returning a relative path so a missing variable can never silently relocate the store, with tests over injected values on every OS. No fix was attempted in this session: selecting the unit is the user's call, and the requested outcome (testing W9's shortcut) was completed and passed.

- **L48 (2026-10-16, agent, evidence):** W22 fixed, with the end-to-end check a unit test could not make.
  - **What changed (`e71d7b5`).** `platform::home_dir(os, env)` reads the variables each OS actually sets — `USERPROFILE`, then `HOMEDRIVE` + `HOMEPATH`, on Windows; `HOME` elsewhere — and rejects blank values. `config::resolve_data_dir` uses it and refuses to invent a path: an unresolvable home is the named `NO_HOME` error, while an explicit `GENTLE_DOT_DATA_DIR` still wins and needs no home unless its value names one with `~`. `load_config` returns `Result`, and `shell::run` reports the error and exits before a window exists. `platform.rs`'s module doc and the design's Windows part record the decision.
  - **The verification that matters is the launch, not the assertions.** With `HOME` cleared (`HOME=[]` echoed by the launcher) and the working directory set to the binary's own folder — the way Explorer and autostart launch it — the stray `target/debug/.gentle-dot` is **no longer created**, and `~/.gentle-dot/daemon.log` is written again (**14904 → 15232 bytes**, mtime 18:31) with the daemon answering 200. The log redirect is the app's own data dir, so it is the app reporting that it found the right folder; a test could not have shown this, because `cargo test` runs with the developer's environment, which is exactly how the bug survived until now.
  - **RED is the recorded defect rather than a red test run.** The new tests could not be written before the fix (they call functions that did not exist), so no unit-level RED was observed; the honest substitute is L47's production reproduction — the token error, the stuck panel, and the stray log — measured on this machine before the change. The two new tests are the regression pins: `platform::tests::the_home_comes_from_the_variables_this_os_sets` (including that Windows never reads `HOME`) and `config::tests::the_data_dir_never_falls_back_to_a_relative_path`.
  - **One failure on the first run, kept because it is the loop working.** The first `cargo test --lib` was **358 passed / 1 failed**: `home_dir` accepted `USERPROFILE="   "` and returned a home of three spaces, because the shared `set` helper filters only empty strings. `home_dir` now has its own blank filter and the suite is **359 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored**.
  - **What is still unverified, stated plainly.** The panel itself on a no-`HOME` launch: the evidence above is the log redirect and the absent stray directory, not a screenshot of a connected UI. The user is running that launch now, and their answer is what closes it.
  - **Closed by the user's own launch, and the logs say so too.** They quit the app and started the debug binary themselves — the Explorer path, so no `HOME` — and the panel loaded and reached `ready` instead of stopping at `starting`. The daemon log corroborates it: the runs at 22:30 and 22:39 wrote their banner to the correct `~/.gentle-dot/daemon.log` (mtime 18:39, 15560 bytes), the stray relative directory stayed absent, and **no `get_history failed` line follows them** — the last one is 21:58, from the run that had the bug. That line comes from the UI asking for history, so its absence is the panel connecting. W22 has nothing left open.

- **L49 (2026-10-16, user + agent, decision):** The Windows autostart stays the plugin's dynamic default, and the documentation is the deliverable.
  - **The decision.** The user asked for the autostart to be **documented** rather than changed. `tauri-plugin-autostart` keeps its default and the docs name what it does; no code was written for it.
  - **Why not pin it to per-user, which is what would fit a per-user app.** `auto-launch` 0.6.0 defaults to `WindowsEnableMode::Dynamic` — machine-wide first, then the current user only on access denied — and `tauri-plugin-autostart` 2.7.0's builder exposes `app_name`, `args` and `macos_launcher` and **no Windows mode setter**. Pinning `CurrentUser` would mean replacing the plugin's default with our own registry write: a unit with its own review surface that would also have to re-implement `auto-launch`'s `StartupApproved` handling, for a difference that only appears when the app runs elevated.
  - **What the docs say now, and it is the measured truth rather than the assumed one.** `docs/install.md` and `docs/windows-testing.md` name the value (`Gentle Dot`), both hives, and the condition — `HKLM` when the app has administrator rights, `HKCU` otherwise, measured on this host, where the shell is elevated — plus that `regedit` needs an F5 to show it; `docs/design.md` records the mode and that the plugin does not let us pin it. The toggle is symmetric either way: `is_enabled` reads both hives and `disable` clears both (L46).
  - **The residual, named rather than hidden.** An elevated run registers a **machine-wide** entry, so the app starts for every user of that machine; that is the plugin's documented behaviour and is exactly why the tester checklist asks for the hive in the report.

- **L50 (2026-10-16, user + agent, decision):** W7 is taken forward by us, as a **named pipe** authenticated by the **PID against the retained handle**, and the unit splits into W7a, W7b and W7c.
  - **The question and the answer.** The user asked whether to finish W7 ourselves because the contributor had not answered. The answer is yes: the draft (`Row0902/gentle-dot#2`) has not moved since 2026-10-14 (`2938f97`), it is a draft, and it now sits **72 commits behind** this branch, so asking for a rebase is asking for the work to be redone. The transport choice is a named pipe, with the retained handle as the credential rather than the pipe's name.
  - **Why this unit and not another.** W7 is the keystone: the secret store (W10), the shortcut and autostart (W9) and the data folder (W22) all landed, so the channel is what moves Windows from "runs degraded" to usable, and it blocks W8d. It is also the unit where a mistake is least detectable, which is why it keeps its high-risk routing.
  - **What we take from the draft, credited.** The pipe transport: the endpoint is non-inheritable **from creation**, so there is no window in which a non-cooperating launcher can inherit it; per-direction `OVERLAPPED` events with a dedicated reader thread and a dedicated writer thread, because one `FILE_FLAG_OVERLAPPED` handle shares implicit completion signaling across clones and aborts the process under concurrent read and write; and cancellation through `CancelIoEx` that never blocks on settlement, retaining the operation, buffer and event until the worker settles. Its own body and the review thread are the reference.
  - **What we do not take, and the reason is a regression rather than taste.** `daemon_windows.rs` is a stub whose `ensure_running`, `wait_ready` and `restart` return `UNSUPPORTED`, and the draft's `lib.rs` `#[path]`-swaps it for `daemon.rs` on Windows. Today Windows runs **degraded but running** (option A, measured in L30/L33); that facade would take it to an app that can do nothing. The channel rides on W6's lifecycle instead. The merge itself is close to working: it needs one symbol, `NO_CHANNEL_WINDOWS` in `app_channel_windows.rs`, which W6b added, and with it `cargo build` was 0 errors and `cargo test --lib` **404 passed / 0 failed / 3 ignored** when it was verified.
  - **The security model, stated before it is implemented.** The pipe's **name is not a secret**: a separate process sees it in `\\.\pipe\` (measured, 1 match over 371 pipes), and the DACL has a single ACE for the user's logon SID, which does not separate same-user processes. What authenticates is the **retained handle** and the PID check against the child the app spawned, with a liveness check so a recycled PID cannot pass. W7b therefore owes an **adversarial test**: a same-user process that enumerates and connects must not be able to drive the channel. The residual is bootstrap **denial of service, not bypass**, and it is documented rather than hidden.
  - **Why three slices.** W7a is the frame-length fix, and it is first because it is **not Windows-specific**: the same asymmetry applied to the fd-3 path on macOS and Linux, it is a real bug, and it is verifiable today on every platform (`7c37e90`, L51). W7b is the transport and the identity binding, which is the risky half. W7c is the trust model in prose, because **S25.1's "nothing listens" becomes false** the moment a named pipe exists, and that sentence is public.
  - **The notice, and attribution.** Published on the draft: `https://github.com/Row0902/gentle-dot/pull/2#issuecomment-6226695552`. It says we are taking it forward, names what is credited, and names what will not be taken and why, so the contribution is acknowledged rather than absorbed. If the contributor returns, W7b's identity-binding tests and W7c's prose are the open pieces.
  - **What is deliberately not decided yet.** Which primitives are kept verbatim versus rewritten, and whether the daemon's own end needs a Windows branch or the existing inherited-socket path is reused, are W7b's design step rather than decisions taken here.

- **L51 (2026-10-16, agent, evidence):** W7a, the frame cap measured in bytes, and the false RED that came before the real one.
  - **What was wrong, in two parts.** `AppChannel.receive` accumulated a string (`this.buffer += chunk`) and compared `this.buffer.length` against `MAX_FRAME`, which counts UTF-16 code units, while the app's own end refuses to send with `Buffer.byteLength`. So a frame of multi-byte characters could pass the daemon's check while the writer would never produce it — and worse, a **complete** over-cap line was never checked at all, because the guard only ran over the leftover partial buffer. The contributor's draft flagged the encoding half; the review of it is what named the per-line half.
  - **The first RED was the test's fault, and that is worth keeping.** The first version of `app-channel-frames.test.ts` asserted synchronously after `stream.write`, and all four cases failed: a `PassThrough` emits `data` on a later tick. Asserting on an empty array is not a red test, it is a broken one. Rewritten with the repository's `waitFor`, the RED is honest: **3 failed / 1 passed**, where the passing one is the preservation case (a multi-byte frame well under the cap is delivered intact).
  - **GREEN, and the boundary is pinned rather than approximated.** The new tests build an ASCII payload so the frame lands on `MAX_FRAME` **exactly** and assert that it is delivered and that one more byte closes the channel, which fixes `>` rather than `>=`; multi-byte cases then pin the accounting. `packages/daemon/test/app-channel-frames.test.ts` is **4 passed**, `MAX_FRAME` is exported so the test names the cap instead of copying it, and `pnpm typecheck` exits 0.
  - **The baseline was separated by measurement, not by assertion.** Two `app-channel-fd.test.ts` cases fail on this host with 20 s timeouts in the afunix `pair.sock` setup. Re-running them with `app-channel.ts` stashed reproduces **the same two failures**, so they are baseline and not this change; the plan already tracks that family (W5b, W5d, W7). The existing `app-channel.test.ts` passes unchanged.
  - **Why this slice is first.** It is the one part of W7 that is **not** Windows-specific: the fd-3 path on macOS and Linux reads through the same `receive`. Fixing it on its own commit keeps the Windows transport's review from carrying an unrelated correctness fix, and it is verifiable today on all three platforms.

- **L52 (2026-10-16, user + agent, decision):** W11's engine cannot come from upstream on Windows, so the packaging pipeline builds `gentle-ai.exe` itself and the installer carries it.
  - **The fact that decides it, read from the installer rather than assumed.** `gentle-pi`'s installer has **no signed Windows release asset**: `gentle-ai` stopped distributing unsigned Windows builds (`c4b764d0`), so v4.0.0 publishes signed Darwin/Linux archives only, and Windows takes the "separately verified exact-tag Go SumDB source-build path" (`gentle-ai-installer.mjs:112-121`). So the row's first option, "a prebuilt Windows binary ships", cannot mean downloading upstream's: it means building it ourselves, which is a different commitment than the row implied.
  - **The decision.** The packaging pipeline compiles `gentle-ai.exe` with Go at package time and stages it into the runtime the installer carries, so an install stays self-contained and the first run does not need Go, npm, or the network for the engine. This is what makes SW5's "carries its own Node runtime, engine, Engram, and UI" true on Windows rather than aspirational.
  - **What it costs, named rather than implied.** The binary is **ours**: not signed by upstream, hash-pinned the way the staged Node and Engram are, and the docs say so instead of implying provenance we do not have. The packaging machine needs Go >= 1.25.10, which this repository already needs on Windows for `pnpm install`. And W11 has to tell `gentle-pi`'s postinstall **not** to rebuild it (`GENTLE_PI_SKIP_GENTLE_AI_INSTALL=1` plus the documented local-binary registration), or the user's first run would undo the staging by building from source anyway.
  - **The two rejected options, kept because the row named them.** Carrying the Go toolchain inside the installer is self-contained but heavy and moves a build onto the user's machine, where it can fail for reasons the installer cannot see. Requiring Go on the user's machine is the cheapest and breaks SW5's promise, which would then have to be rewritten. The chosen option is the only one that keeps the promise and the size sane.

- **L53 (2026-10-16, agent + delegated writer, evidence):** W8d, `Mica` behind a gate, and the one resolution that keeps two consumers from disagreeing.
  - **The rule, in one sentence.** Ask for `Mica` on the Windows panel only when the Windows 11 build (>= 22000), the user's transparency setting, a local session, and DWM composition all hold; otherwise ask for nothing and let the UI paint an opaque panel.
  - **The substance is the single resolution, not the material.** L8's defect had a specific shape: `panel_url(os)` decided whether the UI paints its own background while `with_panel_effects(panel, os)` decided what the window asks the system for, and the two could disagree, which is how a translucent panel with no backdrop happened. The change resolves the effect **once** in `build_windows` and threads the resolved `WindowEffect` into both, so agreement is structural rather than asserted. `panel_url` takes an effect now, and `has_panel_effect`/`panel_effect` take the injected facts.
  - **The reader fails closed, with one honest exception.** `WindowsBackdrop::current()` reads `RtlGetVersion` (not `GetVersionExW`, which lies under a manifest), the `EnableTransparency` value under the user's Personalize key, `GetSystemMetrics(SM_REMOTESESSION)` and `DwmIsCompositionEnabled`, and returns `unknown()` (which resolves to `None`) if any of those reads fails, so it never panics and never guesses. The exception is `SM_REMOTESESSION`: `GetSystemMetrics` has no failure channel, so a failed call reads as "not remote", which is fail-open for that single fact. It is named here and in the row rather than smoothed over.
  - **Tests, and the boundary rather than a sample.** `mica_is_asked_for_only_when_every_window_fact_holds` pins `MICA_MIN_BUILD - 1` against `MICA_MIN_BUILD`, each fact on its own, and the fail-closed default; `a_windows_backdrop_that_is_not_mica_leaves_the_panel_opaque` replaces L8's blanket invariant with the facts-based one; `every_os_picks_its_own_panel_backdrop` and `has_panel_effect_agrees_with_the_effect_it_names` were updated rather than deleted, so macOS `HudWindow` and Linux `None` stay pinned and the URL keeps agreeing with the requested material for every OS and every fact combination.
  - **What cannot be verified here, said in both the code and the docs.** Whether `Mica` **visually lands**. Tauri applies at most one Windows material and discards the result with no readback, so the gate makes it *expected* and cannot prove it; a silent failure remains possible even with all four facts true. `docs/design.md`, `docs/install.md` and `docs/windows-testing.md` now say "gated and unverified" and hand the visual confirmation to the tester, rather than claiming a blur.
  - **A deviation from L36, recorded rather than quietly taken.** L36 deferred W8d behind W7. The user chose to take it now, which is defensible: the gate is independent of the channel, and the alternative was a panel decision waiting on the largest remaining unit. The gated shape L36 specified was kept exactly, so nothing about the decision changed, only its order.
  - **Verification, two-sided because the change is not Windows-only.** `cargo test --lib` on Windows 11 = **360 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored, exit 0**, reproduced by the parent (the `platform` selector is 15), and `cargo build` exits 0. The signature changes on `panel_effect`, `has_panel_effect` and `panel_url` compile on macOS and Linux too, so the POSIX side is checked with `cargo check --lib --tests --locked` under the Fedora toolchain in WSL, and that check **finished with 0 errors in 7m 21s** — so the signature changes compile on Linux (and by the same code path, macOS) and not only on the host that ran the tests. The Windows compile alone would not have shown that.

- **L54 (2026-10-16, agent + delegated writer, evidence):** W11a, the Windows runtime staged for real, and two findings that shape W11b.
  - **What landed.** `TARGETS` gains `win-x64` (canonical) and `win-arm64`; `windows-x64` is accepted as an **alias** so SW5's literal spelling works; the archive names return `.zip` for `win32`, because that is what both releases ship there (`engram_3.4.0_windows_amd64.zip` measured, and Node's `node-v<v>-win-x64.zip`). `stage-runtime.mjs` extracts Windows zips with PowerShell's `Expand-Archive` and **not** `tar`, because Git for Windows puts a GNU tar ahead of the bundled bsdtar and a GNU tar cannot read zip; it finds `node.exe` wherever the archive nests it, lays out `node/node.exe` + `npm.cmd` + `npx.cmd` + `node_modules/npm` and `bin/engram.exe`, and stops treating a missing execute bit as "not a binary" on a platform where `chmod` is a no-op. `runtime-build.ts` writes `bin/pi.cmd` (CRLF, `%~dp0..`, injectable platform) on win32 and keeps the `#!/bin/sh` shim elsewhere. `daemon.rs`'s `launch_spec` gains the Windows arm.
  - **RED before GREEN, on both sides.** The two TypeScript test files were **4 failed / 13 passed** with the old code (the `windows-x64` target was asserted to throw, `bin/pi.cmd` did not exist, and the archive name had no extension source) and **17 passed** after. The Rust arm was verified the other way round, by temporarily disabling it: **358 passed / 2 failed** (both bundled-runtime `launch_spec` tests), then **360 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored** with it restored, reproduced by the parent.
  - **The staging run is the measurement that counts.** `stage-runtime --target win-x64` finished **exit 0 at 318.0 MB** (333,401,795 bytes), and the artifacts were exercised rather than listed: `node/node.exe` reports `v24.14.1`, `bin/pi.cmd` runs the engine and prints its version, `bin/engram.exe` reports `engram 3.4.0`, npm is `11.11.0`, and `MANIFEST.json` names target `win-x64`, node `24.14.1`, engram `3.4.0`, gentlePi `4.0.0`, gentleAi `4.0.0`. A unit test cannot make that claim; running the staged binaries can.
  - **Finding 1, and it changes W11b.** The staged runtime **already carries a built `gentle-ai.exe`**: gentle-pi's postinstall does the Go SumDB source build *at staging time*, which is why the manifest records `gentleAi 4.0.0` and why the second attempt failed with `Gentle AI Go SumDB source installation failed`. So L52's "the pipeline builds it" is, in practice, already what staging does, and W11b may need **no** separate pre-placement step. What W11b must therefore do is *verify* the engine runs from a staged runtime on a machine with **no Go**, and only add `GENTLE_PI_SKIP_GENTLE_AI_INSTALL=1` plus the local-binary registration if that fails. The reverse would be machinery for a problem that does not exist.
  - **Finding 2, recorded because it will bite someone.** The packaging path has a **live network dependency** at npm-install time, and two of the three attempts failed on it (`ECONNRESET`, then the Go SumDB), with the third succeeding. A packaging run can therefore fail for reasons that have nothing to do with the code, and the honest statement is "retry", not "it passed on the first try".
  - **Two named deviations rather than silent ones.** The bundled Windows `PATH` is `{root}/bin;{root}/node` and does **not** mirror the POSIX arm's `/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin`, because Windows resolves `System32` through its system-directory search rule before consulting `PATH`, so those entries would be redundant; and the canonical target key is `win-x64` (matching the `darwin-arm64`/`linux-x64` convention and Node's own spelling) with `windows-x64` kept as an alias for SW5's literal. Both are decisions W11b and W11c must follow rather than rediscover.
  - **What is not verified here, stated plainly.** POSIX staging could not be executed on this host at all: `assertHostTarget` refuses a cross-target run, so the requirement that the Linux and macOS branches stay byte-for-byte identical rests on inspection and on the preserved tests, not on a run. That is the same limit the plan already accepts for cross-platform work, and it is why the tests that pin the POSIX strings were kept rather than rewritten.
  - **Reproduced by the parent, with the artifacts exercised rather than listed.** A second `stage-runtime --target win-x64` finished **exit 0 at 317.9 MB (333,388,995 bytes)**, independently reproducing the writer's run, and this time on the first attempt, which softens Finding 2 to "the network can fail a packaging run" rather than "it usually does". What landed was exercised directly: `node/node.exe --version` prints `v24.14.1`, `bin/engram.exe version` prints `engram 3.4.0`, `bin/pi.cmd --version` run through `cmd.exe` prints `1.0.4`, and `ui/`, `bin/` and `daemon/` are all present. `MANIFEST.json` records target `win-x64`, node `24.14.1`, engram `3.4.0`, gentlePi `4.0.0`, engine `1.0.4`, gentleAi `4.0.0`.
  - **An advisory the writer did not surface, found by reading the log closely.** Staging prints `gentle-ai 4.0.0 from undefined (sha256 undefined)`: `openGentleAi` reads an `asset`/`assetSha256` pair, while the Windows `integrity.json` uses different keys (`method`, `binarySha256`, `moduleChecksum`, `goVersion`, `module`, `tag`). It is **display-only** (staging exits 0 and the manifest records the version correctly) and it is pre-existing rather than introduced here, but it is Windows-only in effect because the source-build path is the only one Windows has. The good news inside it: the built `gentle-ai.exe` **is** hash-pinned on Windows after all — `binarySha256 2d4ec00e…`, `goVersion go1.27.0`, module `github.com/gentleman-programming/gentle-ai/v4`, tag `v4.0.0`, `buildMode exe` — so L52's "hash-pinned like the staged Node and Engram" holds; what is missing is printing it. - **L59 (2026-10-17, user + agent, decision):** W7b's channel wiring exposes W19, and the user chose to **fold W19 into W7b** so Windows reaches the same daemon lifecycle as Unix instead of shipping a channel that bricks the app after an abrupt exit.
  - **The dependency is measured in the code, not assumed.** Making `with_channel` declare a handler on Windows sets `wants_channel = true` (`daemon.rs:662`), which the plan consults: `start_plan` returns `StartPlan::Refuse` when the port is held by a healthy daemon this app did not launch and no orphan is attributable (`daemon.rs:663-671`). But `process_started` is the `cfg(not(any(target_os = "macos", target_os = "linux")))` placeholder returning `None` (`daemon.rs:88-90`), so `record_launch` never writes the pid file and `live_orphan` is always `None` (`daemon.rs:196-198`). Concretely: a normal quit stops the child (`shell.rs:954`), but a crash or `taskkill` leaves a daemon holding 4317, and the next launch could neither attribute it nor replace it — it would refuse and stay dead. Today that cannot happen only because `wants_channel` is false on Windows.
  - **The decisions taken by user.** Fold W19 into W7b: port `process_started` (`GetProcessTimes`) and `stop_orphan` (`OpenProcess` + `TerminateProcess`) to Win32 inside this unit. W19's `explore → decide` route is retired to the folded row.
  - **The design taken by the agent from L50, resolved down to the interface.** The channel is the **named pipe** (not stdio), because it keeps one `AppChannel` shape on both platforms and the daemon's stdout/stderr keep their meaning; the app creates the server with `FILE_FLAG_FIRST_PIPE_INSTANCE` **before** it spawns the child and asks for a **single** instance (`nMaxInstances = 1`), which is what a same-user process cannot add to: measured, a second `CreateNamedPipeW` on that name fails with `ERROR_PIPE_BUSY` while the same call with 2 succeeds (L61). The flag alone would only fail *our own* create when the name already exists. It hands the name to the child through a new environment variable. The credential is the accepted connection's `GetNamedPipeClientProcessId()` compared against `child.id()` plus a `try_wait()` liveness check, so a recycled pid cannot pass. The daemon's end gets a Windows branch that opens the same pipe with `net.connect`; the name is not a secret and S25.1's "nothing is written to an environment variable, and nothing listens" becomes W7c's prose to correct.
  - **The module shape: a Windows arm inside `app_channel.rs`, never the contributor's `#[path]` swap.** L50 already rejected swapping `daemon.rs`; duplicating the whole file (the draft's 1621 lines) would fork the frame, pending and secret-serving logic that must stay one rule. The shared core keeps one home and the transport is the only platform-specific part.
  - **What is taken from the draft, credited.** The pipe primitives: non-inheritable from creation, per-direction `OVERLAPPED` completion with a dedicated reader/writer pair, and `CancelIoEx` cancellation that does not block the closing thread (the draft's own measurement: one `FILE_FLAG_OVERLAPPED` handle used for concurrent read and write aborts the process).


- **L60 (2026-10-17, agent + delegated writer, evidence):** W7b is implemented and measured on both platforms, but its **two independent checks did not run**, so the row is not closed.
  - **What landed.** `b201838` (W19's Win32 `process_started`/`stop_orphan`, 385 → 389 tests) and `a7dec73` (the W19/W7b decision) were already committed; this unit added `a36df01` (the Rust channel: one `AppChannel` over either transport, the Windows pipe arm, the `daemon.rs`/`shell.rs` wiring) and `0c9826d` (the daemon's end). `NO_CHANNEL_WINDOWS` is deleted, so a connector change takes the same path on every platform.
  - **The adversarial test is the one the plan owed, and it measures what it claims.** The test process (a same-user process) enumerates `\\.\pipe\`, finds the endpoint name, connects, writes a frame, and reads **nothing**; the accept loop refuses it by PID, re-arms, accepts the real child, and the handler sees the child's approval as the **only** one. That is the residual L50 named (bootstrap DoS, not bypass) reproduced rather than asserted.
  - **Two defects the parent found in the writer's own report, both fixed before the commit.** (1) The helper the writer tested (`openNamedPipeChannel`) was **not the code production ran**: `startDaemon` wraps a `Duplex` itself, so `cli.ts` passed the raw socket and the tested wrapper had no production caller. The fix makes the tested function the shipped one (`namedPipeSocket`, mirroring `inheritedAppSocket`). (2) The accept liveness closure compared `process_started(pid) == started` with `started: Option<u64>`, so `None == None` read a dead child as alive for the whole 30 s deadline; it now fails closed. Both are the shape the harness warns about — a tested twin and a fail-open comparison — and both were caught by reading the diff, not by trusting the handoff.
  - **RED was produced by disabling the production path, not by asserting it later.** Renaming only the environment variable `spawn()` pushes fails the wiring test at `.expect("a channel to the spawned daemon")` (`1 failed`); restoring the name is green. The transport's own tests were new with the transport, so their RED is the compile error before it existed.
  - **The measurement, with its limits.** Windows: `cargo test --lib` **397/0/4, exit 0**; the Node pipe test **2 passed**; the focused daemon channel suite **15 passed / 2 failed**, both the afunix baseline in `app-channel-fd.test.ts` that W7a already isolated by re-running it with the change stashed; `pnpm typecheck` and `pnpm lint` clean except the gitignored host cache. Fedora/WSL `cargo check --lib --tests --locked` exits 0, which is a compile of the unix arm only — **the unix fd-3 channel's runtime behaviour was not re-run** (WSL has no network for the sherpa-onnx build script), so "the refactor changed nothing observable on macOS/Linux" rests on inspection plus that compile, not on a run.
