The authority is **not** burned and nothing was replayed.
  - **What this means for the reader.** The implementation is measured by its author and the parent, with a real adversarial test and a real RED; it has **no independent outside view yet**. That is a missing proof, not a passing one, and the honest next step is the unblock named in the row rather than another self-check.

- **L61 (2026-10-17, agent, evidence):** the narrow independent verifier returned **NO BLOCKER** on six claims, and the one assertion it could not settle was settled by a probe instead of by argument.
  - **The verifier's rerun, and why the first one died.** The first `gentle-ai-verify` did not fail on its model: its own task record (`gentle-agents/tasks/mvcofdws-3-6t9u.json`) resolved `model: opencode-go/deepseek-v4.1-flash`, `thinking: low`, and then reported `Command timed out after 120 seconds` with `retrying (1/3)`, `(2/3)`, `(3/3)`. The prompt had invited `cargo test --lib`, which does not fit the tool's 120 s bound. The rerun was read-only and **forbade executing anything**; it returned PASS on identity, rejection, non-inheritance, the unix arm, `close()`/`CancelIoEx`, and honest messaging, with the unix tests byte-identical to `a7dec73` and no hunk touching them.
  - **The one UNVERIFIED assertion, and the probe that settled it.** `FILE_FLAG_FIRST_PIPE_INSTANCE` only fails *our own* create when the name already exists; the verifier correctly refused to accept the row's claim that "no same-user process can squat the name" without evidence, because a same-user process may hold `FILE_CREATE_PIPE_INSTANCE` and could add a **second** instance of the discovered name — which the daemon's `net.connect` might land on, handing it the approvals and the DPAPI secrets. Measured on Windows 11 (`%TEMP%\w7b-pipe-probe.ps1`): with the app's `nMaxInstances = 1` the second `CreateNamedPipeW` is **refused** (`ERROR_PIPE_BUSY`, "all pipe instances are busy"), while the same call with `2` **succeeds** as a positive control. So the protection is the single-instance limit, not the flag, and the squat concern is falsified rather than argued away; L59's bullet was corrected to say exactly that.
  - **Pinned by a test, with both halves observed.** `a_second_instance_of_the_endpoints_name_cannot_be_created` (`app_channel.rs`, `windows_tests`) creates an endpoint, tries the attacker's create on the same name without `FIRST_PIPE_INSTANCE`, and asserts failure, then asserts `FIRST_PIPE_INSTANCE` refuses a duplicate too. **RED**: relaxing `create()`'s `nMaxInstances` to `2` fails it at `a second instance of the app's pipe name was created`; restoring `1` is green. Full `cargo test --lib` after it: **398 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored, exit 0**, only the pre-existing `Preferred` warning.
  - **Two advisories recorded rather than fixed.** (1) The verifier's gap case: if a rejected client disconnects and the real child connects in the window before `DisconnectNamedPipe`, the child is severed — a **one-shot channel loss**, the same bootstrap-DoS class L50 already documented, never a wrong-pid accept. (2) Two suggested probes were deliberately not added: asserting `cli.ts`'s source selects `namedPipeSocket` is a source-text assertion rather than a behavioural one, and asserting the accepted handle's client PID directly would need a test-only accessor on `Connected`; the identity is already proven behaviourally, because only the child's request reaches the handler (`handler.asked == 1`) and the refused peer reads nothing.

- **L62 (2026-10-17, agent, evidence):** W7c, the trust model in prose, and the three public sentences a named pipe made false.

The paragraph's fd-3 mechanics (posix_spawn `adddup2`, the probe child, libuv's close-on-exec) are Unix-only, so they are now framed as such and the Windows half follows them.
  - **The honest sentence, which is the point of the unit.** What authenticates is the **retained connection**: `GetNamedPipeClientProcessId()` must equal the pid of the child this app spawned, with a liveness check so a recycled pid cannot pass, and a connection that fails it is refused and disconnected. The name is discoverable by any same-user process and is deliberately treated as not secret, so the prose states what the name is worth and what the connection is worth, separately.

Every relative link in the touched files resolves, and every command quoted in them was read from the code above. **Evidence:** `f903965`.

The fix was a **local** pin, `<git-common-dir>/gentle-ai/profile-pin.json` → profile `current`: it is git metadata, so it stays invisible to the worktree and cannot enter the frozen review scope, unlike `<worktreeRoot>/.pi/gentle-ai/profile.json`. The observation is right and the consequence is not: the OS resets the event for each new overlapped operation, and `a_second_pending_read_on_one_direction_still_waits_for_its_data` pins that with the condition the claim needs — a second read that is **genuinely** pending, because nothing is written for 250 ms after a completed transfer — and passes. The existing round-trip test crossed two frames but never guaranteed the second read was pending, which is why it could not settle this. `R3-WINDOWS-FFI-BUFFER` (reliability, BLOCKER) claimed windows-sys declares `ReadFile`/`WriteFile` with `*mut c_void` and that the Windows build does not type-check; windows-sys 0.61.2 declares `lpbuffer: *mut u8`, and the Windows build was already green at 399/0/4.

The provider's own continuation carries the exact CLI form with `--base-ref` and `--committed-only`, which is what resolved the correct scope.

- **L65 (2026-10-17, user + agent, evidence, decision):** W21 is landed, and the row's premise was wrong: the engine already had a Windows branch, and W4c never used `cmd /C`.
  - **The decision, and why the two options the row named were not the real ones.** The row asked for "`cmd /C`, as W4c did for the daemon's other shell work, or a refusal that names Windows". Both halves were wrong. W4c chose option (a) (L22, L24): it **removed** the login-shell probe on Windows instead of probing a shell Windows has, so no `cmd /C` was ever written anywhere in this tree. And the engine does not hardcode `/bin/sh` either. Read in the staged runtime, `@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent/dist/core/resolve-config-value.js` and `dist/utils/shell.js`: on Windows the engine resolves `getShellConfig()` — Git Bash under `%ProgramFiles%`, then `%ProgramFiles(x86)%`, then `bash.exe` found with `where` — and only when that throws does it fall back to `execSync(command)`, which is the host's default shell (`ComSpec`). A legacy WSL `bash.exe` takes the command on **stdin** with `-s`. The user chose to mirror that.
  - **Measured before a line was written.** `getShellConfig()` on this Windows 11 host returns `{"shell":"C:\\Program Files\\Git\\bin\\bash.exe","args":["-c"]}`; `resolveValue("!echo hi", process.env, "probe")` threw `probe could not be read: its command gave nothing.`; and `connectors-guided.test.ts:162` already proves the engine itself resolves `!echo pwned` to `"pwned"` here. So the daemon was the only one of the two that could not resolve the value, and the engine was the reference to copy rather than a behaviour to invent.
  - **The engine is not imported, and the reason is measured rather than stylistic.** `getShellConfig` **is** exported from the package root, but importing `@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent` costs about **two seconds** of daemon startup (2055 / 1959 / 2008 ms against a 4-6 ms baseline), and `resolveValue` is synchronous and reachable from the daemon's startup path through `connectors.ts`. The decision is therefore mirrored as a pure `commandShell(env, platform, exists)`, the same shape as W4c's `engramBinaryName(platform)`, and a win32-only test holds the mirror to the engine's real answer so it cannot drift silently.
  - **The one deliberate deviation from the engine, stated.** The engine asks `where` for the PATH hit; `commandShell` scans `env.PATH` instead, so the decision stays pure and reads the environment the command will actually run in. And POSIX stays `/bin/sh -c` even though the engine prefers `/bin/bash` there: changing the POSIX shell is a separate behaviour change with no defect behind it, so it was left alone on purpose rather than as an oversight.
  - **The swallowed failure, and the two differences it leaves on POSIX.** The empty `catch {}` is gone. The differential ran the base commit's `resolveValue` and the new one in the same process on a native filesystem (Fedora/WSL, `mktemp -d` scratch, never committed): **17 inputs, 15 byte-identical, 2 differences**, both of them the intended message for a command that fails — `!no-such-command-w21` goes from `gave nothing` to `the command exited with status 127`, and `!exit 3` to `status 3`. Successful commands, stderr, empty output, `$NAME`, `${NAME}`, `$$`, `$!`, `$MISSING`, `${}` and the literal cases are unchanged. That is the one POSIX-visible change in the unit, and it is declared rather than smuggled.
  - **No doc changed, and that is a finding rather than an omission.** `docs/design.md:141` already said the proxy "resolves them the same way before it sends them upstream". On Windows that sentence was false before this commit and is true after it, so the code moved to the prose instead of the prose moving to the code. Nothing else describes the message that changed.
  - **Limits, named rather than left implicit.** The legacy WSL `-s`/stdin transport is asserted purely through `commandShell`, but its invocation was not exercised here: this host has Git Bash, so `getShellConfig()` never reaches `C:\Windows\System32\bash.exe`. The `ComSpec` fallback (`shell: true`) only runs on a Windows host with no bash at all, which this host is not. And the new test's "a command that runs and gives nothing" case uses `!echo`, which prints nothing in bash but `ECHO is on.` in `cmd.exe`, so that one case depends on a bash being present.

The cause was measured rather than guessed: this session runs at **High Mandatory Level** with **`SeBackupPrivilege` and `SeRestorePrivilege` enabled**, which bypass the DACL. Four channels were probed on this host: `console.warn` at collection (0 occurrences in the default reporter), `console.warn` inside the body before `ctx.skip()` (0), the same write from a `beforeAll` (0), and a raw `process.stderr.write` at collection (**1**). Only the last one is visible in the default run, and only at collection time. The skip reason now goes there, **per call** and never deduplicated, so a second fixture in the same file cannot go silent behind the first.

Measured independently by the parent: a junction to `packages/daemon/node_modules` fails to resolve a dependency that is itself a symlink (which is the pnpm layout) with `MODULE_NOT_FOUND`, while the plain directory symlink resolves `ws`. Windows therefore builds a real folder holding one junction per dependency's real path, and nothing under the output is a link Node has to follow through a reparse point.

Splitting keeps the review narrow and gives the class an owner instead of leaving it named only in a log entry.
- **L67 (2026-10-17, user + agent, evidence, decision):** W20 folds into W5b, because the provisioning lock and the kill-tree are one decision — how a child is stopped on Windows — and the exploration changed what W20's options even were.
  - **The lock already expires, so the row's third option was already true.** Read in the bundled engine (`apps/desktop/src-tauri/target/release/runtime/daemon/node_modules/gentle-pi/bin/gentle-shell.mjs`, gentle-pi 4.0.0; the same region reads the same in the dev harness's 4.3.0): `SETUP_LOCK_STALE_MS = 15 * 60 * 1000` at `:960`, checked by **mtime age** at `:1047`, with a double-check-then-remove window at `:1055-1068`. "Ask upstream for a lock that expires" is therefore already upstream's behaviour, and the measured defect is a **15-minute window**, not a permanent state.
  - **The lock carries nothing, which is why age is all it can use.** `acquireSetupLock` creates it with `openSync(lockPath, "wx")` at `:1035-1038` and **writes nothing**: no pid, no timestamp, no token. The path is `join(home.dir, ".gentle-shell-setup.lock")` at `:1134`, so it follows `--home` (the daemon passes `agentHome`, `daemon.ts:180`) and is not independently configurable.
  - **Release is a `finally` and nothing else.** `releaseSetupLock` (`:1074-1079`) is called from the `finally` at `:1177-1179`. There is **no** `process.on("exit")` and no `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT` handler that releases it; the signal handlers that exist (`:448-453`) only forward to the spawned child. A hard kill runs no JavaScript at all, so the `finally` cannot run — which is exactly what Windows does.
  - **Our arm is the one that ignores its own grace period.** `daemon.rs:562-571`, the Windows `stop_child`, takes `_grace: Duration` and **does not use it**: it calls `child.kill()` (TerminateProcess) at once and `wait()`s only to reap. The Unix arm (`:538-560`) sends `SIGTERM` and polls `try_wait` every 50 ms until `grace`, then kills. `WaitForSingleObject` already exists in the same file (`:299-301`, inside `stop_orphan`, which is unreachable on Windows under option A), so the waiting primitive is not new work.
  - **A polite stop of the daemon is not enough on its own, and that is why the two rows are one.** The chain is app → daemon → engine, and on Windows **Node's own `child.kill()` is `TerminateProcess` too** (`supervisor.ts:167`/`:189`), so even a daemon that stops gracefully kills the engine abruptly and the `finally` at `gentle-shell.mjs:1177` still never runs. Fixing the lock's cause means giving the **engine** a cooperative exit, not just the daemon.
  - **Nothing we own touches that lock today.** Searching the repository (excluding `node_modules` and build output) for `.gentle-shell-setup.lock`, the log message, or `provision` finds only prose in this document. We do write the engine home's sibling marker `.gentle-shell-home` (`isolation.ts:33`, `daemon.ts:165`), so the setup directory is not wholly untouched, but a lock-clearing fix would be the first code of ours that manages the engine's own setup state — an argument for deciding it once, with the kill-tree, rather than twice.
  - **What is still not visible from the code, and must not be assumed.** Whether `TerminateProcess` on the daemon also kills the `gentle-shell` grandchild or orphans it: neither `spawn_daemon` (`daemon.rs:500-511`) nor `supervisor.ts:219` creates a Job Object, so the grandchild's fate is unobserved. And whether the engine has a cooperative exit path at all (an RPC verb or a stdin message) was not established, which is what a polite stop would need before it can be specified.
  - **Why folding rather than closing.** W20's third option was already upstream's behaviour; its first (clear a stale lock) would be our first code managing the engine's setup state and risks the concurrent-provisioning guarantee the lock exists for; and its second (a moment to clean up) is a property of `stop_child`, which is W5b's subject. Folding keeps one design decision inside one unit, the way L59 folded W19 into W7b.
- **L68 (2026-10-17, agent + independent verifier, evidence):** W5b is closed in four commits, and the independent verifier earned its keep: it falsified one of the claims the unit was about to ship, and its one unverified claim was then measured on Windows.
  - **What the verifier ran against, and its verdict per claim.** It read the four commits and the files as they stand. **Verified:** the lock rule has exactly the four specified branches with no fifth path; our code never writes into the lock (the lock path is only `exists` and `remove`, and the only write is to our own owner record); the supervisor clears before each spawn attempt and records the owner pid only once the child exists, so a failed spawn writes nothing; the Windows Rust half touches only `#[cfg(windows)]` and `#[cfg(not(any(unix, windows)))]` code, with the Unix `stop_child` diffed byte-identical to its parent; and the `netstat` parser cannot return a wrong pid — it rejected `0.0.0.0:14317` for port `4317`, a bare `4317` with no colon, a UDP row, a header line, an `ESTABLISHED` foreign port and a non-numeric pid, and deduplicated an IPv4/IPv6 pair.
  - **The falsification, and it was mine to take.** The commit claimed the residual overlap was unreachable because two live daemons on one engine home are prevented by the port bind and the app's single-instance channel. The verifier showed the bind only stops a second daemon on the **same** port: the port comes from `GENTLE_DOT_PORT` or `config.json` (`config.ts:110-112`), `daemon.ts:386` binds before `:437` starts the engine, and no data-dir lock exists anywhere, so a daemon on another port passes the gate, reaches the spawn, and **overwrites** the owner record, which is last-writer-wins. A later run can then clear a lock a live engine still holds. The consequence is a **concurrent-provisioning window, not data loss**, and it needs a documented port override. `claimEngineHome` does not close it either: it tolerates `EEXIST` silently (`isolation.ts:63`), which is why it was never mutual exclusion. **W24 now owns it**, and the commit message was amended rather than left with a claim the evidence contradicts.
  - **The claim the verifier could not settle, measured here instead.** `isProcessAlive` answers "alive" for every failure except `ESRCH`, which is only sound if Windows really reports `ESRCH` for a process that is gone — the verifier had no Windows host and could not read libuv's mapping, and named this the load-bearing assumption of the whole rule. Measured on this Windows 11 host with Node v24.21.0: a **reaped child** and an absurd pid both answer `ESRCH`, while a live process we may not signal (`System`, pid 4) answers `EPERM`, which this code maps to "alive" — the fail-safe direction. The assumption holds, and it is now a measurement rather than an assumption.
  - **Two hardening items the verifier found, both taken.** `ownerPid` used `Number`, which accepts `0x10` and `1e3` as pids, so a record we never wrote could have been trusted into a deletion decision; it is digits-only now and the test pins `""`, `"0"`, `"-1"`, `"1e3"` and `"0x10"` as unproven. And the verifier noted `engineHomeArgument` scans the supervisor's own args, so under the documented `GENTLE_DOT_AGENT_ARGS` test seam it could pick up a foreign `--home`; that is outside the normal engine path and is recorded here rather than fixed.
  - **One limit the verifier found that is not a defect.** The `netstat` parser matches the English `LISTENING` token, so a localized Windows yields no pid. That is a false **negative** — the listener is left running — never a wrong kill, which is the direction that matters for a `SIGTERM`.
  - **What a unit test still cannot show, stated as before.** The tests exercise the decision and the supervisor's file effects against a fake agent, never the real launcher, so nothing here observes the lock actually being released or safely cleared in the running app; and the Job Object's effect was observed on a directly assigned child, not on a grandchild. Both need the app plus the real engine on Windows.
  - **The unit's shape, for the record.** Four commits, one per separable behaviour, and the high-risk one (`2775d33`) is alone in its commit so a review can take it by itself. The escalation that bought the verifier was worth one falsified claim and one measured assumption.
- **L69 (2026-10-18, user + agent, evidence, decision):** W24 is closed, and the decision was the user's: **the daemon claims its data folder** (option A of three). The exploration before asking changed the option set, and two consequences the row did not name were measured in the code.
  - **What the row got right, verified in the code before proposing anything.** The port bind only stops a second daemon on the **same** port (`daemon.ts:386-388`, plus the app's own refusal in `foreign_daemon_message`); the port comes from `GENTLE_DOT_PORT` or `config.json` (`config.ts:110-112`); `supervisor.ts:224` calls `clearStaleSetupLock` and **ignores its decision**, spawning either way; and `writeSetupLockOwner` overwrites, so the owner record is last-writer-wins.
  - **The two consequences the row did not name, and they made the data folder the right scope.** Two daemons on one data folder share `<dataDir>/sessions` (`supervisor.ts:116`) and the same engine home, and `privateMemory.stop()` kills the memory server on port 7438 when the `.engram/.instance-id` under the data folder matches (`isolation.ts:186-222`) — so the **second daemon, on its way out, stops the first one's memory server**. The row's own wording was the agent home; the resource that actually overlaps is the data folder, which is why the claim is the data folder plus the engine home only when `GENTLE_DOT_AGENT_HOME` puts that outside it.
  - **The three options and why the third lost.** A data-folder claim; an engine-home-only claim (the row's literal wording, which leaves the sessions folder and the memory server shared); or accepting the window and documenting it. The user chose the first. The app is single-instance (`tauri-plugin-single-instance`), so the collision needs a hand-started daemon on an overridden port against the same folder — rare, but the failure it causes is silent, which is what a claim is for.
  - **The rules, and the direction every ambiguous case falls.** Free folder: claimed. Our record with a live holder: refused, naming the pid and the file. Our record with a holder that is provably gone: taken over. An **empty** record (ours, half written — the create and the write are two steps): taken over. Anything else, an unreadable file included, and a record that keeps being replaced under us: **refused and left where it is**, never cleared, because clearing is the direction that loses the mutual exclusion. A refusal releases the folders already taken, so a daemon that did not start leaves nothing behind.
  - **The defect the differential run caught, and it was mine.** The first version claimed before anything else, which assumed the data folder existed; on a fresh **nested** `<tmp>/data` it threw `ENOENT`, and the suite reported it as `cli.test.ts`'s `keeps the access key out of redirected output, such as a log file` failing — a red test in an unrelated file, exactly what the by-name differential is for. The claim creates the folder (private, mode `0o700`) before it writes the record, which is what `ensureToken`'s `ensurePrivateDir` used to do first.
  - **Measured with two real daemon processes on Windows 11**, not only with tests: the second refused with the named error naming the pid and the lock file, the record survived a `kill -9` holding a dead pid, and the next start took it over. The **clean** release is measured in-process (a third `startDaemon` succeeds after `close()`), and that is stated rather than dressed up: an outside `SIGTERM` on Windows is a hard terminate that runs no handler, which is precisely the case the liveness takeover exists for.
  - **What is deliberately not claimed.** The claim is cooperation between our daemons: a user-run `gentle-shell` in the same home is not excluded, and it never was. A pid reused by an unrelated live process reads as a live holder and the start refuses until the file is deleted, which the message names. Neither `pnpm test` nor this measurement observes the memory server actually being spared, because that needs the real engine and a real sign-in; what is measured is that a second daemon never reaches the code that would stop it.
- **L70 (2026-10-18, user, evidence):** the port's first real conversation on Windows, in the app — the manual smoke SW2 asked for, and it passed on the chat half.
  - **What was observed.** On native Windows 11 the user opened the app and held a short conversation in Spanish: the greeting, the capability list (files, commands, web search, memory, subagents, verification) and the closing question. The engine ran from the dev tree with our own identity, and the session was persisted on the Windows side: `~/.gentle-dot/sessions/2026-10-18T00-56-53-329Z_01a14c82-f54e-76aa-bbf3-fca6a3b409ff.jsonl`, **89,321 bytes**. The daemon that served it wrote W24's claim (`gentle-dot-daemon 13924`), taking over the stale record of the daemon killed one launch earlier — the liveness path, now measured in the real app and not only in tests.
  - **What it proves.** SW2's acceptance line — "the daemon serves the UI at `http://127.0.0.1:4317`, chats, and answers asks in the browser" — holds for the **chat** half on native Windows 11, with the bundled engine and the assistant's own identity.
  - **How the app is started matters, and the trap is now in `docs/windows-testing.md`.** A debug binary is not the release path: the one under `target/debug` was produced by `tauri dev`, so it carries `devUrl` (`http://127.0.0.1:5173`) and **no embedded UI assets** — run on its own it opens a blank white window. Measured: two occurrences of the dev URL inside the binary, zero `assets/*.js`, and nothing listening on 5173; with `pnpm --filter @gentle-dot/desktop dev` (Vite first, then the app) the webview loaded and held `ESTABLISHED` connections to 5173 and to 4317.
  - **What it does not prove, and must not be read as covered.** The app channel's own half was not exercised: no connector needed a secret and no action needed an approval, so the named pipe's secret and approval paths are still only unit-tested. Voice (W12), computer control (W13), the shortcut and autostart in an **installed** app, and the release build's embedded UI remain unobserved, and the conversation ran through the dev frontend rather than the embedded one.
- **L71 (2026-10-18, user + agent, evidence, decision):** the user asked how the private memory mode is turned on, and measuring it found that on Windows it was not private. The fix is one line, and the open product question is still the user's.
  - **The question and the switch.** `GENTLE_DOT_ENGRAM=private` is the mode: port 7438, `ENGRAM_URL` removed, the store under the engine's home, and the daemon stops that server when it closes. On Windows the switch reaches the daemon because the app's spawn **does not clear the environment** (`env_clear` appears zero times in `daemon.rs`; `:500-508` and `:468-471` add PATH and a few names) — so a user variable in `HKCU\Environment` lands. None is set today (the registry has only `Path`), which is why the shared Engram was the behaviour in use.
  - **What the first measurement found instead.** With `GENTLE_DOT_ENGRAM=private` the engine's provider ran `engram serve` and Engram resolved its data folder from `%USERPROFILE%`: the server on 7438 answered the **same** `/health` `instance_id` as the user's server on 7437 (`efec67d5da1d0121ac8c9305d85609f`), and nothing was created under `<dataDir>/home/.engram`. So "private" mode opened the user's store in a second process, and the daemon could never stop it: the check compares that folder's `.instance-id`, which did not exist. The cause is in our own code — the mode **deleted** `ENGRAM_DATA_DIR` and let the default follow `HOME`, and Windows has no `HOME` (the mirror image of W22, which had the app reading `HOME` where Windows defines `USERPROFILE`).
  - **The fix, measured.** Private mode now names the store (`ENGRAM_DATA_DIR=<dataDir>/home/.engram`), which is the path `privateMemory` already read. Live, with a scratch data dir and the real engine: 7438 reports `4ed7c38172ab58faf9292cbefcde33f0` while the user's server keeps `efec67d5…`; the store appears under the scratch home with its own `engram.db`, `-wal` and `.instance-id`; the assistant **saved into it and got id 1** (the user's store answers 1365); and after killing the daemon the same store reopened with the same `instance_id`. Two earlier control runs are part of the evidence: with the harness's `ENGRAM_BIN` (`engram-guard`) the engine answered and reported memory **disabled** (the guard exits 78 by design), which is what showed that a provider which must *start* a server fails fast and visibly.
  - **The decision that is still open, and it is not the same as the fix.** Fixing isolation does not make the mode reachable: the switch is an environment variable, `config.json` holds only `port`, `workspace`, `shortcut` and `launchAtLogin`, and `loadConfig` reads just the first two. The user chose to measure before deciding whether to add a `"memory"` key plus a Settings toggle, or document `setx` as the supported way, or leave the shared Engram as the default. **That choice is still theirs**, and this entry records the numbers it should be made with.
  - **Cleanup, because the measurement ran against the user's own machine.** The scratch data folders were removed, the leaked "private" server was stopped, and the user's store was checked after the episode: its server still answers on 7437 with the same `instance_id` and the database is untouched (`engram.db` had not been written since 20:41, before the runs).
  - **The memory path is verified too, in both directions, and it is the shared Engram.** The user then asked the assistant whether it can reach Engram. It answered that it uses it both ways, named the observation it had just written — **id 1363**, `type: discovery`, `project: gentle-dot`, `session_id 01a14c82-f54e-76aa-bbf3-fca6a3b409ff:resume:2`, `created_at 2026-10-18 01:21:56` — and the record checks out, down to that session id being **the app's own conversation file's UUID**. The id also continues the sequence of the harness's own observations, so the app's engine read and wrote the **user's Engram on `127.0.0.1:7437`**, not a store of its own. That is the mechanism the daemon already documents: with no `GENTLE_DOT_ENGRAM=private`, `isolatedAgentEnv` keeps `ENGRAM_PORT`/`ENGRAM_URL` and points `ENGRAM_DATA_DIR` at the user's real home (`isolation.ts:168-177`). Its *recall* is checkable and it checks out: it named "el kit del instrumento, las 22 correcciones de la madrugada, los pendientes", which are observations **1313, 1329 and 1330 — in project `admin`**, a different project from the session's own, so the cross-project read is measured as well. Two things are not proven by this and are not claimed: the write is proven by the record, while the read is evidenced by that accurate naming (it could not be observed at the server, which logs no reads), and the UI's counter said **one** memory use where the reply describes two (a save and a search to confirm it) — which of the two it counted is unknown, and the discrepancy is recorded here rather than smoothed over.

- **L72 (2026-10-18, user + agent, evidence, decision):** the private memory was measured **inside the app**, and the user chose how it is turned on.
  - **Measured** (app launched with `GENTLE_DOT_ENGRAM=private`, W25's fix in place): 7438 answered `instance_id e7f4e3c0b0804ed8305c596d4321d5a4`, different from the user's own 7437 (`efec67d51da1d0121ac8c9305d85609f`); `<dataDir>/home/.engram` appeared with `engram.db`, `-wal`, `-shm` and a `.instance-id` equal to that id — the match `privateMemory.stop()` needs; `mem_save` answered `{"id":1,"status":"saved"}`, the private store's own `mem_stats` reported `total_observations:1`, and `mem_search` recalled the observation. The same search against 7437 returned `[]` and its `instance_id` did not move: no leak. The engine reported `ENGRAM_PORT=7438` with `ENGRAM_URL` and `ENGRAM_BIN` empty.
  - **One contaminated run, and why it is not a defect:** the first launch inherited `ENGRAM_BIN=<…>/gentle-pi/bin/engram-guard` from the harness shell, so gentle-engram failed with `could not initialize the Engram memory provider at http://127.0.0.1:7438: The Engram binary "…engram-guard"…` and memory was disabled — no server, no store. The user's own environment carries **no** `ENGRAM_*` variable (`HKCU\Environment` checked), so a normal launch is unaffected; the trap belongs to L328's dev-tree chain, and the run was clean with those variables removed.
  - **The stop path is not what frees 7438 when the app is killed hard:** the app puts its daemon in a Windows Job Object (W5b, `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE`) and the daemon's children inherit it, so a hard kill of the app takes daemon → engine → Engram down with **no** shutdown line in `daemon.log` at all. 7438 was free, but by the job. A real Quit is `RunEvent::Exit` → `Daemon::stop`, which closes the channel and waits the 5 s `STOP_GRACE` for the daemon to leave on its own; that daemon half was then **measured**, with a named pipe created the app's own way: closing the channel logged `the desktop app's channel closed; stopping the assistant`, the daemon exited 0, and **it killed the private server itself** — `stopped the private memory server (pid 14608)`, exactly the pid that was listening on 7438, not the `serves other data; left running` branch — while the store (`engram.db`, `-wal`, `.instance-id`) survived and 7437 never moved. So `privateMemory.stop()` does run on the graceful path on Windows; a hard kill of the app is the only case that skips it, and then the job takes the tree. Two notes from that run: it first refused to start because of a stale `agent/.gentle-dot-daemon.lock` (mtime 22:09, from a killed daemon) naming pid 12988, which the OS had since handed to `nu.exe`, so W24's documented residual read it as a live holder — deleted by hand and the run repeated; and the synthesized channel never answers `secret_get`, so the daemon logged `connectors.json could not be checked … connectors that need a secret stay locked`, an artifact of the harness rather than of the stop path.
  - **Decision (the user's, after the numbers of L71):** the switch is a **key in `config.json` plus a toggle in Settings** — option A of three —, not merely a documented `setx`. **SW9** carries the acceptance. The private store this measurement created (`~/.gentle-dot/home/.engram`, one probe observation) was deleted on the user's instruction; the user's own Engram (7437, `~/.engram`) was never touched.
  - **Split:** W26 runs as two delegated writers on disjoint surfaces, because SW9 freezes the interface they share: the daemon side (`packages/daemon/**`, `docs/design.md`) and the app side (`apps/desktop/**`, `packages/ui/**`).
  - **Live end-to-end of the switch, which is the acceptance SW9 asks for:** with `"memory": "private"` written into `~/.gentle-dot/config.json` and **no environment variable exported at all**, the app launched normally and took the mode from the file: 7438 answered `instance_id 91cc2cfcd8a1d28d9ea2417af862cd7f` (the user's 7437 stayed `efec67d5…`), the store appeared under `<dataDir>/home/.engram` with a `.instance-id` matching it, a real chat turn saved `ROSA-7439` into it (`id 1`, returned by the private server's own `/search`) and the user's store answered `[]` for the same query — save and recall had already been measured the same way in the `GENTLE_DOT_ENGRAM` run above. The test store was then deleted, `config.json` was restored to its previous content, and the user's own Engram was up and unchanged afterwards. **Not done by hand:** a click on the Settings toggle. The toggle is covered by the UI test (stored value shown, flip calls `memory_set`) and the ACL grant was verified in the generated `gen/schemas/capabilities.json` — both permissions resolve under the `settings` capability for window `panel`, and neither reaches `dot`.

The probe **is** the privileged syscall: Node exposes no way to ask, so it attempts a symlink in `tmpdir()`, checks `lstat().isSymbolicLink()` and removes it.
  - **Decision (the user's, over the two routes):** option (a), **"Sonda + skip ruidoso"** — not a privileged runner.   - **Acceptance:** **SW10**. On any host the daemon suite never fails for lacking that privilege: directory fixtures go through `symlinkDirectory` (a junction on Windows) and run everywhere; each test needing a **file** symlink skips with one raw-stderr line naming the reason and the way out; assertions that need no privilege — the hardlink at `approval-guard.test.ts:231`, the plain path spellings — keep running; a fixture is **never** silently replaced by something weaker; and on a host with Developer Mode, this one, nothing skips and the suite behaves exactly as it does today.

  - **Outcome (W23 closed as `0d015f0`):** the two directory-only sites and both directory halves of the split tests now go through `symlinkDirectory`, the four file-symlink tests skip through `canCreateFileSymlinks`, and the test that mixed the privilege-free hardlink with a file link was split so the hardlink and the plain path spellings keep running. On this host nothing new skips and the suite's failing set is byte-identical. **Stated plainly:** the refusal was **proved by forcing it** (a temporary `EPERM` in the probe's attempt, reverted) and never observed from the OS, because this host holds the privilege; the only place a real `EPERM` appears is the Windows CI job, which nobody has run. `docs/windows-testing.md` now carries the four-test note and the exact skip line, with Developer Mode or an elevated terminal as the way out.

- **L76 (2026-10-18, user + agent, evidence, decision):** the native review of the two commits that closed W17 (`196bfe5`, `3514671`) could not run, and the user chose to record it as unavailable rather than keep retrying.

This is the **second occurrence of the same signature** in this repository (L74's `review-72ad1325504c6969` was the first), so it is recorded as a provider-side pattern rather than a property of this diff.

Delivery therefore follows ordinary repository policy, exactly as the contract says a review outcome never authorizes delivery by itself. The implementation W17 changed was reviewed and approved separately (`review-46b590d6a1280815`, authority burned); only the two Log/status commits that followed it are uncovered.

- **L77 (2026-10-19, user + agent, evidence, decision):** the user asked for a third W7 proposal in the PR thread to be analysed, and chose to answer it, to add its one transferable finding as a unit, and to leave the branch carrying that unit. The proposal is **not** taken (see below).
  - **What was proposed.** @Feanor90 (`Feanor90/gentle-dot@feat/windows-support`, `f22c3a1`, based on `8cb20ee`) keeps fd 3 on Windows instead of replacing it: the app creates a private pipe and opens **both** ends itself, then hands the client end to the daemon as CRT fd 3 through `STARTUPINFOEXW` with `lpReserved2` and `PROC_THREAD_ATTRIBUTE_HANDLE_LIST`. The daemon never connects by name, so there is no client identity to check on its side; the app requires `GetNamedPipeClientProcessId()` to be itself, so a squatter makes the pair fail closed. Evidence offered: `cargo test --lib` on Windows (401 at the time), the Linux checks, and a **launched NSIS build** where connectors (Notion OAuth) and native approvals work end to end. Not verified on macOS.
  - **What it has in its favour, and it is real.** With fd 3 there is no handshake to authenticate, so there is no credential check to get wrong — one whole defect class disappears. The discovery residual narrows from the branch's drop-and-retry over the accept deadline to a fail-closed pair, and S25.1's original redaction ("nothing is written to a file or an environment variable, and nothing listens") becomes true again instead of corrected, as W7c had to correct it (`docs/design.md:151`). Had it arrived before W7b landed, it was probably the better shape.
  - **Why the transport is not taken.** `lpReserved2` is a CRT contract libuv interprets, not documented Windows API, so a change in Node's Windows fd handling breaks it silently, while `net.connect({ path })` is public API; the hand-rolled `CreateProcessW` is new surface in `daemon.rs`, the file that concentrated 46 of W6's 57 compile errors; and W7b is landed, reviewed, tested and documented (W7a/W7b/W7c). With 91 files open, trading a landed transport for a narrower residual and a documentation sentence does not pay. The door is left open in so many words: if S25.1 fidelity ever outweighs the churn, that implementation is the reference. It is a different trade, not a worse design.
  - **The one finding that is independent of the transport, and it is true today.** The contributor's claim that only `{NUL, log, channel}` reach the daemon with his spawn points at a property this branch does not have. Verified against the toolchain's own `std` source rather than argued: Windows spawns with `bInheritHandles = TRUE`, and the only care taken is `std`'s `CREATE_PROCESS_LOCK` mutex around **its own** stdio handles, with the rule stated in its comment — "once an inheritable handle is created, *any* spawned child will inherit that handle" (`library/std/src/sys/process/windows.rs:193`, `:329-345`). `daemon.rs`'s Windows `spawn_daemon` uses `std::process::Command`, so every inheritable handle the app holds at that instant reaches the daemon. The channel's handle is not one of them (W7b creates it non-inheritable), but nothing ever measured what else is. **That is W29**, with the measurement first and the `STARTUPINFOEXW`/`HANDLE_LIST` fix only if the measurement finds a leak, so a clean result closes the row as a measured negative rather than as an assumption. Risk item 2, against the app's handle table.
  - **Two things asked of the contributor instead of a merge.** `win_spawn.rs` split from `win_pipe.rs` as its own reviewable unit, and the installed-NSIS evidence written down, because the PR's known limits say no installed copy has ever been launched, which is the one hole his run closes without touching a line of code.
  - **What the reply says about the branch's own state, so the offer is actionable.** The tip, the CI runs that prove the suite on `windows-latest`, what is done, and the agenda that is still open with a row each: W12 (voice), W13 (computer control), W28 (W17's three advisories), and the four checks nobody has observed — the MSI installed, an installed copy **launched**, `cargo test` on the POSIX side at this tip, and a real symlink `EPERM` on a host without Developer Mode. It also states that the user is likely unavailable for some days for university reasons, that what is wanted is a **fully working** version rather than a perfect channel, and that a contributor taking work should claim **one whole row** before starting so the two do not collide.
  - **The branch instruction, given first because it protects his work.** Do not merge or rebase onto `feat/windows-support`: that branch holds what this one does not (his `win_pipe.rs`/`win_spawn.rs` fd-3 handover and the installed NSIS run), and moving either direction mixes that with 143 foreign commits and hides exactly what he wants to compare. The instruction is a **new branch from this tip** carrying only the files he wants to contrast, so anything of his that wins lands as its own reviewable unit rather than as a merge.

- **L78 (2026-10-20, user, evidence, correction):** the user corrected a claim this plan and the PR body had been carrying, and the correction outranks both.
  - **The correction, verbatim.** "Me gusta, pero recuerda que sí instalé la app 2 veces, solo que se quedó en starting, que es algo que tenemos que solucionar,, la instalé desde el .exe, no desde el msi".
  - **What it falsifies, in three places.** L77 said the installed-copy hole "is the one hole his run closes without touching a line of code"; the PR body's known limits said **"no installed copy of the app has been launched"** and its test plan carried `[ ] An installed copy ... **launched** from where it was installed`; and `docs/windows-testing.md` said "What nobody has done yet is **launch an installed copy** of the app". All three were wrong in the same way: the copy **was** installed, from the NSIS `.exe`, **twice**, and **was** launched. What it did not do is reach `ready`.
  - **Why the distinction is the point.** "Never launched" is an **unverified check**, and it waits for someone with a machine. "Launched and stuck in `starting`" is a **measured defect**, and it is ours to fix. Reporting the second as the first was a claim more comfortable than the truth, which is the failure mode this plan's whole log is written against — the entry stays as a correction rather than an edit of L77 so the error is on the record, in the same spirit as L35, L55, L68 and L70.
  - **What landed because of it.** **W30** is the unit: an installed copy launched from the NSIS installer does not get past `starting`, undiagnosed, first step being `daemon.log` from that launch rather than a hypothesis. `docs/windows-testing.md` no longer says nobody has launched one; it says one **has** been launched and does not reach `ready`, so the installer is not a proven path yet. The **MSI is still uninstalled**, and that check stays open in issue #88 — the correction removes one unverified check and adds one open defect, which is not the same as progress being cancelled.
  - **Not claimed.** No cause is named. The staged `runtime\` beside the executable, the UI served from the bundle instead of the dev frontend, `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME_DIR` resolution and a daemon spawn that fails where `daemon.log` would say so are all consistent with the symptom, and none is evidence. Anything else would be the same mistake one layer down.

- **L79 (2026-10-20, user + agent, evidence):** the user asked for `daemon.log` to be read, and it narrows W30 to a mechanical fact without yet naming the cause. Nothing here is a fix.
  - **The file, and its shape.** `C:\Users\Admin\.gentle-dot\daemon.log`, 316 lines, last written 2026-10-18 20:48 local. Timestamps are `Z`, so the log's last daemon line is `19:13:44Z`; the install's own file times are local (`gentle-dot.exe` 18:21, `runtime\` 18:38), which places the last installed launch **after** that last timestamped line and inside the untimestamped tail.
  - **What crashed, twice.** Two adjacent blocks of raw child stderr, each preceded by `[agent] agent exited unexpectedly (code 1)`:

    ```
    Error: EISDIR: illegal operation on a directory, lstat 'C:'
        at Object.realpathSync (node:fs:2733:25)
        at toRealPath (node:internal/modules/helpers:62:13)
        at Module._findPath (node:internal/modules/cjs/loader:781:22)
        at resolveMainPath (node:internal/modules/run_main:35:21)
        at Module.executeUserEntryPoint [as runMain](node:internal/main/run_main_module:33:47)
    Node.js v24.14.1
    ```

    `resolveMainPath` is the **main entry** path, so node was handed **`C:` as the script to run**, resolved it as a module path, and Windows answered that it is a directory. The engine never started, which is exactly what the panel shows as stuck in `starting`.
  - **It is the installed runtime, said by the version.** `Node.js v24.14.1` is the staged runtime's Node (`runtime\node\node.exe --version` confirms `v24.14.1`; the development host runs 24.21.0). The same log's development sessions name `D:\development\...` module paths, so the two are distinguishable rather than assumed apart.
  - **What was eliminated by measurement, not argument.** The engine is healthy: `runtime\node\node.exe runtime\daemon\node_modules\gentle-pi\bin\gentle-shell.mjs --version` with `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME` set prints `gentle-shell 4.3.0` / `pi 1.2.0` and exits 0. The script exists at the exact path `bundledGentleShell` builds (`config.ts:38-51`), `config.ts:128-133` spawns `process.execPath` + that script rather than the `bin\pi.cmd` shim, and both sides agree on the variable name (`daemon.rs:402` sets `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME`, `runtime.ts:11` reads it; `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME_DIR` is only where the *bundled* directory comes from, `daemon.rs:422-425`). So "missing engine" and "shim mangling" are both out, and the remaining question is **who passed `C:`** — an argument built by splitting, an override variable, or a child other than the engine.
  - **One separate defect the same read found, and it is not this one.** The packaged runtime ships `daemon\node_modules\gentle-pi\bin\engram-guard` as a **`#!/bin/sh`** script, and `isolation.ts` points `ENGRAM_BIN` at that guard whenever there is a packaged runtime. On Windows a `sh` script is not executable, so the private-memory path in the installed app is at best refused — the trap L72 recorded for `tauri dev` reaches the installer. It is recorded here because it was measured here; it is **not** wired into W30, since neither the `EISDIR` nor the `starting` symptom follows from it.
  - **What is claimed and what is not.** Claimed: the installed app's engine child dies with a `C:` entry, twice, on the staged runtime's Node. Not claimed: which code path built that argument. The next step is an instrumented reproduction (log `agentCommand` and `agentArgs` where the supervisor spawns, then launch the installed app once), because every hypothesis that a diff could settle has now been settled and did not match.
  - **The rest of the log, so it is not read as more than it is.** The Windows channel works in the development app: `the desktop app's channel closed; stopping the assistant` appears five times, always followed by an orderly stop (S35.2 observed live). The degraded path is the documented one (`no desktop app channel: connector changes and approvals are refused…` when the daemon is started from a terminal). The private memory server stops cleanly (`stopped the private memory server (pid 7140)`). `connectors.json was signed before and is missing; starting with no connectors` is S25.6 behaving. The repeated `assistant error: Connection error.` / `Request timed out.` and `command get_history failed: the agent did not start in time` are provider/network noise and the same missing agent, in that order — the second is consistent with W30 and does not enlarge it.

- **L80 (2026-10-21, agent, evidence, cause isolated):** W30 is **reproduced outside the app**. The trigger is not the channel, not a missing engine and not the argument builder: it is the **verbatim form of the runtime root** that `resource_dir()` hands over on the installed path.
  - **How it was found.** Three probes, each measured before the next: (1) `loadConfig()` with `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME` set to the plain install path yields `agentArgs[0] = C:\Users\Admin\AppData\Local\Gentle Dot\runtime\daemon\node_modules\gentle-pi\bin\gentle-shell.mjs` and `agentCommand` = the runtime's own node — both correct, so the builder is innocent; (2) starting the installed `cli.mjs` with the **runtime's** node against a scratch data folder and the plain path **works** — the engine starts, loads its extensions and the daemon serves, so the installed tree is healthy; (3) the same run with `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME` set to `\?\C:\Users\Admin\AppData\Local\Gentle Dot\runtime` — the **verbatim** form `daemon.rs:373-379` says `resource_dir()` produces — reproduces the log's defect exactly:

    ```
    [agent] Error: EISDIR: illegal operation on a directory, lstat 'C:'
        at toRealPath (node:internal/modules/helpers:62:13)
        at Module._findPath (node:internal/modules/cjs/loader:781:22)
        at resolveMainPath (node:internal/main/run_main:35:21)
    Node.js v24.14.1
    [agent] agent exited unexpectedly (code 1)
    agent failed to start: Agent exited (code 1); retrying
    ```

    Then it retries forever while the daemon serves — which is precisely what the panel shows as stuck in `starting`.
  - **The mechanics, as far as they are measured.** Node is handed a **verbatim** path as its entry script (`\?\C:\…\gentle-shell.mjs`); its CJS resolver routes it through `_findPath`/`realpathSync`, which reports the path as the bare **`C:`** and fails with `EISDIR`. The engine never starts. Nothing in this chain is specific to the channel, to `GENTLE_DOT_CHANNEL_PIPE`, or to the absence of the app.
  - **Why the installed copy only.** `launch_spec` uses the canonicalized `resource_dir()` for the bundled branch (`daemon.rs:373-405`), and on Windows that is the verbatim form. The same comment records that this exact root already caused one installed-copy failure (`os error 123`), so the class is recurring rather than new. Development runs take the non-bundled branch, which is why every dev measurement missed it.
  - **The fix, shaped by the above but not yet written.** Do not hand Node a verbatim root: normalise it at the boundary and keep the verbatim form only where Win32 needs it. Two candidate places — the Rust side when it sets `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME`, or `runtimeDir()` in `packages/daemon/src/runtime.ts` stripping the `\?\` prefix — and either needs a test pinning that the engine script handed to `spawn` never carries the verbatim prefix, plus the release-only guard W30 asked for. Chosen when the unit is worked, not here.
  - **What is not claimed.** No fix is written, and the exact call inside Node that collapses `\?\C:\…` to `C:` is observed rather than explained from Node's source. What is established is the trigger, the mechanism at the level of "verbatim entry reaches the CJS resolver", and the fact that the plain form starts the same tree.
  - **One more measured fact, still separate.** The runtime ships `daemon\node_modules\gentle-pi\bin\engram-guard` as `#!/bin/sh` and `isolation.ts` points `ENGRAM_BIN` at it whenever a packaged runtime exists, so the installed app's private memory path is at best refused. It stays out of W30's fix and needs its own row.

- **L81 (2026-10-21, agent, implementation):** W30's cause is fixed at the boundary where the bad value enters, and the fix is measured against the node that produced the crash. The unit is **not** closed: nobody has re-measured an installed copy.
  - **The change.** `packages/daemon/src/runtime.ts`: `withoutVerbatim()` strips `\?\` and turns `\?\UNC\server\share` back into `\server\share` **before** `resolve()`, so every consumer of `runtimeDir()` — `bundledGentleShell`, `uiDirOf`, `isolation`, `connectors` — gets a form Node can take, while Win32 still receives the verbatim root where it wants one (the Rust side is untouched; `launch_spec` keeps passing it to `CreateProcessW`).
  - **Test-first, observed.** `runtime.test.ts` gains a `runtimeDir` block with the two Windows cases written against the literal strings the app hands over, and one host-neutral ordinary-root case. **RED:** 2 failed / 1 passed, `expected '\?\C:\Users\me\Gentle Dot\runtime' to be 'C:\Users\me\Gentle Dot\runtime'` and the UNC twin. **GREEN** after the change: `runtime.test.ts` 13 passed / 1 skipped, `config.test.ts` 9 passed, and the combined run of `runtime`/`config`/`isolation` 37 passed / 1 skipped. One earlier combined run reported a single failure that passed alone and did not reproduce; it is the suite flakiness W27 already named, not this change.
  - **The proof that the cause was the cause.** A probe that reads the fixed `loadConfig()` with `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME` set to the **verbatim** root — the value that reproduced the defect in L80 — resolves the engine to the plain form and, spawned with the **installed runtime's own node 24.14.1**, prints `gentle-shell 4.3.0 / pi 1.2.0` and exits 0. Same tree, same node, same env; only the path form changed.
  - **What is still open, and why it is not "done".** The installed app has not been rebuilt and relaunched: `pnpm package:windows` (~26 min) plus an install and a launch is the measurement that closes W30, and nothing here claims it happened. `docs/install.md`'s Windows section should also stop implying the installed copy is unproven once it does.
  - **And one row still owed.** The packaged runtime ships `engram-guard` as `#!/bin/sh` while `isolation.ts` points `ENGRAM_BIN` at it whenever a runtime is packaged, so the installed app's private memory is at best refused on Windows. It was found in L79, is unrelated to the verbatim root, and needs its own unit rather than a line in this one.

- **L82 (2026-10-21, user + agent, evidence, correction):** L80 and L81 were **partial**, and the user's own report is what proved it: with L81's fix deployed and installed, the app still sat in `starting`. The record keeps the error rather than quietly editing L80.
  - **What the installed copy actually did, measured after the fix was in.** The installed `daemon/cli.mjs` carries `withoutVerbatim` (`MANIFEST.daemonCommit` is `5049dca`) and the launch at 23:17 local still produced the same two `EISDIR … lstat 'C:'` blocks with `Node.js v24.14.1` — and, decisively, **no timestamped line at all after `2026-10-18T19:13:44Z`**. A daemon that had started always writes `Gentle Dot is running at …`. So the crash is not the engine: the **daemon itself never started**, and the panel's `starting` is what an app with no daemon looks like.
  - **Why L80's reproduction misled me.** It reproduced the *engine's* crash (a daemon was alive and logging `[agent] agent exited unexpectedly`), and that crash really is caused by the verbatim root reaching the engine through `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME`. But the app's louder failure is one layer earlier: `launch_spec` hands **Node's own entry** over verbatim too.
  - **The proof, in two lines.** Spawning the installed `node.exe` with the daemon script, both in the verbatim form `resource_dir()` produces: **exit 1**, `node:fs:2733 … lstat`. The same spawn with ordinary absolute paths: the daemon starts and logs its channel line. Same node, same tree, same env; only the path form differs. `resource_dir()` is canonicalized, so the installed path always takes the first case.
  - **The fix, and its test.** `daemon.rs`: `without_verbatim()` drops `\?\` (and turns `\?\UNC\server\share` back into `\server\share`) before the runtime root is joined into the program, the script, `GENTLE_DOT_RUNTIME` and the `PATH` entries — the piece the earlier comment blamed on `/` not being a separator, whose real lesson is that the prefix is what Node cannot take. `a_verbatim_runtime_root_never_reaches_node` asserts none of the four carries it, and `cargo test --lib` is **410 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored**. The TS normalisation stays: it is what makes the *engine* start once the daemon is up, and it now runs twice on the same defect from both ends.
  - **Honest note on RED.** The unit test was never seen failing: the defect it pins was observed failing on the real artifact (23:17) and in the two-line probe above, which is the stronger evidence, and the temporary-toggle ritual was not repeated on top of it.
  - **Still open.** A rebuilt installer: `pnpm package:windows`, install, launch. Until that run, W30's fix is measured in its cause and not in the app, and the previous claim that it was fixed is corrected here rather than left standing.

- **L83 (2026-10-21, agent, evidence):** W30 is **measured on an installed copy** and closes. The artifact was built from the branch with both fixes, installed silently, and launched three times against a scratch data folder.
  - **The artifact.** `pnpm package:windows` from `f92eb66`: NSIS **80,807,173 B** (77.1 MB), MSI **127,504,876 B**, staged runtime 27,032 files / 321.7 MB, both payloads checked file for file. The install was silent into a scratch folder (`/S /D=`), exit 0, and `runtime\daemon\cli.mjs` present beside `gentle-dot.exe`.
  - **Launch 1, fresh data folder (100 s).** `daemon.log` is written by the daemon — which is the whole point, since the defect produced no timestamped line at all — HTTP `200` on the port, and `command get_history failed: the agent did not start in time` while the engine provisioned gentle-ai for the first time. **0 `EISDIR`.**
  - **Launch 2 (150 s).** Provisioning restarted (launch 1 was stopped mid-way, so no completion marker) and the same timeout appeared again; daemon up, HTTP 200, **0 `EISDIR`**.
  - **Launch 3 (300 s).** `Gentle Dot is running at http://127.0.0.1:4409/ (the access key is in …\token)`, `Verification checks: 1` from the engine's own setup, `ready` once, `signed in to opencode-go (api_key)`, HTTP 200, **0 `EISDIR`**, **0 `failed to start`**. The 4 `did not start in time` in that file are the two earlier launches and their retries, not this one.
  - **What is claimed, precisely.** The installed app **gets a working daemon and a provisioned engine**, which is what `starting` never had. Not claimed: that the panel *visually* shows `ready` — nobody looked at the window — and nothing about the user's own data folder, which was left untouched; the scratch folder and the scratch install were removed afterwards (`uninstall.exe /S`, exit 0).
  - **A new finding, measured in passing.** The installed engine's provisioning warns `missing dependencies: git`, because `launch_spec` puts only the runtime plus `System32` and `Windows` on `PATH` and Windows' own `git` lives elsewhere. W11a named that as a known deviation; here it is observed in an installed copy, and it is a candidate for its own row because gentle-ai's setup treats a missing dependency as a warning rather than a failure.
  - **For the user.** The install at `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Gentle Dot` predates `dc85486`, so it still has the crash; reinstalling from this artifact is the step, and it can be done whenever, since the measurement above already used a separate folder.

- **L84 (2026-10-21, agent, implementation, issue #90):** W31 opens and lands its code. L83 had recorded the `missing dependencies: git` warning as "a candidate for its own row"; the user reproduced the two notices from an installed copy, and they were filed as issue #90 before they had a row here. **They are one defect with two consumers:** `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` fails with `executable file not found in %PATH%` for RDD's review mode, and the skill registry refuses to write for the same reason, because both notices come from gentle-pi's `gentle-ai` extension running inside the engine, which inherits the daemon's `PATH` from `launch_spec`. **The shape was the user's decision** (the issue listed three): the hybrid — `where.exe git` first, then the default install folders with an existence check — over `where` alone and over candidates alone, because the first covers package-manager and custom installs while the second covers a machine whose `PATH` lost Git.
  - **RED, observed:** the two tests were written first and `cargo test --lib` failed with four `E0425`s naming the missing `windows_git_dirs` and `windows_bundled_path`. **GREEN:** 412 passed / 0 failed / 4 ignored, up from 410.
  - **A trap found by measuring before trusting the shape, and it changed the code.** `where.exe git` on this machine answers `C:\Program Files\Git\mingw64\bin\git.exe` **first**, and `Git\cmd` second. Both name a real `git.exe`, but `…\mingw64\bin` also carries an MSYS `curl.exe` (measured), and since the resolved folder is appended ahead of `%SystemRoot%\system32`, taking the first answer would have handed the engine a different `curl` than the one the packaged `PATH` was designed around — an unrequested behaviour change hiding inside a fix. So an answer in a `cmd` folder wins, `Git\cmd` (which holds git's own wrappers only: `git.exe`, `git-lfs.exe`, `gitk.exe`, `scalar.exe`) is what the tests pin, and the decision is asserted against this machine's real two-line answer rather than a hypothetical one.
  - **Precedence is asserted, not assumed:** `windows_bundled_path` keeps the runtime's `bin` and `node` at positions 0 and 1, puts Git's folder after them, and leaves the system folders last, so the packaged Node still wins over anything the machine has.
  - **Absent Git is a branch, not an accident:** nothing is appended, and the engine keeps the named warning it already prints, so the issue's third requirement — a machine without Git must keep working — holds by construction and by test.
  - **Not measured, and therefore not claimed:** an installed copy. The checks the issue names — `review mode status` answering and the skill-registry notice gone — need a rebuilt installer, the same 16-minute path L83 used. Until then the row stays `pending`, and the code is uncommitted because the commit is the user's decision.
  - **Scope discipline:** `docs/install.md` gained one paragraph on what Git is needed for and how it is found. Its other claims ("no packaged installer yet", 399 Rust tests, the 79,267,489-byte NSIS) are stale from earlier units and were **not** touched here; reconciling them is its own pass.

- **L85 (2026-10-21, user + agent, implementation, issue #90's sibling):** W32 opens. RDD and the skill registry were measured working in the app, and the next call of any memory tool failed instead: `could not initialize the Engram memory provider at http://127.0.0.1:7438: The Engram binary "…\bin\engram-guard" could not be found`. The chain, read from the code and not inferred: the dev launch inherits `ENGRAM_BIN=<the harness's guard>`; `isolation.ts:182` calls `resolveEngramBin` to name the engine's binary, and `runtime.ts:97` returns `ENGRAM_BIN` as its **first** rule, so the "override" is a passthrough; the guard is a `#!/bin/sh` script (`936 B`, `exit 78`, prints `ENGRAM_STARTUP_NOTICE`, never opens a store — read verbatim), which Windows cannot start; private mode (`GENTLE_DOT_ENGRAM=private`, port `7438`, store `<dataDir>/home/.engram`) then never gets a server, and the private store was intact on disk the whole time — `engram.db` plus its `-wal`/`-shm`.
  - **The user chose the second of three shapes:** a guard of our own for Windows, over a validated fallback that would silently drop the guard's property, and over refusing in prose only.
  - **A measurement decided the vehicle before any code:** the provider starts the binary with `spawn(ENGRAM_BIN, …)` and `spawnSync(ENGRAM_BIN, ["instance-id"])`, **no shell** (`gentle-engram` `index.ts:731,1038`). Node refuses `.cmd` without a shell, and Windows starts a program by its extension, so a `.cmd` shim would have been as dead as the script. The guard had to be an executable, which is why it is a Rust crate — `apps/engram-guard`, no dependencies, its own `[workspace]`, built from staging in 1.79 s where the Tauri tree would have cost minutes.
  - **The rule, and the three cases it deliberately does not take.** `refusalGuard` replaces an `ENGRAM_BIN` only when that file **exists and this platform cannot start it**. A launchable `ENGRAM_BIN` stays (the caller's choice); a path that does not exist stays too, because `The Engram binary … could not be found` names a typo better than a refusal would — substituting there would hide a real misconfiguration behind "memory disabled"; and POSIX keeps shebang scripts, which are legitimate programs there.
  - **RED then GREEN, both observed:** five tests first, all failing with `refusalGuard is not a function`; after the decision, `runtime.test.ts` **18 passed / 1 skipped**. `pnpm typecheck` exit 0. One `isolation.test.ts` hook timed out (daemon close) in a two-file run and passed **15/15 twice** on its own — the close-under-load flake this plan already documents, not this change.
  - **Packaging wired, and it is the open measurement.** `stageEngramGuard` builds the crate and copies `engram-guard(.exe)` into `runtime/bin/` next to `engram.exe`, with `apps/engram-guard/target/` gitignored. Nothing has staged, bundled or installed it yet, so the row stays `pending` and no claim about an installed copy is made.
  - **One upstream defect found while verifying, and named rather than absorbed:** `gentle-engram` builds `${message}.` where `message` already ends in a period (`index.ts:743` and `:1705`), so the notice reads `…at the current binary.. Run mem_doctor…`. It is the package's to fix; this repository ships no copy of it.

- **L86 (2026-10-21, user + agent, implementation):** W33 opens and its code lands. The user, testing the app, asked what to do about `command get_history failed: the agent did not start in time`, and the answer was measured rather than guessed: the message is ours (`bridge.ts:738`, `READY_TIMEOUT_MS = 60_000` at `:123`), `get_history` is its only caller, and the budget is a fixed 60 s against a cold start that provisioned for **3m39s** (L-mm/obs: `pi-web-access` 210 packages, 65 s; `pi-btw`, 36 s).
  - **Why removing the wait is the fix and not a shortcut:** `packages/ui/src/client.ts` already sends `get_history` again when the `ready` event arrives, so the wait only converted a slow start into a failed request. An agent that is ready keeps the engine round trip; one that is still starting answers `pageHistory` from the conversation on disk and the refetch replaces the page. `whenReady` and the constant are deleted, so the type cannot come back by accident.
  - **Evidence:** RED first — the new `bridge-history.test.ts` **timed out at 5 s** doing exactly what the panel does, with `FAKE_AGENT_START_DELAY_MS` holding the handshake open; GREEN — that file plus `bridge-stop.test.ts` **2 passed**, `pnpm typecheck` exit 0.
  - **A loaded suite run lied, and the method caught it.** One full daemon run reported **11 failed / 535 passed**, and every failure was a timeout (5 s test, 10 s hook) — no assertion. The app was up with its watcher, Vite and three `engram` servers while 61 workers started. Re-running **only those ten files with `--no-file-parallelism`** gave **125 passed / 7 skipped, exit 0**. That is the same trap this plan already records twice: a parallel measurement is not evidence of a regression, and the class is load, so the clean baseline needs the app stopped.
  - **Not claimed:** a real cold start. The app runs the staged `daemon/cli.mjs`, so this change is observable only in the next staged runtime — the same one W32 waits for.
  - **The clean baseline, measured with the app stopped** (it closed by itself, no orphan, and the daemon stopped its private memory server after checking the identity — `stopped the private memory server (pid 10888)` — leaving the user's own server on 7437 alone): the full daemon suite reports **5 failed / 541 passed / 42 skipped**, and all five are `Test timed out in 5000ms`; the same five files with `--no-file-parallelism` pass **66/3 skipped, exit 0**. So the suite's flakiness on this host is in the parallel run, not in the code under it, and the honest baseline for this machine is "timeouts under full parallelism, green in series".
  - **Deliberately out of scope:** the 3m39s. Pre-provisioning the companion packages at staging removes the delay itself, and that is a packaging unit with its own risk (item 5), not a message fix.

- **L87 (2026-10-21, user + agent, implementation, issue #90):** W34 opens. The user installed the MSI and reported, verbatim: "Si te soy honesto, me estoy cansando que esto siga con fallos a no se cuantos días de haber iniciado este port. Mira que sale con el msi: `Skill registry refresh failed: Cannot verify Git tracking for .atl/.gitignore; automatic writes skipped. Resolve Git detection, or deliberately run /skill-registry:refresh`" — and said he was about to delete everything. **Read, not guessed:** that message is gentle-pi's `skill-registry.ts:365`, thrown when `git ls-files --cached -z -- .atl/.gitignore` fails in a way that is not "not a git repository with no `.git` above": a spawn failure (`ENOENT`) lands there, so the message is about `git` being unreachable, not about the file. `~/.gentle-dot/workspace` is inside the user's home repo (`~/.git` is a valid repo; `git ls-files` there exits 0 from this shell), so the directory is not the problem.
  - **The measurement that settled it:** read the environment blocks of the two live processes of the installed app (a PEB read, `NtQueryInformationProcess` + `ReadProcessMemory`, 60 lines of PowerShell). The daemon had `Path=` with all five entries W31 computes — including `C:\Program Files\Git\cmd` — and the engine had `PATH=C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot\runtime\bin`, nothing else. A probe child confirmed the rule: with both spellings present, the uppercase one wins, in either insertion order.
  - **So W31 was right and never reached the engine:** `isolation.ts` did `(base.PATH ?? "").split(delimiter)` over an env whose key is `Path`, found no `rest`, and set `env.PATH` beside the inherited `Path`. Dev looked fixed because the shell hands the daemon an uppercase `PATH`, which overwrote in place.
  - **Why this is the first thing to report and not another item on a pile:** the engine's whole `PATH` collapsed to one directory, which explains `missing dependencies: git, curl` in L-mm's log, the skill-registry refusal, RDD's review mode being off, and why every previous fix that touched `PATH` appeared to do nothing in an installed copy.
  - **Second defect, same pass:** the default-Git fallback in `windows_git_dirs` was tested with `|path| path.is_file()` on **directories**, i.e. always false; `holds_git` now asks for `git.exe` inside, with a test that writes one.
  - **Not claimed:** the installed copy working. The copy the user installed predates this fix, so the claim waits for the rebuild that has started from this tree.

- **L88 (2026-10-22, user + agent, measurement):** W31, W32, W33 and W34 close on an **installed copy**, and the user's report is the trigger while the numbers below are the evidence. His words: "La app se instaló y funciona perfectamente ahora, no lanzó el error de git, ni de RDD ni del skill-registry. La tuve utilizando para investigar un poco y todo funciona perfecto hasta ahora, de hecho, le conecté varios adaptadores, el Consensus MCP y linear MCP y lo instalé desde el msi, por lo cual confirmé que el msi funciona perfecto también."
  - **The environment, read from the live engine (pid 12600) of `C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot\gentle-dot.exe`:** `PATH=C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot\runtime\bin;C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot\runtime\node;C:\Program Files\Git\cmd;C:\Program Files\Git\bin;C:\Windows\system32;C:\Windows`. One spelling, the runtime first, Git, then the system folders — W34's fix and W31's three entries in the same line.
  - **The log, from the install onward:** grep for `missing dependencies`, `did not start in time`, `skill registry` and `Cannot verify Git` returns **nothing** dated after `2026-10-22T01:52Z`; the last `missing dependencies: git, curl` is from the pre-W31 build and the last `command get_history failed` is `2026-10-21T17:11Z`. The same window records the real work the user did — `signed in to connector linear`, `added connector consensus from the assistant's draft`, `signed in to connector consensus` — and no notice in between.
  - **W32 in the installed runtime:** `runtime\bin\engram-guard.exe` is there and exits **78** with its notice. Its substitution branch is not exercised by this install, because the app's `ENGRAM_BIN` is its own `runtime\bin\engram.exe`, which Windows starts.
  - **Not claimed:** a fully cold provisioning after the install (the agent home was already warm, so W33's 60 s budget was not re-created on purpose), and the MSI's uninstall, which L89 now covers.
  - **What this closes:** the four units that were open, and with them the symptom that had been read as "the port keeps failing at random". It was one variable with two names, one dead predicate on a directory, one program the platform could not start, and one wait that bought nothing.

- **L89 (2026-10-22, user, measurement):** the last check issue #88 carried — that no MSI had ever been installed or uninstalled — closes, and the user's report is the trigger: "el desinstalador funciona también, porque cuando instalé la última compilación pues tuve que desinstalar e instalar el nuevo msi."
  - **What the machine shows afterwards**, read rather than assumed: one `C:\Program Files\Gentle Dot` (the removed install left no second copy), and one Add/Remove Programs entry under `HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall\{AD01D81E-8C39-4D16-87A4-04BF1E8BB1A3}` whose `UninstallString` is `MsiExec.exe /X{AD01D81E-8C39-4D16-87A4-04BF1E8BB1A3}`. The MSI also writes `HKCU\Software\Gentleman Programming\Gentle Dot` with `InstallDir` and the three shortcut flags.
  - **The uninstall keeps the user's data, and that is what the reinstalled app then depended on**: it came back with its connectors and its sign-ins (`signed in to connector linear`, `signed in to connector consensus`, `added connector consensus from the assistant's draft`), because `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Gentle Dot\secrets.dat` and `%USERPROFILE%\.gentle-dot` live outside the install folder.
  - **`docs/install.md` corrected**: it said "the MSI was not installed, because it needs an administrator", which stopped being true when this port was verified; it now carries the measured install and uninstall facts instead.
